## UMR MISTEA - INRAE

Commande optimale de l’irrigation : Double modélisation mathématique et agronomique vers une application au modèle Optirrig [SOURCE: Page_1]

Présentée par Ruben Chenevat le 10 Octobre 2025 Sous la direction de Alain Rapaport, Bruno Cheviron et Sébastien Roux [SOURCE: Page_1]

Remerciements Il est temps de dresser un bilan de ces trois années. Cette section me permet de retracer briève- ment la chronologie de ma thèse, les événements, rencontres et opportunités qu’elle a comportés. Que ce soit en termes de statut ou de contenu, la thèse est à cheval entre la fin des études et le début de la carrière professionnelle. Ce récapitulatif fait donc partie intégrante de mon parcours, à la fois comme introduction au monde de la recherche et comme fondation pour la suite de mes projets. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Tout d’abord, je dois mentionner les personnes et entités qui ont été impliquées dans la créa- tion du poste, la mise en place du financement et le soutien administratif. Ma thèse a bénéficié d’un financement partagé entre le Labex Numev de l’Université de Montpellier et le Département MathNum d’INRAE, ainsi que de deux labellisations par l’Institut Convergences DigitAg et l’École Internationale de Recherche d’Agreenium. J’ai été accueilli dans l’unité MISTEA, qui a fourni le cadre et les moyens matériels nécessaires au bon déroulement de ma thèse. Je remercie donc toutes les personnes qui ont contribué à la gestion d’ensemble de ces trois années. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Ensuite, les deux premières années de ma thèse furent l’occasion de découvrir les activités de recherche et d’enseignement dans le supérieur. J’ai été introduit à diverses communautés scien- tifiques lors de différents événements, dans l’ordre chronologique : Journées Contrôle Optimal, AG du réseau REUSE, journées du GDR MOA, journées SMAI-MODE, DigitAgora, séminaires EIR-A. Merci aux personnes avec qui j’ai pu échanger lors de ces rencontres, qui ont contribué au développe- ment de certaines idées et motivations de recherche. Merci également aux groupes d’étudiants à qui j’ai donné des cours, qui ont confirmé mon souhait de conserver l’enseignement parmi mes projets. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Á partir de la fin de la deuxième année, et jusqu’au dépôt du manuscrit, ma thèse entra dans une phase de concrétisation des idées, de production et de diffusion. J’ai été amené à présenter mes travaux lors des conférences FGS à Gijòn et CDC à Milan, et j’ai eu l’opportunité d’effectuer un séjour de trois mois chez des collègues à Porto (merci encore pour l’accueil). Grâce aux projets SMAI-BOUM, j’ai co-organisé une journée workshop avec de jeunes chercheurs, que je remercie à nouveau pour leur participation. De plus, durant cette période, j’ai pris pleinement part à la procé- dure de production d’articles, depuis la rédaction jusqu’à la publication. Merci aux personnes avec qui j’ai travaillé et qui ont contribué à mon apprentissage de l’exercice. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Enfin, les derniers mois de la thèse furent consacrés à la préparation de la soutenance. Je tiens à remercier tous les membres de mon jury qui ont accepté de prendre en charge l’évaluation de mes travaux, avec une mention particulière aux deux rapporteurs qui ont rédigé deux beaux rapports reflétant leur appréciation de mon manuscrit. Le jour de la soutenance, j’ai eu le plaisir de défendre mes travaux devant une vingtaine de personnes en présentiel, et une vingtaine d’autres en visio. Je remercie toutes les personnes (famille, amis, collègues, autres) qui ont pris le temps de venir et d’écouter ma présentation. Merci encore aux membres du jury pour leurs commentaires et la rédaction d’un compte rendu de soutenance très apprécié. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Je conclus avec des remerciements globaux, adressés aux personnes qui furent présentes tout au long de cette thèse. Mon équipe d’encadrants, qui ont pleinement rempli leurs rôles, avec des compétences et des qualités complémentaires. Mes collègues de l’unité d’accueil, auprès de qui ce fut un plaisir de passer ces trois années. Ma famille et mes amis, qui sont une source de motivation, pour sortir voir du monde, partager des moments, faire du sport ou d’autres activités. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Chaque personne concernée par ces remerciements saura se reconnaître parmi ces lignes, dans l’hypothèse où vous les lirez un jour, bien entendu. En tout cas, sachez que, contrairement à ce qu’on pourrait croire en lisant ces lignes, je n’ai pas oublié vos noms. Et j’ai écrit cette section en essayant de penser à tout le monde. [SOURCE: Page_3]

5 [SOURCE: Page_5]

Résumé Cette thèse étudie, à l’aide de la théorie de la commande optimale, le sujet de l’optimisation des systèmes d’irrigation des cultures en agronomie, à l’échelle de la parcelle, sous serre ou en champ extérieur, dans un contexte de ressources en eau limitées. Ce contexte spécifique fait jouer un rôle crucial au pilotage tactique de l’irrigation et aux prises de décisions de l’opérateur au cours du temps. En utilisant une formalisation mathématique de l’irrigation des cultures en termes de problème de commande optimale, l’objectif est de développer des outils théoriques afin de fournir des stratégies de décision efficaces pouvant se transposer au monde opérationnel. [SOURCE: Page_5]

La première partie de cette thèse se concentre sur l’analyse d’un problème d’irrigation dans un contexte de culture sous serre. Nous utilisons le modèle CCI (Controlled Crop Irrigation) qui est un modèle générique écrit sous forme de système dynamique contrôlé (non lisse), décrivant de façon simple les processus biophysiques d’ordre 1 et permettant une étude des propriétés intrinsèques liés à ces phénomènes physiques principaux. Notre étude nous amène d’abord à revisiter le principe Bang-Bang usuel dans une version portant sur les dynamiques affines par morceaux sous contraintes d’état et une version stochastique, permettant d’en déduire des propriétés structurelles de contrôles optimaux et de leurs trajectoires d’état associées. Ensuite, nous dérivons les conditions nécessaires d’optimalité en utilisant le Principe du Maximum de Pontryagin dans un cadre non-lisse, afin de caractériser plus précisément les solutions optimales de problèmes d’irrigation sujets à différents types de contraintes et de critères. Ces conditions, combinées avec les précédentes propriétés struc- turelles, aboutissent à une formulation d’une classe de politiques optimales sous forme de retours d’état paramétrés. Ceci signifie que la résolution du problème de commande optimale a priori difficile (en dimension infinie) revient à faire une optimisation en petite dimension. Cette petite dimensionnalité nous permet alors d’étudier à l’aide d’une exploration numérique globale les pro- priétés des solutions optimales, notamment la sensibilité de l’instant de prise de décision en fonction des paramètres du système (plante, sol, matériel), et les conséquences (bienfaits et inconvénients) de décisions sous-optimales. [SOURCE: Page_5]

La seconde partie se penche sur la question de l’application de ces résultats théoriques dans un cadre opérationnel plus proche du monde réel. Pour ce faire, nous adoptons tout d’abord une approche de double modélisation, consistant à établir des connections entre le modèle CCI (sim- ple, théorique) et le modèle Optirrig (opérationnel, pratique) afin de confronter et d’affiner leurs hypothèses et résultats. Nous décrivons en particulier l’objectif de tester les scénarios théoriques d’irrigation optimale extraits du modèle CCI, en les implémentant dans le modèle Optirrig et en éval- uant leurs performances dans un cadre pratique. De plus, nous tirons profit des techniques de “con- trôle à coût moyenné” pour formaliser le problème d’irrigation sous certains types d’incertitudes du monde réel. En particulier, nous nous intéressons à la recherche de solutions optimales robustes par rapport à la variabilité de paramètres du modèle les plus sensibles, ainsi qu’à la recherche de solutions qui optimisent “en moyenne” la production de biomasse vis-à-vis d’incertitudes météorologiques, décrites sous forme de sauts des variables d’état. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Abstract This thesis investigates, with the help of optimal control theory, the topic of optimizing crop irrigation systems in agronomy, at the plot scale, in greenhouse or in open-field conditions, in a context of limited water resources. This specific context gives a crucial role to the tactical irrigation piloting and to the decisions made by the operator over time. Using a mathematical formalism of crop irrigation in terms of an optimal control problem, the goal is to develop theoretical tools in order to provide efficient decision strategies that can be transposed to the operational world. [SOURCE: Page_5]

The first part of this thesis focuses on the analysis of an irrigation problem in a context of greenhouse agriculture. We utilize the CCI (Controlled Crop Irrigation) model, which is a generic model written as a controlled (non-smooth) dynamical system, describing order-1 biophysical pro- cesses in a simple way, and enabling a study of intrinsic properties related to these central physical phenomena. Our study first leads us to revisit the usual Bang-Bang principle to a version dealing with piecewise affine dynamics under state constraints and a stochastic version, allowing to deduce [SOURCE: Page_5]

6 [SOURCE: Page_6]

structural properties of optimal controls and their associated state trajectories. Then we derive the optimality necessary conditions through the Pontryagin Maximum Principle in a non-smooth frame- work, in order to more precisely characterize the optimal solutions to irrigation problems subject to various types of constraints and criteria. These conditions, combined with the aforementioned structural properties, result in a formulation of a class of optimal policies in terms of parameterized feedback. This means that solving the a priori difficult optimal control problem (in infinite dimen- sion) amounts to performing an optimization in small dimension. This small dimension thus allows us to study, through a global numerical exploration, the properties of optimal solutions, especially the sensitivity of the timing of decision-making with respect to the system parameters (plant, soil, material), and the outcomes (benefits and drawbacks) of sub-optimal decisions. [SOURCE: Page_6]

The second part addresses the question of applying these theoretical results in an operational framework closer to real-world conditions. To this end, we employ a double modeling approach, consisting in building connections between the CCI model (simple, theoretical) and the Optirrig model (operational, practical) in order to compare and refine their hypotheses and results. In particular we aim for testing the theoretical scenarios of optimal irrigation taken from the CCI model, by implementing them in the Optirrig model and evaluating their performances in a practical framework. In addition, we take advantage of “averaged cost control” techniques to formalize the irrigation problem under different types of real world uncertainties. In particular, we are interested in finding optimal solutions that are robust regarding the variability of the most sensitive model parameters, as well as finding solutions that optimize the biomass production “on average” with respect to meteorological uncertainties, described as jumps on the state variable. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Chapter 1 [SOURCE: Page_11]

General introduction [SOURCE: Page_11]

1.1       Application context: expectations in irrigation science 1.1.1     Water management in agriculture 1.1.1.1    Current situation of water management and the role of agriculture Over the past 60 years, the global water demand has increased, and rapid population growth, urban expansion, intensive agricultural practices have exacerbated the tensions on freshwater re- sources [136, 43, 126, 159, 192]. This continuously growing pressure on water resources causes concerns about long-term sustainability of the competitive use of water for irrigation practices and other sectors in general [80, 119, 193]. Water use conflicts, choice of crops and rotations, varietal selection, soil conservation practices, unconventional waters are all levers that may constrain or influence irrigation management. Furthermore, warming climate and human activities highlight the need to anticipate and make decisions under uncertainties [125, 86]. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Freshwater resources are vital for human and economic development of society, in particular through the production and supply of food. In many countries, particularly in arid and semi-arid regions, water scarcity has become a crucial topic, especially since the frequency, intensity and duration of droughts are increasing [188, 70, 185]. In fact, areas affected by droughts went up by 20%, leading to significant economic damages [126]. For example, summer months water scarcity is particularly high in southern Europe, and the impacts (such as water consumption increase or soil water content decline) are already happening in the Mediterranean basin [2, 116, 64, 56, 96, 135], especially when the water resources are not enough to meet all the local demands [16]. [SOURCE: Page_11]

In this context, agriculture (in particular irrigation) plays a main role for water management. To ensure food security under rapid population growth, a large amount of water is extracted from rivers (70% of freshwater diverted for agriculture irrigation comes from rivers) [118, 187]. Globally, food production accounts for 90% of the freshwater consumption, making irrigated agriculture the largest user of world’s freshwater resources [176, 201]. Only approximately 20% of the world’s croplands are irrigated, but this fraction contributes to about 40% of the global food production [147]. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Thus global food supply increasingly relies on irrigation, raising questions about the viability of freshwater resources, especially given the supply-demand imbalances and the recent drought events. In drylands, rainwater alone cannot sustain healthy crop production, and irrigation is essential [46]. As a results, in such regions, irrigation management (water abstraction, pumping, irrigation) directly decides crop yields and the consequent incomes [197]. Growing agricultural water demands have led to shortages and decreases in availability for other sectors, in particular in arid and semi-arid regions [183, 182]. [SOURCE: Page_11]

1.1.1.2    Expected evolution for the next decades In the next few decades, global water management is expected to face various challenges in- duced by combined pressures of demography, climate and agriculture. Climate change is expected [SOURCE: Page_11]

11 [SOURCE: Page_11]

## 12                                                      CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

to increase the variability of weather events and rainfall patterns and intensity, leading to more vulnerability to droughts and uncertainties about water availability. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Global population keeps rising rapidly and is projected to reach approximately 9.3 billion by 2050 (up from 7 billion in 2011) [180]. This growth is expected to generate at least a 50% increase in food demand and a 30% increase in water demand by 2030, thus potentially leading to shortages [197]. In order to match global food needs, agricultural production will have to increase by an estimated 33% by 2050 [197]. As a result, global freshwater consumption is also expected to rise significantly due to agriculture practices [61, 180]. [SOURCE: Page_12]

The intensification of competitive uses of water resources being exacerbated by growing demands for domestic, industrial and hydroelectric purposes, one can foresee a decrease in overall water availability [163]. In arid and semi-arid regions, the expected rise in frequency, intensity and duration of droughts (due to climate change dynamics) will likely reduce the current stock of freshwater resources, limiting crop development and yields [151, 168, 188]. Then irrigation is supposed to expand in the future in order to meet food security, however this expansion will take place in conditions where long-term agricultural management will be facing various sources of uncertainties and increasing pressure on water systems. [SOURCE: Page_12]

1.1.1.3   Changes in irrigation practices and targets in the near future [SOURCE: Page_12]

The anticipated evolution in climate, population and water demand has significant impacts on both agriculture and other water-dependent sectors [84, 85]. Increasing pressure on water resources may threaten the availability and quality of water for competitive uses, including agriculture [111]. The sustainable management of water resources will thus become a major issue, with global envi- ronmental and socio-economic consequences. [SOURCE: Page_12]

In the agricultural context, irrigation plays a crucial role in food security, however it is also a major driver of environmental impacts. The overuse of irrigation might lead to the depletion of wa- ter resources, the deterioration of water quality, and the degradation of ecosystems, notably through soil salinization. These impacts are particularly severe in dryland regions, where land and water resources are already under stress [147, 50, 55, 68]. The expansion of cultivated areas to increase production is constrained by urbanization (and unwillingness to disturb environment), making it essential to improve productivity on existing farmland. This includes increasing water productivity per unit of area of available land and water resources (or “crop per drop”) to help close the “yield gap” losses, exacerbated by climate vulnerability [20, 132, 128]. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Moreover, crop growth and yield are affected non-linearly by water availability, particularly un- der soil moisture deficit scenarios. Deriving reliable relationships between crop water use and yield thus remains a complex task [51, 169]. As a result, agricultural planning must move beyond the traditional practices. Instead, agriculture must head towards decision-making frameworks that in- tegrate water limitations and able to account for uncertainty, while keeping the objective to improve the system performances [103, 196]. While “precision” or “smart” irrigation practices suit the pur- pose of deficit irrigation and avoiding water waste, one might however obtain a “rebound effect” when small-scale efficiency induces an adverse effect on water resources at the regional scale. [SOURCE: Page_12]

These challenges highlight the importance of evaluating the joint impacts of multiple drivers on agricultural performance, as well as identifying how the constraints affect water management practices and efficiency. In the context of reliable, economically viable, and sustainable agricultural production increase, optimizing the allocation and use of limited water resources is not only a necessity for food security, but also a key research priority for the years to come. It further underlines the need to inform public policies with scientific results and prospectives, seeking social equity and fair data in relation to water management, connecting scientific approaches and operational declensions. [SOURCE: Page_12]

1.1. APPLICATION CONTEXT: EXPECTATIONS IN IRRIGATION SCIENCE                                          13 [SOURCE: Page_13]

1.1.2     Main issues in crop irrigation: recent advances and current chal- lenges 1.1.2.1   Questions in irrigation related to the aforementioned context, with a focus on operational decision making In regions affected by severe water scarcity, optimally managing water resources is critical [140]. The operational decisions taken in these contexts have direct implications for the sustainability and efficiency of agricultural practices. Among the key decision making clues is the timing of water allocation : crop yields are closely related not only to the total availability of water but also to the temporal distribution of soil water content throughout the growing season. In water-limited condi- tions, the ability to prevent crop water stress (especially at important development stages) depends on thoroughly timed irrigation, making irrigation scheduling a central issue in decision-making. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Achieving a sustainable equilibrium between available water resources and irrigation demands requires following robust strategies able to account for limitations while still being productive. The decisions must then integrate multiple factors, including climate variability, resource availability, and production targets. Poorly planned irrigation might lead to numerous insufficiencies and long-term problems, such as resource depletion, waterlogging or salinization (especially in arid and semi-arid areas having a thin room for maneuver) [164]. [SOURCE: Page_13]

On a wider scale than the plot, irrigation scheduling is only one part of water management chal- lenges. For example, these challenges include the conjunctive use planning (with multiple sources of water), groundwater level management, seawater intrusion prevention, cropping patterns, reservoir systems, or even solid waste water management [164]. Each of these issues adds complexity in the global decision-making process and highlights the important of integrated approaches. Ultimately, operational irrigation management would have to aim for improving production efficiency as well as anticipating the environmental impacts associated with unsustainable practices, using for example the “environmental footprint”, possibly in a way to discriminate relevant practices from those that are not (or less). Further, enlarging the targets would mean heading towards a different logic, as “robustness” instead of “performance”. [SOURCE: Page_13]

1.1.2.2   Advantages and drawbacks of existing approaches To address the growing pressure on water resources and improve irrigation outcomes, several ap- proaches and modelling tools have been developed, particularly for regions in which “rainfed” crops are not sufficiently productive anymore (as in arid and semi-arid areas). Among these, supplemental irrigation and deficit irrigation have emerged as potentially effective approaches to improve water productivity while dealing with limited water availability [132]. These strategies tend to parsimo- niously allocate irrigation water in order to target critical crop growth stages, ensuring convenient yield without requiring full irrigation coverage. It is possible to represent the water use and yield relationship, as resulting outcomes of different irrigation schedules, with crop kites [165]. These graphs can provide the capacity to visualize and assess water efficiency over a wide range of strate- gies and agroclimatic conditions. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Modeling and simulation tools, including those based on the FAO guidelines (such as Irrigation and Drainage papers 33, 56 and 66 [3, 169, 51, 100, 114, 195]) or dynamic models like AquaCrop [137, 110, 67, 63] and alternative approaches [33, 21, 148], are commonly used in order to develop decision-making support. The purpose of such models is to provide useful estimations of crop de- velopment and enable meaningful design of irrigation strategies, yet being conscious that modeling approximations embedded in simulations might carry error margins comparable to (or greater than) climate uncertainties, which might complexify the decision support process [130, 171]. [SOURCE: Page_13]

From a modeling point of view, various optimization frameworks have been developed to im- prove irrigation efficiency. Their formulations vary from complete to constrained, from discrete to continuous, from strategy-based to calendar-based, and may or may not take in account weather uncertainties [164]. It is also possible to consider ex-post analyses, real-time decision support, or ex- ante prospective works. The exhaustive optimization problem remains challenging due to the high [SOURCE: Page_13]

## 14                                                       CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

dimension of the problem (a naive formulation including daily decision for the irrigation volume would introduce numerous variables and constraints, and lead to computationally expensive resolu- tion). Besides, the simplifications induced by models might be questionable regarding biophysical processes, especially in situations with deficit irrigation (involving additional processes due to water stress) [25], needing cautious interpretation of results, and a proper scientific assessment of strategies. [SOURCE: Page_14]

The design of such models enables to develop Decision Support Systems (DSS) which help farm- ers by giving suggestions for irrigation timing, fertilization, crop rotation or other decisions. They can integrate soil moisture variables, plant growth stage, weather patterns, market conditions [200]. Various methodologies have been applied to DSS, such as Dynamic Programming, Stochastic ap- proaches, Artificial Intelligence for example, with their respective strengths and weaknesses (ability to adapt to sequential decision-making, dealing with non-linearity, limited in variables and memory required, dependent on data acquisition) [164]. Nevertheless, every DSS should face the challenges, difficulties and limitations due to the need to adapt in accordance to different farming types, objec- tives, philosophy and constraints [1]. However, we found relatively few approaches in this literature that propose tactical policies (over the whole agronomic season) in terms of feedback (most of the approaches deal with open-loop controls). [SOURCE: Page_14]

While these approaches contribute meaningfully to the development of practical irrigation strate- gies, they often require computationally expensive processes. In contrast, when model structure is compatible, analytical frameworks (such as tools from open-loop or closed-loop control theory) offer the possibility to derive irrigation policies with a more “high-level modeling” point of view. Opti- mizing irrigation can be formulated through deterministic approaches (using Optimal Control for example) or approaches incorporating rainfall variability (as Stochastic Control) as well as adaptive approaches (such as Model Predictive Control) [157, 158, 199, 83, 142, 189]. Analytical frameworks provide a solid mathematical exploration and enable some flexibility in managing the tradeoff be- tween the complexification of models and the search space approximation (i.e. finding a compromise in terms of formulation and resolution accuracies). This kind of approach facilitates a deeper un- derstanding of intrinsic properties of the system dynamics, and might help investigating decision policies in a structural point of view. [SOURCE: Page_14]

1.1.2.3    Why the use of optimal control theory is relevant to be investigated for irrigation problems Among the many promising directions of improvement for crop irrigation management, the in- tegration of automatic control and real-time feedback (or closed-loop) systems seems particularly interesting. Controlled irrigation potentially offers notable advantages: increased accuracy in water distribution, reduced waste, lower costs, and decrease in human error [120]. In particular, these benefits are relevant in the context of water scarcity where parsimonious use of water is essential to ensure both healthy crop production and sustainable resource management. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Recent advances in sensor-informed agricultural decisions and smart farming technologies involve the development of automated systems capable of monitoring soil moisture and crop growth in real time [23, 45, 123, 184]. These sensor-based monitoring systems (seen as feedback control systems), can dynamically adjust irrigation inputs according to current operating conditions, seeking to op- timize the chosen criterion. In particular, closed-loop strategies, where soil moisture measurements directly indicate the recommended irrigation volume, represent a natural application of control the- ory to agriculture. Such approaches enable the design of irrigation systems able to respond efficiently to varying environmental conditions, and to adapt the decisions in order to better follow the farmer’s objectives. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Several recent studies based on classical control methods (such as on-off control [71], PID [146], MPC [117, 121]) or machine learning techniques (such as fuzzy logic, multi-agent systems) [62] have shown potential, however they often assume full water availability or omit legal or technical con- straints on the use of water resources, making those considerations improvable regarding real-world conditions, especially for arid and semi-arid regions. [SOURCE: Page_14]

There remains significant room for improvement, aiming for an irrigation system able to be [SOURCE: Page_14]

1.1. APPLICATION CONTEXT: EXPECTATIONS IN IRRIGATION SCIENCE                                         15 [SOURCE: Page_15]

robust under unexpected environmental change, taking advantage of real-time optimization and control methodologies in a more systematic way [115]. Open key challenges include the resource allocation problem with competitive demands, the integration of long-term objectives or weather uncertainties, and sensor and actuator placement [37]. A generic, adaptable control framework that can account for various farming objectives and operational constraints would be a considerable progress. [SOURCE: Page_15]

In this context, the application of optimal control theory to irrigation management provides a generic methodology for optimizing water use, integrating real-time decision-making (by seeking feedback formulations) and long-term operating constraints (by setting a terminal target for the budget). This suggests a co-existence between different time horizons: the operational horizon (decision-making, few hours or days), the tactical horizon (cropping season or part of it), the strate- gic horizon (long-term view in which practices should be viable). Such considerations, combined with a probabilistic formalism of weather and climate, certainly represent a research direction to investigate in order to enhance both the efficiency and the robustness of controlled irrigation poli- cies. Achieving the development of such strategies would make their implementation in realistic operational conditions meaningful and impactful. [SOURCE: Page_15]

1.1.3     Motivation of the thesis 1.1.3.1   Research positioning As mentioned in the above section, among the existing approaches involving the control theory for studying crop irrigation problems, we choose to focus on an optimal control framework, in order to take advantage of the possibility to derive necessary conditions to seek optimal solutions. More- over, optimal control formulations allow us to carry out an optimization that goes beyond the local (or small window of time) optimization by integrating constraints and conditions that apply to the global time span. Aside from the numerical studies (see [108, 153, 95, 110]) whose optimization study either depends on some model restrictions or rely on open-loop scenarios, there exists several works that aim for a more analytical use of the optimal control theory. They usually consist in defin- ing a dynamical system representing their crop irrigation model, then introducing some hypotheses, and optimizing a cost function (which is linear in [157, 112, 19, 101] and quadratic in [113, 102]). Typically, their study follows the process of writing and deriving necessary conditions, in order to obtain at least properties on optimal control that can be explored through direct or indirect method, or even directly the expression of optimal controls. Moreover those structures of strategies will be generally valid for a wide class of operating conditions and parameter values [177, 75]. Theoretical works rely on simple dynamics (rather abstract as in [112, 101] or more physical as in [157, 19]), might contain an additional state constraint (as in [113]), might contain water use limitations ([19]) or assume full water availability ([112, 101]), and might ([112, 101]) or might not ([157, 19]) consider rainfall. The study [102] takes a different point of view and addresses the question of robustness, evaluating the sensitivity of the optimal solution with respect to poorly known parameters, through a non-linear programming formulation. [SOURCE: Page_15]

The previous work of [19] appears to be a good compromise between model simplicity and flex- ibility, and explicit physical sense. Its main weaknesses are the lack of consideration for critically low soil moisture levels, and the absence of rainfall and drainage contributions. This work uses the Pontryagin Maximum Principle in a non-differentiable framework to show, under certain conditions based on a simplified irrigation model ([91]), that there exists a non-trivial (yet parameterized in low dimension) structure of irrigation strategies (or controls) which maximize the biomass production in a situation of water scarcity. The originality of such structures relies in the emergence of “singular arcs” specifically at some given soil moisture setpoints. However this study is still incomplete since it does not take into account the water contributions from unknown precipitations. Moreover, the derived strategies present a major issue: under some conditions it is possible that the computed op- timal solution involves undergoing critically low soil water contents that lead to the crop’s death. A following study ([72]) investigated the problem with rainfall considerations, using the optimization numerical tool Bocop ([173]) based on a direct method (using the Ipopt library), foreseeing new directions for the development of solutions to the problem with rainfall and weather forecast. [SOURCE: Page_15]

## 16                                                     CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

However, the solutions found in these studies are unfortunately not directly applicable to opera- tional situations, either because of the lack of a suitable complex operational formulation, or because their optimization relies on an external numerical resolution via a direct method. For these reasons, this thesis will integrate a double modeling approach (see [73]) detailed in Chapter 5, consisting in making a correspondence (in some sense) between a small irrigation model and the Optirrig model ([34, 33]) which has been developed in view of operational purposes. The double modeling approach takes advantage of both small theoretical model and detailed physical model to derive efficient control laws for a complex system. Double modeling has rarely been considered with the primary objective of designing control strategies, but rather as a mean to conduct analyses on a calibrated reduced model to enhance the performances of a complex model. In this thesis, we seek to develop irrigation strategies on a small theoretical model and assess their efficiency on the model Optirrig. Notice, however, that the small model won’t be a reduced version of Optirrig, making their correspondence a real challenge. [SOURCE: Page_16]

1.1.3.2   Application framework: the Optirrig model The application framework relies in the Optirrig model, a crop irrigation numerical model aiming for the generation, analysis and optimization of irrigation scenarios (we use the same name “Op- tirrig” for the model and its numerical implementation as a software). The specificity of Optirrig compared to other existing model-embedded DSS is that it focuses on the declension of irrigation strategies rather than the detailed description of crop processes. The Optirrig model considers a relatively parsimonious modeling of the plant biophysical processes and the water transfer physical processes. Typically, the issue to be solved consists in “using less water than a given quota to obtain more than a given yield, with maximal economical benefits”. [SOURCE: Page_16]

To this end, the development of Optirrig requires a multisciplinary collaboration, from agronomy, hydrology, physics, mathematics and numerical science research fields, and its aim is to contribute to various operational purposes in crop irrigation management. These purposes divide in four main categories: ex-post analysis (assessment of passed practices and alternative choices), ex-ante anal- ysis (guidelines for adapting to climate change and to increasing constraints on water resources), real-time decision support (decision rules, watering thresholds, closed-loop control if measures are available), and tactical or strategic analysis (needs of major crops for support to public policies). [SOURCE: Page_16]

Most of the current developments aim for establishing reasoned irrigation strategies, in classical agricultural contexts (such as great crops in open-field conditions with aspersion irrigation), taking into account a restrictive usage of water and the need to think and promote alternative irrigation strategies (such as deficit irrigation). Those strategies might get economical “rewards” for their effi- ciency or virtuous features, modifying the nature of the economical problem and potentially shifting the optimal solutions for irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_16]

In this thesis, the objective is to provide innovative strategies embedded from theoretical frame- work into Optirrig. In particular, this thesis aims for enabling an efficient real-time operational use, by conceiving short-term decisions integrating long-term considerations, thus improving the current way to make such scenarios with uncorrelated considerations. In other words, the goal is to suggest irrigation decisions according to the yield that is still possible to obtain. [SOURCE: Page_16]

1.2       Framework for mathematical and methodological answers 1.2.1     Double modeling approach and challenges The double modeling approach consists in using two models, that may be coupled or sequential or interactive, in order to capture different aspects of a complex system, by taking advantage of the respective strengths of each model. This method follows the need to overcome single-model studies limitations, by integrating two points of view about the subject of study, that may correspond to different scales, processes or domains of application. For instance, in the crop irrigation field, one may consider models focusing on biophysical, economical or control aspects, for a management at the plant scale, the plot scale or the basin scale, and describing processes in a more detailed or more [SOURCE: Page_16]

1.2. FRAMEWORK FOR MATHEMATICAL AND METHODOLOGICAL ANSWERS                                          17 [SOURCE: Page_17]

abstract way. The usual methodology consists in identifying the compatibility of the two models, calibrating them, then performing integrated simulations. This double modeling method provides interesting features, such as improved realism, modularity and flexibility, and is a promising tool for enhanced decision support systems. However it might face several difficulties, such as model incompatibility, calibration complexity, or might increase the simulation costs. [SOURCE: Page_17]

In this thesis, the double-modeling challenge consists in establishing the connection between for- mal analytical approaches in the field of optimal control, represented by the home-made Controlled Crop Irrigation model (see Section 1.2.2), and a more physics-based approach in the field of water management, represented by the Optirrig model. The purpose is to take advantage of both types to derive efficient control laws for a quite complex crop irrigation problem, possibly allowing us to highlight and understand the emergence of decision-making phenomena (expected or not). This model connection and the progressive drift of results (see Figure 1.1) across disciplinary fields goes along with the necessity of keeping a single, overall objective: it is the same method or methodolog- ical framework that is first established on firm theoretical grounds, then illustrated in sufficiently controlled conditions, with eventually the objective of being applied slightly out of its conditions of validity. [SOURCE: Page_17]

As a result, this thesis project addresses the optimization of irrigation strategies in a context of climate change and water resources limitations, and their application to the Optirrig model. The suggested solution should provide a decision : in real-time, integrating seasonal management constraints like water quotas, integrating weather conditions with mid-term and long-term uncer- tainties. The double modeling approach allows the identification of intrinsic possibilities of the crop irrigation system to reach some given objectives while fulfilling some given constraints (see [4]). It is reasonable to apply this methodology from a control problem formulation (the CCI model) into Optirrig, since Optirrig was designed by focusing on the irrigation scenarios (or strategies) rather than a detailed crop physiology. Notice though that the compatibility of the CCI model and Op- tirrig is yet to be proven before considering to apply strategies from CCI into Optirrig. Moreover, underline that strategies that are optimal for the simple model are sub-optimal for the Optirrig model, but they can be compared with existing strategies to evaluate the benefit of the approach. Such association of optimal control theory and a more complex numerical model through the double modeling approach is original, since double modeling is a renown methodology but with very few applications in agronomy and water sciences. [SOURCE: Page_17]

1.2.2     Presentation of the Controlled Crop Irrigation model 1.2.2.1   Basis formalisms and dynamics Let us introduce the foundations of the model which is the main subject of analysis of this thesis, and that we named: “Controlled Crop Irrigation” model (CCI). In this section, we present its initial formulation and hypotheses, and we will detail the complementary elements specific to this thesis in Section 1.3.1. The CCI model is based on an initial formulation in [133] related to the FAO crop-water simulation model AquaCrop [170]. The formulation in [133] uses a system of Ordinary Differential Equations (ODE) to describe several biophysical processes involved in crop development, such as soil water contents dynamics, nitrogen uptake, canopy cover, biomass and yield. It addresses a focus on water management, then assuming than nitrogen is not a limiting factor and omitting its description. As a result, the formulation used is a simplification of the one in [133], in particular there is only three state variables : soil moisture (S), crop biomass (B) and water budget (V ) (see [91, 19, 28]). Basically, the CCI model is a parsimonious dynamical system of ODEs, suitable for applying the optimal control theory while containing the order-1 biophysical processes at play. [SOURCE: Page_17]

As in [133], soil properties are assumed to be spatially homogeneous with an even plant spacing, not needing to consider any spatial pattern or heterogeneity within the modeled area. With char- acteristics seen in such a spatially-averaged way, the CCI model consists in a small set of ordinary differential equations representing the interplay between soil moisture, biomass production and the remaining water amount from the original quota, during the crop growth period of the cropping season, denoted by a time interval [0, T ]. The CCI model is written as a continuous-time dynamical [SOURCE: Page_17]

The basis equation of study is the following, describing the balance of soil water content: [SOURCE: Page_18]

∆S V ol      = −T R − EV + IR + RA − DR,                                                   (1.1) ∆t where V ol ∆S ∆t represents the variation of quantity of water (V ol∆S) over a time interval (∆t) and in a soil reservoir of size V ol (recalling that the spatial homogeneity allows us to consider this quantity normalized per unit square meter). The notations T R, EV , IR, RA and DR respectively denote plant transpiration, soil evaporation, irrigation, rainfall and drainage rates, i.e. variations during the time interval ∆t. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Following [91] and [73], we now detail the expression of each term. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Plant transpiration T R is calculated as the reference evapotranspiration ET$_0$ (or climatic de- mand) multiplied by the water stress related coefficient KS (or transpiration efficiency) and by the percentage of canopy cover φ (or radiation interception efficiency, see [11]). Given that the transpi- ration efficiency depends on the current soil moisture, and considering that quantities are varying over time, the instantaneous plant transpiration rate can be written as [SOURCE: Page_18]

T R(t) = ET$_0$ (t)KS (S(t))φ(t).                                                  (1.2) [SOURCE: Page_18]

Soil evaporation EV is calculated as the reference evapotranspiration ET$_0$ (or climatic demand) multiplied by the soil water content related coefficient KR (or evaporation efficiency) and by the percentage of soil not covered by canopy 1 − φ (or radiation not intercepted). Given that the evaporation efficiency depends on the current soil moisture, and considering that quantities are varying over time, the instantaneous soil evaporation rate can be written as [SOURCE: Page_18]

EV (t) = ET0 (t)KR (S(t))(1 − φ(t)).                                                 (1.3) [SOURCE: Page_18]

1.2. FRAMEWORK FOR MATHEMATICAL AND METHODOLOGICAL ANSWERS                                             19 [SOURCE: Page_19]

The irrigation rate is taken as the maximal irrigation rate Fmax (or capacity of the material) multiplied by the percentage of irrigation valve opening u (or normalized irrigation). Considering that quantity u is time-dependent, we can write the instantaneous irrigation rate as IR(t) = Fmax u(t).                                       (1.4) Soil water variation induced by rainfall RA is calculated as the quantity of precipitation r (per unit square meter) multiplied by the coefficient α (or rainfall infiltration efficiency). Considering that quantity r is time-dependent, we can write the instantaneous variation induced by rainfall as RA(t) = αr(t).                                          (1.5) Drainage DR is calculated as the maximal drainage rate ksat (or saturation water losses) multi- plied by the coefficient KD (or drainage efficiency). Given that the drainage efficiency depends on the current soil moisture, and considering that quantities are varying over time, we can write the instantaneous drainage rate as DR(t) = ksat KD (S(t)).                                  (1.6) All quantities KS (S(t)), KR (S(t)), KD (S(t)), φ(t), u(t), α are defined as efficiencies (or percent- ages), thus belong to [0, 1]. The crop functions KS , KR and KD are piecewise affine and continuous functions of the soil moisture S, and are parameterized by characteristic values of soil water content [3, 73], in ascending order : Srw the residual water content, Spw the permanent wilting point, S ∗ the point of incipient water stress, Sf c the point of field capacity, Ssat the soil moisture saturation point. Typical shapes found in the literature are represented in Figure 1.2. In the following, we will always set Sf c to be equal to 1, as we interpret the quantity S to be the ratio between the amount of water in the system and the available space in the soil that the water may fill. As a consequence, situations where S would be greater than 1 correspond to overfilling, and that means there is too much water compared to the capacity of the soil to assimilate it, thus generating drainage. [SOURCE: Page_19]

1.2.2.2    Model hypotheses First let us precise the conditions given over the different parameters related to biophysical processes, whose information is gathered in the different constants involved in the formulation of Equations (1.7) to (1.9). In fact, all the quantities ET$_0$ , V ol, Fmax , ksat , α and k^3 are positive by definition, and in a healthy operational context the capacity of the irrigation material is likely to be greater than the climatic demand. Hence the following assumption. [SOURCE: Page_20]

Assumption 1.2.1. The constants k^1 , k^2 , ksat , α and k^3 satisfy [SOURCE: Page_20]

k^1 > 0 ,   k^2 > 1 ,   ksat > 0 ,   α>0,      k^3 > 0. [SOURCE: Page_20]

The radiation interception efficiency φ is an increasing function of time and taking values in [0, 1], related to the canopy growth throughout the season, hence giving more weight to evaporation early in the season then to transpiration later in the season. Increasing φ values also means there is no human intervention on the canopy, like for example cutting branches and leaves of the plants during the crop growth phase. Reliable hypotheses on the shape of φ will be made later for numerical purposes. [SOURCE: Page_20]

Assumption 1.2.2. The function φ : [0, T ] → [0, 1] is increasing and of class C 1 . [SOURCE: Page_20]

The crop coefficients KS , KR and KD follow empirical laws derived from experimental data (see [3]), and we consider only structural hypotheses in order to preserve some flexibility. [SOURCE: Page_20]

Following the dynamics presented in the previous section, and the hypotheses introduced in this section, we aim at studying some formulations of optimal control problems that will be detailed later. These formulations may contain additional conditions or constraints according to the operational context, and the study will carry out all the resulting difficulties. [SOURCE: Page_21]

1.2.3     Issues, difficulties and theoretical background As discussed previously, this thesis focuses on the study of several optimization problems for- mulated as optimal control problems under constraints. More precisely, the problems involve a dynamical system (the CCI model) over a time interval with a fixed horizon T , various criteria formulated as terminal costs, various terminal targets, and some state constraints (detailed later). The dynamics are non-linear, non-autonomous and non-smooth with respect to the state, but so that they are affine with respect to the control u. Such optimal control problems are in general non trivial to tackle and require a rigorous and deep analysis [36, 104, 35, 190, 75]. Our study case however presents a particularity, since the dynamics are regionally affine (and continuous) with respect to the state, and we will consider state constraints. As a consequence, the non-smoothness of the dynamics resides in a discrete set (the junctions of the affine parts), a finite number of points where the dynamics is non-differentiable. Everywhere else, i.e. over a dense set of the state space, the dynamics are locally affine. [SOURCE: Page_21]

For globally linear (or affine) optimal control problems, there exists a literature covering a very wide range of topics and a great variety of results. In particular, for time-optimal problems relying on linear (or affine) dynamics, there exists a classical result about structural properties of the opti- mal solutions. Indeed, it is well-known (see for example [79]) that there exists an optimal control taking values in the set of extreme controls, for a compact convex set of controls (this result is called the “bang-bang principle”). Since our subject of work involves dynamics that are not linear (nor affine), it is not possible to make such a statement. However, as our system dynamics are regionally affine, it may seem intuitive that we should be able to extract some structural information about optimal solutions with the help of the dynamics structure and the well-known classical bang-bang principle. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Note that our irrigation problem will be formulated with intrinsic state constraints that will be detailed in Section 1.3.1. It is well-known that state constrained optimal control problems are far [SOURCE: Page_21]

## 22                                                      CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

from easy to solve, and possess the particularity to generate jumps in the adjoint vector. Various works (see [122, 78, 87, 152, 174]) provide some results and techniques able to deal with such difficul- ties, under different kinds of hypotheses (such as order of the state constraint, junction conditions, state augmentation, regularization). The idea of adding a state variable (whose dynamics typically accounts for the state constraint violation), and replacing the running state constraint by a terminal target, present the promising feature to make the adjoint vector continuous. However, one might observe (due to the addition of an adjoint component) a situation corresponding to the degeneracy of the previous formulation, which does not give any useful information when deriving the necessary conditions. In this thesis, we combine this idea with a budget penalization that avoids the addition of a state variable, but requires to modify the formulation, and thus prove that it remains an equiv- alent problem. [SOURCE: Page_22]

One of the main objectives of this thesis is to find irrigation scenarios in terms of control strate- gies, preferably formulated as feedback controls. Thanks to the optimal control formulation, it is possible to write some necessary optimality conditions (using the non-smooth Pontryagin Maximum Principle (PMP) [134, 98]) and derive them in order to aim for constructive properties of optimal controls. Moreover, the non-differentiable feature of our system dynamics brings additional diffi- culty into the analysis, since it will require to derive a non-differentiable version of the PMP (see for example [36, 35]) using especially the notion of generalized gradients. This method may or may not result in a completely analytical description of optimal solutions, but it can at least typically provide some structural properties over the nature of candidates for optimal controls. It is then customary to complement these properties by a numerical solving through a direct or an indirect method in order to approximately find the optimal solutions. In our study, we will not only derive the optimality necessary conditions, but we will aim for providing a complete feedback solution without the need of a numerical complementary analysis. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Then, thinking of operating application, it is usually interesting not only to know about the structural properties of optimal irrigation policies, but also to explain how this structure depends on the physical components of our irrigation system. In order to provide such insights about the drivers of some model outputs, it is customary to project a numerical model exploration, provided that we have sufficient information about the ranges and distributions of model parameter values. A simple, inexpensive way to explore the model is to conduct parameter perturbations using the “one factor at a time” method (see for example [127]). However such method is not suitable for non-linear models and unable to detect effects from parameters interactions. Therefore we need to consider more expensive methods, such as global sensitivity analysis [41, 166, 57] through a global numerical exploration and variance-based computational methods [88, 150]. This method is suitable for our investigation, but will require a significant amount of runs over sufficiently wide ranges of parameter in order to extract effective results. Moreover, we may consider taking advantage of such a numerical exploration to conduct a complementary analysis about the effects of decision pertur- bations on the model outputs, over the global exploration settings. The main challenge to overcome for such numerical analyses is the computational cost. [SOURCE: Page_22]

In the open-field conditions context, the dynamics involve a non-deterministic term to take into account the probabilistic behaviour of rainfall sequences. There exists several ways to consider and model such non-deterministic phenomena, from fully stochastic formulations to parameterized laws of probability or the use of markovian processes. In this thesis, the choice went towards a probabilis- tic formalism, considering that each sequence of annual rainfall will be represented as a time-varying function defined over the cropping season interval [0, T ]. At each time t, it should represent the current rainfall rate. Since the sequence of annual rainfall is not known a priori, we consider a finite set of all possible sequences of annual rainfall, and we assume that each sequence has some known probability to actually occur. The optimal control problem formulation will thus shift from a single controlled soil moisture equation to a set (finite in our case) of controlled soil moisture equations, each one of these corresponding to a different sequence of rainfall. However the control has to be common to all these scenarios and represents the generic approach, a decision that is able to take into account any possible future. The criterion to be optimized will become an average of the indi- vidual costs associated to each equation, weighted by the corresponding given probability that each sequence may occur. Such problem formulation is called “averaged cost control” problem [12, 13], [SOURCE: Page_22]

1.3. CONTRIBUTIONS TO THE OPTIMIZATION OF CROP IRRIGATION                                               23 [SOURCE: Page_23]

and provides a framework to apply optimality necessary conditions that are somewhat similar to the case with only one equation, where the additional difficulty obviously relies in the analysis of the consequences derived from the weighted Hamiltonian maximization. [SOURCE: Page_23]

Finally, it has been mentioned that annual rainfall sequences will be represented by time-varying “inputs”. In fact, there is different possibilities to chose the type of inputs we could use. In our work we decided to consider that rainfall events might occur on various time scales, while the physical process of infiltration and assimilation by the soil can be considered as a slow process. As a consequence, according to the nature of rainfall that may occur especially in arid and semi-arid regions (relatively high rate over a limited period of time), it is reasonable to assume that rainfall events could be modeled as instantaneous. This results in representing a possible sequence of annual rainfall with a (finite) set of instants (dates of rainfall event ti ) and their corresponding depth (hi ), thus allowing the soil moisture state variable to present some jumps (or discontinuities) at the times of instantaneous rainfall events. The system dynamics will then be written as a differential equation complemented with state jumps and extended theoretical tools will be needed to carry out the aforementioned analysis. To this end, we refer to [160, 161, 139, 124, 44, 191, 198] describing how dynamical systems with state discontinuities may be dealt with, in particular how to transform it into a standard dynamical system (using a change of time scaling), in order to apply and derive standard optimality necessary conditions. [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.3       Contributions to the optimization of crop irrigation 1.3.1     Contribution to the CCI model and to the irrigation problem for- mulation First, in order to prevent critically low levels of soil water content that would lead to severe plant degradation or early senescence (or even crop’s death), we added to the initial CCI model formulation a state constraint on the soil moisture representing the risk aversion or inclination of the operator regarding the tolerance to water stress. More precisely, the system is subject to the following state constraint: ∀t ∈ [0, T ], Sope ≤ S(t),                            (1.11) where Sope is called the operating constraint (or tolerance threshold), and verifies Spw < Sope < S ∗ . [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.3.1.1    Operational contexts The dynamics Equation (1.7) possess a non-deterministic term due to the rainfall uncertainties. However, it is possible to consider agricultural practices where rainfall does not occur, and where the variations of weather conditions in general have low impact on the crop development. Indeed, greenhouses can even enable to tend towards a fully controlled crop irrigation system, provided that the operator is able to maintain a stable temperature, and uniform wind and air humidity condi- tions. In such a framework of controlled greenhouse conditions, it justifies the use of deterministic dynamics, and we can just remove the term of rainfall without any more concern. [SOURCE: Page_23]

An implicit consequence of fully controlled greenhouse irrigation systems is that the operator is able to command irrigation with a rather high precision (thanks to sensor-based monitoring for example). As a result, the operator will likely avoid wasting water, i.e. they should not reach a situation where the soil moisture would exceed S = 1 (which would generate water losses by drainage). Therefore the state constraint Equation (1.11) in the context of greenhouse becomes ∀t ∈ [0, T ], Sope ≤ S(t) ≤ 1.                                (1.12) Moreover, as mentioned above, fulfilling the new state constraint Equation (1.12) is equivalent to consider that drainage is no longer involved in the soil water content dynamics. Consequently the equation ruling soil moisture variations in the controlled greenhouse context is now Ṡ(t) = k^1 − φ(t)KS (S(t)) − (1 − φ(t))KR (S(t)) + k^2 u(t) .            (1.13)  [SOURCE: Page_23]

Finally, this context will be investigated in detail in Chapter 3 and Chapter 4, where the de- terministic formulation allows us to derive optimality necessary conditions from the Pontryagin [SOURCE: Page_23]

## 24                                                      CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

Maximum Principle, leading to the design of a class of optimal structures of solutions capturing some intrinsic properties of the crop dynamics. This way, we will be able to extract information from the inner “soil-plant” system without environmental disturbances, justifying the interest of such a deterministic formulation. [SOURCE: Page_24]

It is also possible to keep the non-deterministic dynamics of Equation (1.7), which means that we accept to take into account the effects of rainfall over our study system. This implies placing our study in the framework where rainfall events occur, i.e. in open-field conditions. Furthermore, such weather events are by nature unknown in advance and present a wide range of variability, thus might have significant impacts on the instantaneous soil moisture balance. In particular, an intense rainfall event, involving great amounts of water in a short period of time, might overfill the soil reservoir and generate a non-negligible drainage, which increases up until a saturation level above the field capacity S = 1. [SOURCE: Page_24]

As a consequence, the use of both terms of rainfall and drainage in Equation (1.7) is well justified in open-field conditions. Moreover, even if the operator has full control over the irrigation amounts and their precision, they cannot prevent the soil moisture to reach levels above the field capacity, thus resulting in some undesired yet unavoidable water losses. The state constraint S(t) ≤ 1 is thus removed, and only Equation (1.11) is sufficient to reflect the margin of risk aversion (or inclination) of the operator. [SOURCE: Page_24]

This way, considering a study case in open-field conditions has the advantage to get closer to actual operational situations, because assuming fully controlled irrigation might be seen as a slighlty too strong hypothesis, due to existing variability in the biophysical processes and the crop itself. Therefore adding uncertainty to the analysis is a significant argument to claim that we are now looking for irrigation strategies that present some robustness with respect to perturbations. Fur- thermore, taking into account an unknown future implicitly means that the designed strategies will be generic, able to adapt to any upcoming uncertain event, and that the strategy relies on a system- atic methodology. This might be particularly interesting as a support of public policies to operating decisions. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Finally, this context is the main subject of investigation in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6. More precisely, in Chapter 5 we will explore the possibility of evaluating the performance of the candidate strategies “greenhouse optimal solutions” in situations close to real-world conditions, and conclude about their eventual robustness to uncertainty. Then, Chapter 6 introduces the analytical frame- work that aims for deriving optimality necessary conditions in a similar way to the “greenhouse” deterministic context, but presenting much more complicated challenges mostly due to the fact that the soil moisture trajectories associated to all possible rainfall sequences must be controlled by the same uniform decision. [SOURCE: Page_24]

1.3.1.2   Deficit irrigation framework Remark 1.3.1. Notice that the worst case scenario of the open-field context would be that of getting zero precipitation throughout the whole season, which would formally coincide with a greenhouse context (at least in the way we are simulating climatic conditions, via ET 0 only). It is only a formal comparison and does not mean that greenhouse crops will be worse, since greenhouse in fact protects the crop from climate aspects so that some parameters could be different than in open-field conditions. Still, it allows us to define a uniform framework to represent the situation where water resources are limited, such as the already mentioned water scarcity in arid and semi-arid areas. In both greenhouse context and open-field worst case scenario, the soil moisture will decrease if there is no irrigation (i.e. u = 0). Moreover, according to the biomass production dynamics, the crop does not suffer any stress (hence production is maximal) when the soil moisture is above the setpoint S ∗ . As mentioned before, our study will consider situations where full water availability can not be satisfied, in other words the limitations in water resources must generate water stress to some extent. Let us then define the least consuming irrigation chronicle such that the soil moisture trajectory is sufficient to ensure no water stress, and denote this irrigation u∗ . Since the initial condition of soil [SOURCE: Page_24]

## 26                                                              CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION

producing more than a given yield, while ensuring maximal economic benefits”. This issue can lead to different kinds of optimization direction. In particular, we may choose if there is strict obligations or if there is flexibility on some variables. As we want to apply control theory, the strict obligations will be formulated as mathematical constraints, and the flexible variables will play the role of the criterion to be optimized. Typically, we might have a strict limitation of water access and have freedom over the biomass production (as in [19]), or conversely we might have a strict objective for biomass production and keep freedom over the water use (as in [157, 112]). Alternatively, we might not have any strict limitation or objective, and rather looking for economic optimization without any regards on the values of water use and biomass production. In this thesis, we will tackle all these study cases. We then introduce three optimization problems corresponding to three natural points of view about the operational targets and the criteria to be optimized. [SOURCE: Page_26]

• (P 1) : maximizing the biomass production at the end of the season, when using a limited quota of water, i.e. maximizing B(T ) under the terminal condition V (T ) ≤ Q where Q is the initial available water. [SOURCE: Page_26]

• (P 2) : minimizing the water consumption to reach a certain target of biomass, i.e. minimizing V (T ) under the terminal condition B(T ) ≥ Y where Y is the objective of biomass production at the end of the season. [SOURCE: Page_26]

• (P 3) : maximizing a financial balance between biomass sales and water consumption expenses, i.e. maximizing the value g(B(T )) − c(V (T )) where g and c are given functions representing the earnings and spendings, with g(0) = c(0) = 0, g ′ > 0 and c′ > 0. [SOURCE: Page_26]

These problems formulations are in fact subject to the “admissibility” question. More precisely, we must look for control functions u that aim to optimizing the respective criteria, while fulfilling the different constraints (over the values taken by u, the values taken by the consequent trajectory of S, the terminal condition). If a control u is able to satisfy all the mentioned conditions, then it will be called “admissible”. For each problem (P 1), (P 2) and (P 3), and for each context declension, there exists some set of admissible controls. As a result, the optimization will only take place amongst the admissible controls corresponding to each study case. The nature of such admissible sets will be detailed later for some more specific cases. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Consequently, we prepare ourselves to formulate the irrigation problems as the following optimal control problems. [SOURCE: Page_26]

be detailed in Chapter 3), we formally define [SOURCE: Page_26]

(P 1) :    sup      B(T ), u(·)∈AC 1 [SOURCE: Page_26]

(P 2) :     inf     V (T ), u(·)∈AC 2 [SOURCE: Page_26]

(P 3) :    sup      g(B(T )) − c(V (T )). u(·)∈AC 3 [SOURCE: Page_26]

1.3.2      Extension of the bang-bang principle for piecewise affine dynamics As mentioned previously, the system dynamics are piecewise affine (and continuous) with respect to the state, and affine in control. Since we aim for providing structural properties about optimal solutions to the irrigation problem, it is reasonable to investigate the well-known bang-bang principle for globally linear (or affine) problems, and to seek to extend such a result in some sense for piecewise affine systems under state constraints. In Section 2.1, as an extension of the classical bang-bang principle for linear systems, we show that for non-autonomous piecewise affine optimal control problems with state constraints, the bang-bang principle does not hold globally even when the dynamics is continuous with respect to the state variable. However, we show that there exist optimal trajectories that are generated by extreme controls at the loci where the dynamics is differentiable, which is a weaker version of the classical bang-bang statement. Moreover we provide two examples which highlight the potential existence of singular arcs exactly at the loci of non-differentiability of [SOURCE: Page_26]

1.3. CONTRIBUTIONS TO THE OPTIMIZATION OF CROP IRRIGATION                                               27 [SOURCE: Page_27]

dynamics, i.e. at the boundaries of the regions. More precisely, consider the following dynamical system: Ẋ = F (t, X) + B(t)u,                                (1.17) where X ∈ Rn and u ∈ U, with U the set of measurable functions taking values in a convex compact set U ⊂ Rr . Also, the drift function F is defined by [SOURCE: Page_27]

F (t, X) = Ai (t)X + Ci (t),    (t, X) ∈ Pi ,                        (1.18) [SOURCE: Page_27]

where Ai and Ci are n × n− and n × 1− time measurable matrix-valued functions, B is a n × r− time measurable matrix-valued function, and F is assumed to be continuous. In addition, the sets Pi define a countable partition of the set Ω of the trajectory constraints. In this framework, we show the following result, illustrated by Figure 1.3. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Theorem 1.3.2 (Extended bang-bang principle). Considering a minimum-time control problem subject to the non-autonomous regional affine settings Equation (1.18), if the initial condition X$_0$ ∈ Rn belongs to the attainability set of the target Γ ⊂ Rn , then there exists an optimal solution composed of a succession of bang-bang arcs over time interval where the trajectory belongs to a region P̊i , and possible singular arcs along the boundaries ∂Pi . [SOURCE: Page_27]

Remark 1.3.3. Notice that the locus ∂Pi might be an interface between two regions but might also belong to the boundary of the domain Ω. [SOURCE: Page_27]

We can observe that the trajectory corresponds to a “bang-singular-bang” control, and that the singular part is exactly along boundaries of the space partition. [SOURCE: Page_27]

This result may be then applied to the crop irrigation optimal control problem in the context of greenhouse. Indeed, this context formulation only involves a piecewise affine (and continuous) dynamics, due to the definition of the crop coefficients KS and KR . As a consequence, we deduce from the extended bang-bang principle for piecewise affine systems that there exists optimal solutions fulfilling structural properties consisting in bang arcs when the state trajectory travels inside an affine region, and may contain singular arcs only at the non-differentiable points of the dynamics, i.e. at the points Srw , Spw , S ∗ , or along state constraints. This property will provide a significant help in Chapter 3 where we aim for describing the structures of optimal irrigation policies. [SOURCE: Page_27]

1.3.3     Extension of the bang-bang principle in stochastic settings In view of similar structural properties of optimal solutions to the irrigation problem taking into account the rainfall uncertainties, we may think once again of a similar bang-bang like property. [SOURCE: Page_27]

28                                                                         CHAPTER 1. GENERAL INTRODUCTION [SOURCE: Page_28]

However, it may be difficult to directly head towards an extension involving both piecewise affine dynamics and a non-deterministic term. A first step is to investigate the bang-bang principle for globally linear (or affine) dynamics containing a stochastic term. To this end, we wrote the stochastic term as a standard brownian noise, enabling a rather generic analysis for different kinds of applications. In Section 2.2, we revisit the classical bang-bang principle, established for deterministic affine controlled dynamics, in a stochastic framework when the dynamics is subject to a brownian motion. We show that such a principle does not hold in general in this setting, in the sense that the reachable sets (with any control and with only extreme controls) do not coincide. However, with stochastic controls, we show, under some conditions, that Mayer problems with criterion in expectation and a compact control set are equivalent to the same problems with controls taking values in the closed convex hull of the control set. More precisely, consider the following dynamical system: [SOURCE: Page_28]

1.3.4       Optimal structures of crop irrigation strategies in greenhouse context In the context of fully controlled greenhouse crop irrigation (i.e. without environmental variabil- ity), we look for structural properties of optimal solutions that are intrinsic to the crop development dynamics. In Chapter 3, we investigate the optimal control problem of crop irrigation with such non-autonomous and non-smooth dynamics corresponding to the aforementioned greenhouse con- text. Depending on contexts and objectives, several formulations associated to different constraints and criteria can be derived. Non-smoothness makes the problems a priori more difficult to study (from the numerical point of view, a direct method requires a regularization), but our work will [SOURCE: Page_28]

1.3. CONTRIBUTIONS TO THE OPTIMIZATION OF CROP IRRIGATION                                           29 [SOURCE: Page_29]

precisely relies on these non-differentiable points to describe the structure of solutions. Our work aims at providing optimal feedback solutions for these problems by deriving and analyzing the optimality necessary conditions. To this end, we assemble the different problems into a common formulation, and we carry out a dedicated way of handling state constraints, before applying the non-differentiable Pontryagin Maximum Principle without state constraint. We show that all opti- mal irrigation strategies belong to a family of simple parameterized time-varying feedback controls, for a large class of objectives, and suitable for computational purposes. More precisely, consider the soil moisture balance from Equation (1.13), the state contraints Equation (1.12), the hypotheses Assumptions 1.2.1 to 1.2.4, and the optimization problems defined in Definition 1.3.3. [SOURCE: Page_29]

We first show in Proposition 3.3.2 that an optimal solution to a reformulated problem, in the sense that we replace a state constraint (thus allowing more state trajectories and more admissible controls) by a penalization inside the budget consumption dynamics, is in fact an admissible control of the initial constrained problem. We show here that the penalization is exact in the sense that an optimal solution has necessarily a null penalization. As a result, it is equivalent to solve the uncon- strained optimal control problem, and it allows us to derive optimality necessary conditions without state constraints. After showing some properties satisfied by optimal solutions (especially that the terminal targets of their respective problem formulation are saturated Proposition 3.3.5), we write the optimality necessary conditions from the non-differentiable Pontryagin Maximum Principle and we derive the consequent properties about the adjoint variables. First we show that there is no abnormal extremal, then we use the extended bang-bang principle for piecewise affine dynamics (see Theorem 2.1.5) to provide structural conditions over the optimal solutions, and a brief analysis of the associated switching function has implications on the possible number of switches (see Proposi- tion 3.4.8). Finally, we gather all the above properties to conclude with Theorem 3.4.9 showing that the optimal irrigation policies can be formulated as time-varying parameterized feedback controls, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_29]

1.3.5          Exploring the optimality of Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies We provide in Chapter 3 a class of parameterized irrigation strategies in the context of green- house water management, but the result is still lacking of some crucial elements. In particular, even if it has been proven that it is computationally affordable to determine the optimal parameter values for the optimal irrigation policy, the theoretical result does not explain the relationship between the context and the computed optimal values, or the relative efficiency of optimal strategies compared to less efficient practices. It turns out that the properties of the Controlled Crop Irrigation (CCI) model led to optimal solutions written as Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies. In Chapter 4, we aim at studying both the optimal TBIS (the number of thresholds, their values and the most influential parameters which dictate the shape of optimal irrigation scenarios, see their shape declen- sion in Figure 1.5), and their sub-optimal features (the effects of errors made on their determination). [SOURCE: Page_30]
