1.5. INTRODUCTION GÉNÉRALE                                                                           55 [SOURCE: Page_55]

ttent de traiter ces incertitudes, mais nous avons choisi dans cette thèse d’adopter une approche “déterministe” (en un certain sens), car elle permet d’envisager une extension des arguments déjà explorés dans Chapters 2 and 3. À cette fin, nous introduisons un outil théorique appelé “contrôle à coût moyenné” (ou “averaged cost control”), qui permet de formuler des problèmes de contrôle optimal sous incertitude comme un ensemble de scénarios possibles, chacun associé à une probabilité d’occurrence. Il s’agit alors de rechercher un contrôle commun à tous les scénarios, et qui soit opti- mal au sens où il optimise le coût moyen sur l’ensemble de ces scénarios. L’objectif de l’introduction de cette approche est de l’appliquer à des situations plus proches des conditions réelles, comme les cadres dans lesquels de telles situations peuvent être formalisées et que nous projetons d’étudier plus en détail. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Figure 1.17: Exemple d’un ensemble de dix profils possibles pour la fonction paramètre φ. Ces fonctions représentent la variabilité de la croissance des cultures qui peut se produire en situation réelle, ou qui peut être connue de façon approximative. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Tout d’abord, rappelons que les paramètres du système sont en général connus de manière approximative, ce qui implique que l’optimalité d’une solution peut être sensible à la variabilité de ces paramètres estimés. En particulier, nous avons mis en évidence dans le Chapter 4 que le temps optimal de décision est principalement dicté par la forme de la fonction φ. En conséquence, nous étudions le problème d’irrigation formulé dans le cadre du contrôle à coût moyenné, afin de rechercher des stratégies d’irrigation tenant compte de la variabilité potentielle de la fonction φ (voir Figure 1.17), ce qui peut être vu comme une propriété de robustesse. Nous cherchons donc des stratégies d’irrigation qui optimisent la production moyenne de biomasse en tenant compte des incertitudes sur la fonction φ. Pour cela, nous considérons que φ appartient à un ensemble fini de profils possibles φi , chacun associé à une probabilité d’occurrence µi . Nous supposons que la fonction est initialement donnée avec une certaine probabilité, de façon inconnue, mais qu’elle n’est pas modifée au cours du temps. Nous formalisons alors notre problème d’irrigation en dupliquant la dynamique de base autant de fois qu’il y a de fonctions φi . Nous obtenons ainsi un problème de contrôle optimal “déterministe” de grande dimension, pour lequel il existe une version adaptée des conditions nécessaires d’optimalité. L’établissement de ces conditions permettra d’obtenir des propriétés utiles à la recherche et à la caractérisation des solutions optimales. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Ensuite, rappelons que nous visons l’application des stratégies d’irrigation en conditions de plein champ, et qu’il est donc nécessaire de prendre en compte l’occurrence des précipitations, lesquelles sont par définition non déterministes. Par conséquent, nous projetons d’étudier le prob- lème d’irrigation en tenant compte de ces incertitudes liées aux précipitations, et nous souhaitons utiliser le cadre du contrôle à coût moyenné, en considérant qu’une séquence annuelle de précip- itations appartient à un ensemble fini de séquences possibles, chacune associée à une probabilité d’occurrence. Toutefois, nous avons précédemment mentionné que les précipitations pourront être considérées comme des événements instantanés générant des sauts dans la variable d’état d’humidité du sol. Les dynamiques du système seront alors écrites sous forme d’équations différentielles com- plétées par des sauts d’état, ce qui ne constitue pas un cadre standard pour l’application directe [SOURCE: Page_55]

de la théorie du contrôle à coût moyenné. Par conséquent, nous cherchons à utiliser une technique de reformulation du temps, basé sur une complétion de graphe, mais qu’il convient de formaliser de manière à ce que la nouvelle variable de temps soit la même pour tous les scénarios considérés. Ce procédé permettra de transformer notre dynamique initiale non standard en une dynamique plus adaptée à l’application des conditions nécessaires d’optimalité, et nous projetons ensuite de rechercher et de caractériser des solutions optimales “moyennes” efficaces face aux incertitudes liées aux précipitations. Part I [SOURCE: Page_61]

Contributions to optimal control theory and analysis of the optimal irrigation policies in a greenhouse context [SOURCE: Page_61]

61 [SOURCE: Page_61]

Chapter 2 [SOURCE: Page_63]

Preliminary results in optimal con- trol theory [SOURCE: Page_63]

This chapter is devoted to the introduction of theoretical results in optimal control theory, in particular about the well-known “bang-bang principle” for minimum-time problems ruled by linear dynamics. We revisit this principle in two different frameworks (piecewise affine dynamics under state constraints, and affine dynamics with a stochastic drift term) and we present their respective result formulated as extended bang-bang properties. Such properties provide practical consequences in terms of the nature of the trajectories that reach the target in minimal time. In particular, a numerical resolution will thus be reduced to the class of controls corresponding to the structure induced by its contextual bang-bang principle extension. [SOURCE: Page_63]

2.1     Extension of the bang-bang principle for piecewise affine dynamics under state constraints This section corresponds to Article [29], which has been submitted to the journal Automatica (in November 2024), and is currently under review. It is an extension of the conference article [27] presented in the IEEE 63rd Conference on Decision and Control, held in December 2024. We investigate the bang-bang principle in a context of non-linear dynamics, more precisely piecewise affine dynamics subject to state constraints. In this framework, even inside an individual region where the dynamics is affine, it is not possible to directly apply the classical bang-bang principle since it cannot guarantee that the trajectory generated by the obtained bang-bang control. A more meticulous study of the local behaviour of the response of trajectories with respect to bang controls is thus needed. We still show that a bang-bang property holds in the sense that there exists an optimal solution composed of extreme controls when the trajectory evolves inside a region of affine dynamics, and might present singular arcs necessarily located at the boundaries, i.e. the non-differentiable parts of the dynamics. We exhibit examples where singular arcs are indeed optimal. [SOURCE: Page_63]

2.1.1    Introduction The bang-bang principle is a pillar of the theory of linear controlled systems (see [97, 167, 74, 98]). Consider a linear dynamical system [SOURCE: Page_63]

Ẋ = A(t)X + B(t)u,      X ∈ Rn ,    u ∈ U ⊂ Rr ,                        (2.1) [SOURCE: Page_63]

where A(·), B(·) are time-measurable matrices of adequate dimensions, and U is a convex compact set. Denote Ext(U ) the set of extreme points of the set U , i.e. points that cannot be written as a convex combination of two distincts points of U (see [98]). For any initial condition (t^0 , X$_0$ ) in R+ × Rn , the attainability set A(t), resp. ABB (t), is defined as the set of X(t) where X(·) is the solution of Equation (2.1) for the initial condition (t^0 , X$_0$ ) and any time-measurable controls u(·) that takes values in U , resp. in Ext(U ). The bang-bang principle states that A(t) and ABB (t) coincide. This result has consequences in terms of controllability and the nature of trajectories that [SOURCE: Page_63]

63 [SOURCE: Page_63]

## 64                 CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARY RESULTS IN OPTIMAL CONTROL THEORY

join two points of the state space in minimal time. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Moreover, it is customary to consider optimal control problems under constraints. These prob- lems usually contain challenging difficulties – especially when it comes to applied areas – and the literature is wide and various, depending on the formulation of the different kinds of constraints and the forms of necessary or sufficient optimality conditions. A certain amount of cases is listed in detail in [78]. [SOURCE: Page_64]

We shall consider in this paper non-autonomous regional affine controlled systems, defined by a family of controlled affine systems Ẋ = fi (t, X) := Ai (t)X + B(t)u + Ci (t),   i ∈ I,                     (2.2) over a partition of Ω ⊂ R+ × Rn (where Ω may be corresponding to the constraint time-state space), and whose dynamics is given by one of these linear systems on each subset of the parti- tion. Such a dynamics is in general discontinuous and the question of well-posedness of solutions raises (see for example [81], [109], [5]). In particular sliding modes [181] or Filippov solutions [58] may be considered. Controllability of such systems has been well investigated in the literature (see [10, 24, 89, 141, 186]). In the present work, we shall assume that the dynamics are continuous with respect to X (but not necessarily differentiable), which does not bring any issue to define unique so- lutions as for any Lipschitz continuous differential equations. There is surprisingly much less works dedicated to this precise setting excepted for instance [175] that investigates the controllability of such systems. [SOURCE: Page_64]

For practical interest one may wonder if the bang-bang principle still holds in this simple setting, and if not where the control has to be a non extreme point. The underlying question is to know if it is worth anticipating the change of region with non bang-bang solution in the interior of the regions. To the best of our knowledge, this question has not been addressed specifically in the liter- ature, although the problem of regional optimal control has got recent interests in the literature (see [6, 7, 8]). For instance in [8], a minimal time problem with piecewise affine dynamics is presented, but whose dynamics is discontinuous. Note that considering a family of linear systems with the continuity assumption appears to be very restrictive (as it would impose the common boundary of two domains Pi , Pj to belong to the kernel of Ai − Aj ). However in many applications one deals with saturation induced by min or max operators. This is why we consider families of affine rather than linear systems. Let us underline that applying directly the bang-bang principle for trajectories joining two points in a same subset Pi does not guarantee that the trajectory with extreme controls remains in the same set Pi . This is precisely the point investigated in the present work that leads to extend the classical bang-bang principle. We will then explore through examples the possible existence of singular arcs, also noticing that a constrained arc in this framework will be seen as a singular arc. [SOURCE: Page_64]

The following is organized as follows. In Section 2.1.2, we make precise our notations and assumptions, making distinctions between linear and affine bang-bang principle for the minimal time control problem. In Section 2.1.3, we give our main result. Section 2.1.4 is devoted to examples. [SOURCE: Page_64]

2.1.2     Settings and assumptions In this section we present the framework of spatially-temporally regional affine systems, and highlight their similarities and differences with classical linear systems. [SOURCE: Page_64]

We consider an non-autonomous system that is linear with respect to u: Ẋ = F (t, X) + B(t)u,                                     (2.3) where X ∈ Rn and u ∈ U, with U the set of measurable functions taking values in U ⊂ Rr . Consider the following assumptions. Assumption 2.1.1. Given the set Ω ⊂ R+ × Rn such that Ω̊ is non-empty, connected, projt Ω = R+ , and there exists a partition                 [ Ω=     Pi ,                                        (2.4) i∈I [SOURCE: Page_64]

2.1. EXTENSION OF BANG-BANG PRINCIPLE PIECEWISE AFFINE CONTINUOUS                                    65 [SOURCE: Page_65]

where the P̊i are pairwise disjoint, non-empty and connected subsets for any i ∈ I (where I is countable), such that F (t, X) = Ai (t)X + Ci (t), (t, X) ∈ Pi ,                  (2.5) where Ai and Ci are n × n− and n × 1− time measurable matrix-valued functions, B is a n × r− time measurable matrix-valued function. Moreover F is continuous with respect to X for any t. Let us stress that we do not impose any regularity of the boundaries of the sets Pi . Assumption 2.1.2. The set U is convex compact. We consider (P) the minimum time problem to reach a compact target set Γ ⊂ Rn among con- trols in U, from an initial state (t^0 , X(t^0 )) ∈ Ω that belongs to the attainability set of Γ, while fulfilling the constraint (t, X(t)) ∈ Ω for any time t. [SOURCE: Page_65]

In the case where there is only one area (ie I = {α}), the dynamics is linear (ie Cα = 0), and there is no constraints (i.e. Ω = R+ × Rn ), we have in fact a classical linear system [SOURCE: Page_65]

Ẋ(t) = Aα (t)X(t) + B(t)u(t).                                 (2.6) [SOURCE: Page_65]

For this problem, the classical bang-bang principle is often stated for linear dynamics, as follows. Theorem 2.1.1 (Classical bang-bang principle [79]). If X$_0$ belongs to the attainability set of Γ, then there exists a control function u(·) that takes values in E(U ) for which the associated trajectory of Equation (2.6) reaches Γ in minimal time from the initial condition X(t^0 ) = X$_0$ . We recall that this result can be stated as above for a linear system, or similarly for an affine system as in [98]. In fact these two statements are equivalent, as detailed in the following Lemma. Lemma 2.1.2. Consider the affine system in Rn [SOURCE: Page_65]

Ẏ (t) = L(t)Y (t) + M (t)u(t) + N (t),                           (2.7) [SOURCE: Page_65]

where u(t) ∈ U , a convex compact set of Rp , and L, M and N are n × n−, n × r− and n × 1− time mesurable matrix-valued functions. Then, the conclusion of Theorem Theorem 2.1.1 holds for dynamics Equation (2.7). Proof. The proof only consists in rewriting the affine system into an equivalent linear one in higher dimension, and we explicitly construct it as follows: [SOURCE: Page_65]

Ỹ˙ (t) = L̃(t)Ỹ (t) + M̃ (t)u(t),                           (2.8) [SOURCE: Page_65]

where L̃ and M̃ are (n + 1) × (n + 1)− and (n + 1) × r− matrix-valued functions. Let us set the additional state variable ẏn+1 (t) = 0 , yn+1 (0) = 1. Then we define Ỹ ∈ Rn+1 such that the j-th coefficient of Ỹ is Ỹj = Yj for all 1 ≤ j ≤ n, and Ỹn+1 = yn+1 . We posit L̃ ∈ Mn+1,n+1 (R) such that the coefficients (k, j) are L̃k,j = Lk,j for all 1 ≤ k, j ≤ n, the coefficients (k, n + 1) are L̃k,n+1 = Nk for all 1 ≤ k ≤ n, and the coefficients (n + 1, j) are L̃n+1,j = 0 for all 1 ≤ j ≤ n + 1. We similarly define M̃ ∈ Mn+1,r (R) such that the coefficients (k, j) are M̃k,j = Mk,j for all 1 ≤ k ≤ n and 1 ≤ j ≤ r, and the coefficients (n + 1, j) are M̃n+1,j = 0 for all 1 ≤ j ≤ r. With such a construction, we easily check that the system Equation (2.8) describes the same dynamics as the system Equation (2.7). We may now introduce the main issue of this work. Considering a time-optimal problem subject to the dynamics Equation (2.3), we will discuss about an extension of the bang-bang principle for such settings. It is well known that for hybrid dynamical systems or switched systems the optimal time trajectory may lie on the locus of discontinuity of the dynamics, and it is called a sliding mode. In this work, the systems we consider might be seen as spatially hybrid systems (with a non-autonomous regionalization), but with an additional condition of continuity at the boundaries. One may wonder if such sliding mode behaviour is still possible with continuous piecewise affine dynamics. A straightforward application of the classical bang-bang principle would fail in the framework of Assumption 2.1.1, since we can not ensure that the obtained bang-bang trajectory [SOURCE: Page_65]

## 66                  CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARY RESULTS IN OPTIMAL CONTROL THEORY

doesn’t cross any boundary ∂Pi (especially since these boundaries might be moving), which would thus modify the dynamics driving the system. Therefore we have to be a bit more cautious and examine the evolution of the system in order to locally apply the classical bang-bang principle, and then build a candidate bang-bang control step by step. Remark 2.1.3. We underline the fact that this work does not focus on the question of the control- lability of our systems, and we will always assume that our problems are feasible, i.e. the target is attainable in finite time. [SOURCE: Page_66]

2.1.3     Extended bang-bang principle Let us now establish the statement and the proof of the main issue of this work. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Let u∗ (·) ∈ U be an optimal solution of the time-optimal problem (P) driven by the dynamics Equation (2.3). Denote X ∗ (·) the associated optimal trajectory and T ∗ ∈ R∗+ the corresponding optimal time. Assume that there exists a time interval [a, b] ⊂ [0, T ∗ ] such that (b, X ∗ (b)) ∈ ∂Pi where Pi is a fixed region of the partition, and (t, X ∗ (t)) ∈ P̊i for all t ∈ [a, b). [SOURCE: Page_66]

First we are going to restrict our analysis on a segment strictly inside P̊i . For any [td , tf ] ⊂ [a, b), we study the evolution of the system over [td , tf ], thus following the dynamics of the region Pi : [SOURCE: Page_66]

Ẋ(t) = Ai (t)X(t) + B(t)u(t) + Ci (t).                               (2.9) [SOURCE: Page_66]

We aim to apply the classical bang-bang principle for affine systems, but we need to ensure that all the trajectories undergo the same dynamics. 2.1.4     Examples In this section we are going to present two examples of problems fulfilling the settings described in this work, and we illustrate the result of Theorem 6 by presenting complete optimal trajectories composed of both bang-bang and singular arcs. The first one is a simple autonomous system driving an optimal time problem where the geometry of the (constant) partition of the space gives the intuition of the existence of a singular arc. The second one is from a crop irrigation model [19], and is written as a non-autonomous system driving an optimization problem with a fixed terminal time, an integral cost, and constraints over the input and the state. R t Let us assume that there exists an optimal solution of the problem (Irrig). Define B(t) = 0 φ(τ )KS (S(τ ))dτ . In this case, there is a corresponding optimal terminal cost B ∗ > 0 and a final state S(T ) = S.  e We then consider that it gives in fact an additional terminal condition corresponding to the additional state B(·), i.e. B(T ) = B ∗ . Now we may replace all the terminal conditions by a target [SOURCE: Page_72]

(Sf , Vf , Bf ) = (S, e Vinit , B ∗ ). [SOURCE: Page_72]

With all these transformations, we now have a problem written as a non-autonomous regional affine system with continuous dynamics with respect to the state, and the previous optimization is thus by definition equivalent to a minimum time problem to reach the target (Sf , Vf , Bf ). Indeed, if there exists t̂ < T such that B(t̂) = B ∗ and V (t̂) = Vinit , then the control u = 0 over [t̂, T ] would still get V (T ) = Vinit , and the dynamics of B would imply B(T ) > B(t̂), which would contradict the optimality of B ∗ in the initial formulation of the problem. [SOURCE: Page_72]

The extended bang-bang principle (Theorem 2.1.5) thus states that there exists an optimal solution consisting in bang arcs over time intervals when the trajectory belongs to a differentiable area, and possible singular arcs on boundaries. We illustrate this result on our irrigation system with a numerical resolution computed via Bocop (see [173]), using φ(t) = (t/T )α and the parametrization in Tables 2.1 and 2.2. We highlight in Figure 2.4 the partition of the time-state set (only showing the state S, the partition being constant with respect to the other states), by Ω = P$_1$ ∪ P$_2$ ∪ P$_3$ ∪ P$_4$ . The definition of the problem (Irrig) implies that the singular arcs occur only at discrete values of S, then the corresponding singular controls are easy to compute using the equation of Ṡ. 2.1.5    Conclusions We focused our study here on extending the classical bang-bang principle for minimum time problems with non-autonomous regional affine continuous dynamics and under constraints. As we showed, an optimal solution to reach a target can always be written as a succession of bang arcs inside the areas of the partition of the time-state set, and eventual singular arcs where the dynamics is non-differentiable, as illustrated by our examples. [SOURCE: Page_73]

We expect that the result of optimal bang arcs in the interior of the regions holds true in a more general framework where the dynamics is discontinuous, which would require redefining the notion of solution suited to such framework. For future perspectives, we may wonder about the conditions of occurrence of singular arcs, and we can discuss about the possible difficulty to compute the value of such singular controls. Moreover, in order to numerically search in an efficient way optimal solutions of an optimal control problem in our framework, we may consider to adapt the multiple shooting method [15]. [SOURCE: Page_73]

## 74                    CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARY RESULTS IN OPTIMAL CONTROL THEORY

2.2        Extension of the bang-bang principle for controlled affine dynamics with brownian noise This section corresponds to Article [31], which has been accepted in the Journal of Convex Anal- ysis (in September 2025). This work is based on a collaborative thinking with Dan Goreac, an associate professor at Université Laval in Québec. The starting point of the thought process is the fact that there doesn’t exist any bang-bang like property for dynamics involving a stochastic term, even in the linear case. In fact, we highlight the existence of a pathological behaviour induced by controlled noise where the reachable sets (from any control and from only extreme controls) do not coincide, thus preventing a direct bang-bang property. Therefore we investigate systems with non- controlled noise and problems with terminal cost. We first show that in this context the reachable sets (of “any” and “extreme” controls) are convex, strongly closed and weakly compact. Then we show a bang-bang like property in the sense that the optimal costs of the two problems (with “any” and “extreme” controls) have the same optimal value. [SOURCE: Page_74]

2.2.1       Introduction The bang-bang principle is a pillar of the control theory, which has important implications in terms of controllability and optimal control of deterministic linear systems in finite dimension. Given a control system in Rn (for some n ∈ N∗ ) of the form [SOURCE: Page_74]

dX(t) = a(t) + A(t)X(t) + B(t)u(t),       t ≥ 0,                       (2.20) dt where the control u(t) takes values in a compact set U ⊂ Rd (for some d ∈ N∗ ), and a(·), A(·), B(·) are time-dependent matrices of adequate dimension, the (linear) bang-bang principle (see e.g. [99]) states that for any initial condition X(t^0 ) = X$_0$ , the reachable set at a given time T > t^0 coincides with the reachable set with controls that takes values in the closed convex hull of U , denoted co U . Alternatively, the bang-bang principle is often stated in the literature for convex compact sets U , whose sets of extreme points Ext(U ) is closed, as the equality of reachable sets with control in U or in Ext(U ) (we recall that the extreme points u of a convex set U are such that U \ {u} is convex). Typically, when the set U takes the form di=1 [ui , ui ], one deduces that a reachable target can be Q reached in minimal time using controls ui (i = 1, . . . , d) taking values ui or ui only [97]. [SOURCE: Page_74]

The purpose of this work is to investigate the validity of this principle in a stochastic framework, adding a brownian part to the dynamics, considered then as a stochastic differential equation. The paper is organized as follows. In the next Section 2.2.2, we give the stochastic setting we are considering, with some definitions and preliminary comments. Section 2.2.3 is devoted to our main results. [SOURCE: Page_74]

2.2.2       Stochastic setting and preliminaries A natural way for extending dynamics Equation (2.20) to a stochastic framework, keeping the linearity property with respect to the state and the control, is to consider the following stochastic differential equation: [SOURCE: Page_74]

dX(t) = a(t) + A(t)X(t) + B(t)u(t) dt + c(t) + C(t)X(t) + D(t)u(t) dW (t), t ∈ [0, T ], (2.21)                                    [SOURCE: Page_74]

where W denotes the standard one-dimensional brownian motion on a complete standard probability space (Ω, F := (Ft )0≤t≤T , P), for a fixed finite time horizon 0 < T < +∞, and time-dependent matrices a(·), A(·), B(·), c(·), C(·), D(·) are of appropriate dimensions. We are not aiming at complicating the measurability statements by asking specific integrability for the coefficients of these matrices. For this reason, we make the following assumption. Assumption 2.2.1.           1. The control space U is a compact subset of the euclidean space Rd (for some d ∈ N∗ ). 2. The matrices a, c ∈ L∞ ([0, T ]; Rn ), A, C ∈ L∞ ([0, T ]; Rn×n ), and B, D ∈ L∞ ([0, T ]; Rn×d ) are deterministic. [SOURCE: Page_74]

We aim at studying when these problems are equivalent, in the sense that they have the same value. Reachable sets are a natural way of expressing Mayer cost problems. While the bang-bang principle recalled in the introduction for the deterministic setting is a well-established property, it appears to be more difficult to obtain in the stochastic framework (as already suggested by the pathological behavior of Example 2.2.1). [SOURCE: Page_76]

The deterministic setting suggests that, at least for linear, or affine dynamics, optimality for a Mayer cost only requires the convexity of the reachable set (and not of the control family U itself). In the Brownian diffusive case, the standard to obtain strict optimal controls is set by [54], which, even for linear dynamics, amounts to the convexity of U . The alternative, as suggested for instance in [59], [14], [18], is to consider occupation measures and their convex hull in order to guarantee the existence of relaxed optimal policies. [SOURCE: Page_76]

This is why, inspired by the deterministic setting, and with the understanding that convexity is the basis for the spirit of the arguments in [54], we are looking for specialization of bang-bang principles for a class of affine brownian diffusions. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Proof. In the deterministic framework, the bang-bang principle is inferred from the convexity of these reachable sets. The idea of proof in this setting (i.e. C = 0, c = 0) is to use piecewise (time-) constant control policies (since this class is dense in the family of Borel-measurable controls), then approximate elements in co U with convex combinations of elements in U . The reader may look at the proof of [99, Theorem 1A, last part], for instance. The proof goes through without any change for stochastic dynamics, provided that the considered controls are deterministic. Let us make some comments about the bang-bang principle with stochastic controls. Remark 2.2.4. A similar argument than the one used in the proof of Corollary 2.2.3 could be tried for stochastic control policies, albeit the fact that constant in time would imply the usage, not of elements of co U , but Ft -measurable, co U -valued random variables. with ui being an Fti -measurable, co U -valued random variable. Although, ω-wise, ui (ω) is a convex combination of pure controls in U , the dependence on ω does not allow to conclude the same property for the associated trajectory. [SOURCE: Page_79]

2.2. EXTENSION OF THE BANG-BANG PRINCIPLE IN STOCHASTIC SETTINGS                                     81 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Remark 2.2.6. The reader will have noticed that the requirement on g being convex is crucial in the last part of our argument (on the existence of minima). Even when g is Lipschitz-continuous (instead of merely lower semi-continuous), although the functional E[g(·)] is strongly continuous on L$_2$ (Ω, FT , P; Rn ), the reachable subsets are convex, closed subsets, but they are only weakly-compact, and further assumptions guaranteeing the weak (sequential) lower-semicontinuity are required to infer the existence of strict optimal controls. Nonetheless, for the equivalence of the two problems (and, therefore, existence of nearly-optimal controls), convexity plays no role whatsoever. [SOURCE: Page_81]

2.2.4     Conclusion In this work, we have proved that the bang-bang principle in a stochastic framework holds if one considers uncontrolled diffusion terms and deterministic controls. With stochastic controls, such a Principle cannot be established, at least in the form of equality of the reachable sets. However, if one considers Mayer problems under some mild hypotheses (but still with uncontrolled diffusion), then we have shown that the value functions coincide, which is probably more useful for concrete applications. Future investigations beyond this work include the consideration of multi-dimensional Brownian motions and piecewise affine dynamics, as in Section 2.1. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Chapter 3 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Optimal structures of crop irrigation strategies under state constraints [SOURCE: Page_83]

This chapter corresponds to Article [30], which has been accepted in the Journal of Optimization Theory and Applications (in September 2025). It consists in an improved version, with detailed proofs, of the conference article [28] presented in the IEEE 63rd Conference on Decision and Control, held in December 2024. In this work, we investigate the crop irrigation problem in the context of greenhouse agriculture. Therefore we study the corresponding model formulation, and we aim at showing properties of optimal solutions to several optimization problems representing various operating objectives. Non-smoothness features are a priori difficult to study (a numerical point of view requires a regularisation), but our work precisely relies on these non-differentiable points to describe the structure of solutions. In addition to these various problem formulations, we improve previous modeling works by considering a new state constraint representing the risk aversion of the operator, and preventing low soil moisture that would imply irreversible damage to the crop. Since we aim for deriving the optimality necessary conditions through the Pontryagin Maximum Principle, we first develop an equivalent problem formulation thanks to an original technique (that we call “budget compensation”) consisting in penalizing the budget dynamics by a function of constraint violation. This way, we are able to use a standard PMP to derive necessary conditions, which, combined with a structural property coming from the piecewise affine nature of the dynamics, allow us to show that an optimal control belongs to a low-parameterized class of time-varying feedbacks. [SOURCE: Page_83]

3.1     Introduction The development of agriculture plays a significant role in the worldwide management of natural resources. Among all the consequent challenges, the management of water resources is critical espe- cially in areas where the scarce precipitations need to be more and more supported by irrigation. It is possible to save water through different agronomic and technological approaches, such as selecting stress resilient species, upgrading equipment performances, improving irrigation scheduling. Prac- tices based on enhancing irrigation planning [155], coupled with a framework of deficit irrigation [66, 138] show promising potential to increase efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_83]

Numerous studies tackle the agricultural water management by incorporating modeling tools, such as complex numerical models to describe precisely the physical processes of plant growth [11, 33, 90]. They typically present a detailed structure for predefined crop or regional context [110, 194], and contain significant constraints due to biological requirements. Their complexity im- plies computational limitations, it is thus reasonable to allow a more parsimonious description in order to improve flexibility and analytical capacity. To this end, irrigation planning is seen as a control problem, and using advanced control theory show good potential to improve efficiency in agriculture [48, 145]. Some studies have explored model predictive control [47, 149, 156] target- ing adaptive and robust strategies regarding unknown entries like weather or model uncertainties, through numerical simulations. In a more deterministic approach towards analytical strategies, some studies addressed the irrigation problem using optimal control tools [19, 102, 112, 157]. These [SOURCE: Page_83]

83 [SOURCE: Page_83]

## 84                                  CHAPTER 3. OPTIMAL STRUCTURES IN GREENHOUSES

works explore models based on simple dynamical systems and decision making through only a few variables, and require a limited number of hypotheses on the physical processes. According to their various assumptions and objectives some models consider more or less detailed soil water balance and more or less constrained contexts. [SOURCE: Page_84]

The desired benefit of using analytical models is to aim for strategies depicting optimal irri- gation thanks to time-varying feedback controls suitable for the low number of model inputs and state variables that are related to quantities accessible in real world. Several major agronomic ob- jectives match with a formulation of a constrained optimization problem : maximizing the biomass production with a water availability constraint, minimizing the consumption of water to reach a yield target, maximizing a financial cost related to production sales and water expenses, avoiding harmful stress due to critically low level of soil moisture. In this work, we investigate these prob- lems with a unified methodological point of view, and we employ a model that we call Controlled Crop Irrigation (CCI) model, inspired from [19, 91, 133]. This model is prone to analytical optimal control techniques (even though it presents non-smooth features) and is thought to possess enough physical sense to describe the order-1 biophysical processes, especially for greenhouse application. The various objectives discussed previously lead to the formulation of three optimization problems (according to three different couples of criterion and target, all fulfilling a state constraint) that we study in a unified procedure. In order to look for a common structure of the optimal irrigation strategies in terms of feedback, we shall establish and derive the optimality necessary conditions. There exists various ways to deal with state constraints ([78, 152]), but in our study we introduce an original “compensation technique”, and we work out an equivalent problem formulation without state constraint on which we can apply a standard unconstrained Pontryagin Maximum Principle [35]. [SOURCE: Page_84]

In Section 3.2, we provide a description of the CCI model and its hypotheses, then we present the different criteria and targets that we will study and we formalize their associated optimal control problems. After explaining the compensation technique, that we use, leading to equivalent problem formulations freed from state constraints, we show in Section 3.3 some necessary properties of optimal solutions. Then we apply in Section 3.4 the non-smooth Pontryagin Maximum Principle and we derive the optimality necessary conditions to finally get optimal structures of irrigation strategies. [SOURCE: Page_84]

3.2     Model description and hypotheses 3.2.1    Basis model description We consider the Controlled Crop Irrigation model developed by [91], inspired from [133] and simplified for control theory purposes. A first analysis of these dynamics was made in [19] and we extend the study with targets, criteria and constraints as discussed below. The CCI model relies on a soil moisture balance incorporating plant transpiration, soil evaporation and water irrigation input. It also tracks biomass production and water consumed through irrigation, as follows:                                                                     Ṡ(t) = k 1   − φ(t)K S (S(t)) − (1 − φ(t))K R (S(t)) + k 2 u(t)  , Ḃ(t) = φ(t)KS (S(t)),                                                    (3.1)  V̇ (t) = u(t).  [SOURCE: Page_84]

The system is supposed to progress over a fixed time period [0, T ] corresponding to the crop season from sowing to harvest. The soil moisture is represented by S ∈ [0, 1], B is the production of biomass and V denotes the water consumption. Irrigation is represented by the control u(·) which is a measurable function defined on [0, T ] and taking values in [0, 1]. The parameters k1 and k2 are constants depending on agronomic and operational characteristics. The value k1 is the ratio of the daily reference evapotranspiration over the volume of water reservoir, and the value k2 is the ratio of the maximal irrigation rate over the daily reference evapotranspiration. Assumption 3.2.1. The constants k1 and k2 satisfy k1 > 0 ,   k2 > 1. [SOURCE: Page_84]

3.2. MODEL DESCRIPTION AND HYPOTHESES                                                                   85 [SOURCE: Page_85]

The function φ models the radiation interception efficiency, related to the growth of plant cover. Assumption 3.2.2. Assume that φ : [0, T ] → [0, 1] is increasing and of class C 1 . The functions KS and KR respectively describe the variations of soil water content through plant transpiration and soil evaporation. Assumption 3.2.3. The functions KS and KR are defined for S ∈ [0, 1], and are non-decreasing, piecewise affine, continuous. Typically, KS and KR are found in the literature ([19, 133]) as [SOURCE: Page_85]

0        , S ∈ [0, Sw ),   KS (S) = S ∗ −Sw , S ∈ [Sw , S ∗ ), S−Sw (3.2a) 1       , S ∈ [S , 1]; ∗   ( 0      , S ∈ [0, Sh ), KR (S) = S−Sh                                                 (3.2b) 1−Sh  , S ∈ [Sh , 1], where 0 < Sh < Sw < S ∗ < 1 represent respectively soil water levels depending on soil and plant characteristics (residual water point, wilting point, point of incipient water stress). Remark 3.2.1. It is possible to consider more generic shapes than Equations (3.2a) and (3.2b) (with more non-differentiable points for example), as long as they remain non-decreasing, piecewise affine, continuous, and verify KS concave over [Sw , 1] and KR convex over [0, 1]. Notice that KS and KR are Lipschitz, and denote LS > 0 and LR > 0 their respective Lipschitz constant. Assumption 3.2.4. The system has the following initial conditions: S(0) = S$_0$ ∈ (S ∗ , 1] ,        B(0) = 0 ,   V (0) = 0. Remark 3.2.2. It is reasonable to consider a quite high initial soil moisture, since the crop season begins after the year period with usually wet weather. Remark 3.2.3. The condition k^2 > 1 ensures local controllability of the S variable, in the sense that it is possible to select controls in order to chose the sign of Ṡ over [0, T ]. 3.2.2     Framework and constraints According to the context of deficit irrigation, we restrict our study to (optimal) trajectories inducing water stress. In addition to the context of deficit irrigation, we consider a state constraint on the soil moisture, representing the biological need to prevent critically low levels of soil water content. More precisely, the system is subject to the following state constraint: [SOURCE: Page_86]

As a consequence, we set our study gathering deficit irrigation and biological constraint : each biomass target Y considered will satisfy Bope < Y < B ∗ , and each water consumption target Q considered must fulfill Vope < Q < V ∗ . [SOURCE: Page_86]

3.2.3      Problems formulation The problem of optimizing crop irrigation might be seen in various ways according to classical agronomic objectives. We introduce three optimization problems corresponding to three natural points of view about the operational targets and the criteria to be optimized. [SOURCE: Page_86]

• (P 1) : maximizing the biomass production at the end of the season, when using a limited quota of water, i.e. maximizing B(T ) under the terminal condition V (T ) ≤ Q where Q is the initial available water. [SOURCE: Page_86]

• (P 2) : minimizing the water consumption to reach a certain target of biomass, i.e. minimizing V (T ) under the terminal condition B(T ) ≥ Y where Y is the objective of biomass production at the end of the season. [SOURCE: Page_86]

• (P 3) : maximizing a financial balance between biomass sales and water consumption expenses, i.e. maximizing the value g(B(T )) − c(V (T )) where g and c are functions representing the earnings and spendings, with g(0) = c(0) = 0, g ′ > 0 and c′ > 0. [SOURCE: Page_86]

We introduce the associated sets of admissible controls for each problem. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Definition 3.2.4. For any control u(·), consider its associated solution to Equation (3.1) denoted by (S(·), B(·), V (·)), and define n                                                                               o U P 1 = u ∈ L$_1$ [0, T ], [0, 1] ; S(·) verifies Equation (3.6), V (T ) ≤ Q, Vope < Q < V ∗ ,  n                                                                               o U P 2 = u ∈ L$_1$ [0, T ], [0, 1] ; S(·) verifies Equation (3.6), B(T ) ≥ Y, Bope < Y < B ∗ ,  n                                                   o U P 3 = u ∈ L$_1$ [0, T ], [0, 1] ; S(·) verifies Equation (3.6) .  [SOURCE: Page_86]

Consequently, the three considered problems are formally written as the following optimal control problems. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Remark 3.2.4. From the theory of optimal control (see [98, 190] for instance), our problems satisfy the usual assumptions that guarantee that the above supremum and infimum are reached. As mentioned in the introduction, these optimal control problems present several levels of diffi- culty. Indeed the model dynamics are non-autonomous and non-smooth, trajectories are subject to running state constraints and admissible controls are constrained to additional terminal conditions. [SOURCE: Page_87]

3.2.4     Preliminaries about trajectories of soil moisture 3.2.4.1    First lemmas Lemma 3.2.5. Consider a control u ∈ U P j and denote S(·) its associated solution of Equation (3.1). Then ∀t ∈ [0, T ], S(t) > Sh . Proof. Denote S 0 (·) the solution of the system Equation (3.1) using the constant control u^0 = 0. Since u ≥ 0, we have Ṡ(t) ≥ Ṡ 0 (t) for all t ∈ [t^0 , T ], and then S(t) ≥ S 0 (t) for all t ∈ [t^0 , T ]. [SOURCE: Page_87]

Moreover the trajectory Sc (·) such that Sc (t) = Sh for all t ∈ [0, T ] is a solution of the differential equation of the state variable S using the constant control u^0 . By Cauchy-Lipschitz Theorem, we know that two trajectories of the same equation with different initial conditions don’t coincide at any time. Thus for all t ∈ [0, T ] one has S 0 (t) > Sc (t). As a consequence, one gets S(t) > Sh for all t ∈ [0, T ]. Lemma 3.2.6. Let S$_1$ (·) and S$_2$ (·) be two solutions of the system Equation (3.1) and 0 ≤ t^1 < t^2 ≤ T such that S$_1$ (t^1 ) = S$_2$ (t^1 ) and S$_1$ (t) ≥ S$_2$ (t) for all t ∈ [t^1 , t^2 ]. Let us define the three new problems corresponding to this formulation, recalling that this formulation now allows a wider range of trajectories and bigger sets of admissible controls. Remark 3.3.1. We evidently have U P 1 ⊂ U Pf$_1$ , U P 2 ⊂ U Pf$_2$ and U P 3 ⊂ U Pf$_3$ , thus any admissible control of a problem (P j) is admissible for (P fj). [SOURCE: Page_90]

The additional term Cf (S(t)) plays the role of a cost penalization for the problems (Pf$_2$) and (Pf$_3$). However for the problem (Pf$_1$) it may be seen as some room of maneuver for the input u(·) which is restricted by the terminal target. The proposed technique consists in determining a well- suited (large enough) constant C > 0 such that a variation of a trajectory of S travelling below Sope over some time interval I, induces a variation of the quantity Cf (S(t)) on the interval I that matches with the variation of corresponding input u(t) over I. This way, we will be able to claim that the optimization of the new problems (Pf$_1$), (Pf$_2$) and (Pf$_3$) is equivalent to the optimization of the initial ones (P 1), (P 2) and (P 3). That is the purpose of the following proposition. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Remark 3.3.6. Consider an optimal solution uPf$_3$ to the problem (Pf$_3$), with its associated trajec- tory (SPf$_3$ (·), BPf$_3$ (·), WPf$_3$ (·)) and its associated optimal cost JPf$_3$ = g(BPf$_3$ (T )) − c(WPf$_3$ (T )). From properties above and Lemma 3.2.6 we deduce that WPf$_3$ (T ) ∈ [Vope , V ∗ ]. [SOURCE: Page_96]

If WPf$_3$ (T ) = Vope then uPf$_3$ = uope , and if WPf$_3$ (T ) = V ∗ then uPf$_3$ = u∗ . They correspond to trivial trajectories and these situations will then not be considered in the rest of our analysis. [SOURCE: Page_96]

If WPf$_3$ (T ) ∈ (Vope , V ∗ ), we can set Q = WPf$_3$ (T ) ∈ (Vope , V ∗ ) and solve the problem (Pf$_1$) associated to the quota Q. The optimal solution uPf$_1$ and its corresponding trajectory (SPf$_1$ (·), BPf$_1$ (·), WPf$_1$ (·)) satisfy WPf$_1$ (T ) = Q and BPf$_1$ (T ) ≥ BPf$_3$ (T ). To conclude, we have JPf$_3$ = g(BPf$_1$ (T )) − c(WPf$_1$ (T )), i.e. there exists an optimal control of the problem (Pf$_3$) which is an optimal control of the problem (Pf$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_96]

We write the optimality necessary conditions, and we show further properties about the signs of adjoint states. [SOURCE: Page_96]

3.4.2     Structures of optimal solutions In this section, we aim at characterizing the structures of optimal solutions from the necessary optimality conditions. To this end, we first discuss about the possible existence of singular arcs and the loci where they can exist, and then we investigate the switching function in order to extract information about the number of switches. [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.4.2.1    Singular arcs [SOURCE: Page_99]

Proposition 3.4.6. For any optimal solution to the problem (P fj), a singular arc can only occur at SP fj = Sope or SPfj = S ∗ . [SOURCE: Page_99]

Proof. The problems (Pf1) and (Pf2) are written with piecewise affine and continuous dynamics with respect to the state variables, and their cost can be rewritten as an integral cost with a piecewise affine continuous integrand function. Moreover, following Remark 3.3.6, either the problem (Pf3) is trivial (and presents singular arcs at Sope or S ∗ ), or it can be solved with an optimal control of the problem (Pf1). [SOURCE: Page_99]

## 100                                       CHAPTER 3. OPTIMAL STRUCTURES IN GREENHOUSES

As a consequence, the optimal control problems (Pfj) fulfill the framework of [27], and we deduce that there exists an optimal solution of (Pfj) composed of a succession of bang-bang arcs over time interval when the trajectory belongs to a region where the dynamics Equation (3.11) is differen- tiable, and possible singular arcs located where the dynamics Equation (3.11) is non-differentiable, or when a state constraint is active. [SOURCE: Page_100]

By definition of the functions KS and KR , and using optimality properties of Section 3.3, we deduce that a singular arc can only occur at the non-differentiable points Sope and S ∗ . [SOURCE: Page_100]

Remark 3.4.7. We see that the loci of singular arcs are the non-differentiable points of the dy- namics, therefore the use of more generic shapes for the functions KS and KR as discussed in Remark 3.2.1 is compatible with our methodological process. In particular, more non-differentiable points in KS and KR would simply generate a higher number of possible values of singular arcs. [SOURCE: Page_100]

3.5     Conclusion We dealt with a family of optimal control problems, and we showed, using dedicated tools (in particular an application of the extended bang-bang principle), that optimal irrigation strategies for these problems share the same structure of optimal feedback. These strategies can be interpreted as threshold-based strategies, where the values of thresholds correspond to non-differentiable points of the dynamics. This result allows to compute numerically the optimal solutions as a simple low dimensional optimization of the parameters of the structured optimal policies, hence enabling large numerical model exploration. Such threshold-based irrigation strategies can also be tested on more details models (for which the analytic study of the optimal feedback strategies would not be possible), and are suited to closed-loop experimental implementation, typically using soil moisture sensors. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Chapter 4 [SOURCE: Page_105]

Exploring the optimality of Threshold- Based crop Irrigation feedback Strategies [SOURCE: Page_105]

This chapter corresponds to Article [26], which in preparation for an upcoming submission. This work is based on the properties of the Controlled Crop Irrigation (CCI) model, for which it has already been proven (see Chapter 3) that optimal policies are Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies. We aim at studying both the optimal TBIS (the number of thresholds, their values and the most influential parameters which dictate the shape of optimal irrigation scenarios), and their sub-optimal declensions (the effects of errors made on their determination). Much attention is drawn to the crucial ”decision time” at which the operator decides to increase soil water content from the low soil moisture threshold (corresponding to the risk aversion level) to the high soil moisture threshold (corresponding to the lowest level of maximal biomass production rate). To these ends, we couple known results from optimal control theory and numerical methods for sensitivity analysis. We show that the optimal decision time is mostly driven by parameters related to plant growth dynamics and the evaluation of stress effects on biomass production, and that suboptimal decisions induce a trade- off between biomass production, irrigation water use and plant water stress. We provide visuals and we explain their qualitative interpretation for operating purposes of efficient water management. [SOURCE: Page_105]

4.1     Introduction Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies (TBIS) produce irrigation scenarios, i.e. chronicles of ir- rigation events, from the application of decision rules in which irrigation is triggered when selected values of one or several variables of the soil-plant-atmosphere system are reached, or assumed to be reached, via observations, measurements or modeling. This leaves the possibility of considering different irrigation triggering criteria, associated with different irrigation amounts, across the dif- ferent stages of the cropping season. However, the settings, setpoints and objectives of irrigation are most often predefined thus neither reconsidered nor adjusted during the course of the season. Such ambitions are rather left for ex-post analyses or ex-ante planning purposes, facing the issue of relevant if not optimal resource management. The classical problem is to reduce water consump- tion under a certain limit (quota) while still ensuring enough biomass production (objective) and economic viability, for various operational contexts and environmental constraints. [SOURCE: Page_105]

TBIS are common practice in irrigation management, either (i) implicitly, in an informal way, throughout expert irrigation decisions [38, 93, 105] aiming to counterbalance the observed detri- mental effects of unobserved resource depletion, or (ii) explicitly via indications on soil water status drawn from soil water content or matric potential measurements [53, 202, 203], whereas (iii) mod- eling tools and their decision support declensions [17, 60, 40, 65, 92, 106, 162] remain surprisingly rarely used in practice. And yet, apart from costly and risky trial-and-error attempts, this is the only approach that allows generating, comparing and improving scenarios of water consumption, [SOURCE: Page_105]

105 [SOURCE: Page_105]

## 106                                           CHAPTER 4. EXPLORING OPTIMALITY OF TBIS

biomass production and economic yield, as controlled by the tested decision rules, thresholds and associated parameter sets. [SOURCE: Page_106]

TBIS require choosing threshold values endowed with physical sense from the points of view of soil physics, crop physiology and root water uptake properties and performance, while also playing on the explored ranges of values for the sake of optimization purposes. Chosen threshold values are expected to be related to characteristic values of soil water content. These values are θsat the saturation value, when all soil pores are filled with water, θf c the field capacity value, above which drainage occurs as water contents between θsat and θf c are non-equilibrium conditions, θx a quite lower value, whose definition and evaluation are uncertain, under which water uptake by plant roots becomes difficult and incomplete, hampering crop transpiration process as the result of incipient water stress, θwp a far lower value, known as the permanent wilting point, at which water uptake by the roots becomes impossible, and θr the residual water content, not even accessible to evaporation [3]. The objective assigned to optimization is thus to maximize biomass (in this paper) or benefit (in general) for known constraints on system performances and total water consumption afforded, while staying within realistic admissible limits in terms of soil water content. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Ancestral habits, field-based knowledge and common sense lead to an intuitive but fuzzy com- promise between favoring crop production by avoiding too low water contents, staying above θx (if possible), and sparing water by staying under θf c but only in situations in which water costs are sig- nificant, water availability is really limited and irrigation amounts may be properly adjusted. More often than not, the irrigators’ behaviour is quite different between the initial part of the cropping season and the quick growth, most productive part of it. A current practice, in the frame of deficit irrigation, is indeed to delay the first irrigation and provide limited water amounts in the first series of irrigations, both assuming limited early crop demands and aiming to favor root deepening, thus tolerating lower water contents and lower irrigation triggering thresholds than in the productive stage [25, 94, 144]. In the latter, the rate of biomass production is deemed strongly correlated to the absence of any significant water stress, while the cumulative biomass produced will benefit from low cumulative water stresses [32, 66, 107]. On the other hand, even for models having proven their ability to reproduce crop development and production in various mild stress conditions, literature shows a lack of frameworks, methodologies or formal approaches for exploring the merits and draw- backs of different TBIS [172]. [SOURCE: Page_106]

As regards mathematical formalisms and methods to be employed, irrigation planning can be seen as a “control problem”, which consists in “finding a control” (an irrigation chronicle following decision rules) for a dynamical system (coupled model equations describing soil filling and biomass production in dynamic interaction) over a period of time (the full cropping season) to handle the evolution of the system according to a certain objective. Taking advantage of advanced control the- ory in agriculture presents a high potential to improve operating efficiency [48, 145]. Some studies investigated the irrigation problem through the model predictive control point of view [47, 149, 156], targeting adaptive and robust strategies regarding unknown entries like weather or model uncer- tainties. In a purely deterministic approach, other studies addressed the problem of optimizing irrigation planning, using optimal control tools where a certain objective function is optimized (e.g. final biomass production). To this end, several analytical formalisms have been used to investigate the optimal irrigation problem with various points of view [91, 112, 154, 157]. The choice made in this paper is to address the generic, methodological objective by resorting to a parsimonious Controlled Crop Irrigation (CCI) model [19, 28], prone to analytical optimization techniques but thought to possess enough physical sense to describe the order-1 biophysical processes at play. The CCI model is suited to fully controlled conditions, assuming all obtained effects are due to irrigation (greenhouse conditions, associated crops, well-gauged contexts). In addition, the mentioned context (greenhouses) certainly allows feedback loop (process where the output observation can drive the input action), allowing to stick to the predefined plan, within closed-loop modeling. So TBIS typi- cally are control feedback strategies that naturally follow the CCI model approach. [SOURCE: Page_106]

The originality of our work is to bring, thanks to the CCI model framework, a coupled analysis of optimal TBIS strategies gathering results from the optimal control theory and statistical methods of sensitivity analyses. Indeed, the CCI model has the key property to present optimal solution [SOURCE: Page_106]

4.2. MATERIAL AND METHODS                                                                           107 [SOURCE: Page_107]

in terms of TBIS, and is suited to a large numerical exploration. A first objective is to find what characterizes the structure of optimal TBIS, for a wide variety of contexts (soil capacity and initial filling, climatic demand, crop type) and constraints (water quota, irrigation rate). A closely-related objective, of applied interest, is to assess the effects (on water consumption, biomass production and overall irrigation efficiency) of mistakes committed on the various thresholds. In coherence, the present work now explores the optimality or sub-optimality of various sets of irrigation-triggering thresholds. [SOURCE: Page_107]

4.2       Material and methods 4.2.1     Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies A TBIS is composed of time periods when the farmer does not irrigate, time periods when the farmer irrigates at maximum rate, and time periods when the flow of irrigation is adapted to have the soil moisture remaining constant at or near a chosen target (threshold) value, possibly oscillating around it across repeated cycles of water input then consumption, via transpiration and evaporation. To be general, let us renormalize the irrigation rate by its maximal value. That is, if the irrigation rate is called Ψ, then let us consider that no irrigation means setting Ψ = 0, an irrigation rate based on different threshold values of the soil moisture across time will be denoted Ψ    e and is supposed to be valued between 0 and 1, and maximum irrigation rate means setting Ψ = 1. Then a TBIS can be characterized by corresponding “decision rules”, denoted R$_0$ (for no irrigation), RSk (for an irrigation based on a soil moisture threshold value Sk ) and R$_1$ (for maximum irrigation). These rules can depend on time, soil moisture S and a vector of model decision variables D. The set of decision rules is supposed to be complete and mutually compatible, i.e. at any time (or situation) there is exactly one decision rule that holds true. This description of TBIS may be written as a time-dependent feedback instruction, as follows:  0,              if R$_0$ (t, S, D) is true, Ψ(t, S, V ) = Ψ(t, Sk ), if RSk (t, S, D) is true, e                                                       (4.1) 1,          if R$_1$ (t, S, D) is true.   [SOURCE: Page_107]

4.2.2     The Controlled Crop-Irrigation model 4.2.2.1   Model description We denote here as CCI the Controlled Crop Irrigation model introduced in [19, 28, 91] and based on in the initial formulation [133] related to the FAO crop water simulation model Aquacrop [170]. Basically, the CCI model is a parsimonious dynamical system of ODEs suitable for applying the optimal control theory [91]. The CCI model has only three state variables : soil moisture (S), crop biomass (B) and water budget consumption (V ). [SOURCE: Page_107]

As in [133], soil properties are assumed to be spatially homogeneous with an even plant spacing, not needing to consider and spatial pattern or heterogeneity within the modeled area. With char- acteristics seen in such a spatially-averaged way, the CCI model consists in a small set of ordinary differential equations representing soil moisture, biomass production and water budget consumption, during a growing season denoted by a time interval [0, T ]. In the present work, contents of soil water S will be normalized between 0 and 1, rescaling θ = θf c to S = 1 (and keeping θ = 0 to S = 0). Taking S = 1 at θf c means not considering drainage, following the framework of controlled green- house conditions, justifying a deterministic model. The CCI model is written as a continuous-time dynamical system, that can be understood as a system evolving with a certain variation per unit of time (for example per day), and parameters representing rates can thus be seen as quantities per unit of time (for example daily). [SOURCE: Page_107]

The equations describing the variations of S, B and V over time write [SOURCE: Page_107]

[SOURCE: Page_108]

The terms φ(t)KS (S(t)) and (1 − φ(t))KR (S(t)) are respectively for plant transpiration and soil evaporation, where KS and KR account for the decreasing efficiency of the transpiration and evaporation processes for reduced water contents, denoted as levels of S. Hence, the last term in Equation (4.2) somehow denotes the strength of anthropic controls (irrigation decisions) relative to the climatic demand (ET$_0$ ) and soil water loss via transpiration and evaporation (the KS and KR terms). [SOURCE: Page_108]

The crop coefficients KS and KR are piecewise affine and continuous functions of the soil moisture S, and are parameterized by characteristic values of soil water content [3], following the rescaling θ = θf c to S = 1, in ascending order : Srw the residual water content, Spw the permanent wilting point, S ∗ the point of incipient water stress. Their shape and their formulas can be found in Fig- ure 4.1. [SOURCE: Page_108]

As in [28], in this work we aim at maximizing the final biomass production B(T ), among all the admissible irrigation controls u(·). A control u is said to be admissible if it takes values in [0, 1] for all t ∈ [0, T ], and if the corresponding state variables [SOURCE: Page_108]

4.2. MATERIAL AND METHODS                                                                           109 [SOURCE: Page_109]

fulfill the model constraints. To this end, the system is endowed with biological and operating constraints as follows: [SOURCE: Page_109]

• S(t) ≥ Sope for all t ∈ [0, T ] : water stress is tolerated to some extend, but it is assumed by the operator that soil moisture should not go below a value Sope in order to avoid critical stress. In the following, this so-called operating constraint Sope ∈ (Spw , S ∗ ) is assumed chosen by the operator and denotes their risk aversion or inclination: the lower Sope is, the more water is saved but the riskier the strategy is for biomass production. [SOURCE: Page_109]

• V (T ) ≤ Q : the total amount of irrigation water used during the season fulfills a quota constraint (following the initial condition V (0) = 0). The value of water quota Q might depend on the availability and cost of irrigation water, or intentional water savings in a context of deficit irrigation, hence either reflects the irrigator’s choice in examining various scenarios, or an strict, external constraint. [SOURCE: Page_109]

4.2.2.3   Biomass renormalization In the deficit irrigation context there is not enough water to keep soil filling at or above S ∗ throughout the whole cropping season, meaning that the maximum biomass production will not be achieved. The present work deals with various limiting quotas for which the optimum irrigation dynamics, thresholds and setpoints are sought. For any given problem parameterization, the lowest cumulative irrigation amount needed to maintain the above condition is identified as the limit Q∗ quota. In accordance, any quota Q < Q∗ will result in periods of stress, hence in losses in maximum possible final biomass, i.e. B (S$_0$ ,Q) < B Q∗ , where B (S$_0$ ,Q) is called the “contextual optimum” and B Q∗ is called the “overall optimum”. In fact, in absence of any water stress, that overall optimum is only dependent on plant growth characteristics, depicted by the φ function and its specific “shape parameters” (δ, γ) detailed later. Hence, for a given φ function, there is only one value of B Q∗ , common to all possible combinations of all other parameters. Following Equation (4.3), one has RT B Q∗ = k^3 0 φ(t)dt. In this work, for each study case and numerical analysis, we will deal with normalized biomass (thus cancelling the factor k^3 ), typically in the following way: [SOURCE: Page_109]

B(t) β(t) =        .                                      (4.6) B Q∗ [SOURCE: Page_109]

4.2.3     Methodological tools for analysis 4.2.3.1   Optimal control We will make a link between TBIS and results of optimal control, that has already been success- fully applied to the CCI model [28]. As mentioned previously, the optimal control problem, given initial conditions (S$_0$ , 0, Q), is formulated as follows: [SOURCE: Page_109]

B (S$_0$ ,Q) = max B(T ) ,     such that V (T ) ≤ Q,                       (4.7) u(·) [SOURCE: Page_109]

where u is admissible and (S, B, V ) are the associated solutions to Equations (4.2) to (4.4). [SOURCE: Page_109]

The CCI model is written as a non-autonomous dynamical system under constraints, and is piecewise affine and continuous with respect to the state variables. This framework is then suited to apply theoretical control theory results to characterize optimal solutions. In the first place, a theoretical result about piecewise affine systems [27] has been involved in order to extract properties about the candidates structures of optimal solutions. Secondly, a complete analysis based on the derivation of the non-smooth Pontryagin Maximum Principle [35] has been conducted [28] and led to a formulation of optimal irrigation strategies in term of a simple parameterized time-varying feed- back. In the following we present these properties and give their interpretation in terms of modeling. [SOURCE: Page_109]

An optimal irrigation strategy subject to Equation (4.7) is necessarily seen as a TBIS with two threshold values, in which an optimal irrigation chronicle can be written as a time-varying feedback strategy. This optimal irrigation strategy for the CCI model is a particular case of the TBIS general [SOURCE: Page_109]

4.2. MATERIAL AND METHODS                                                                            111 [SOURCE: Page_111]

framework, thus they will be referred in the following as “TBISCCI ”. The strategy will consist in a succession of time intervals with no irrigation, time intervals with maximal flow irrigation, and time intervals where the amount of irrigation matches the water demand from soil evaporation and plant transpiration in order to keep the soil moisture constant, either at a bottom threshold (denoted Sbot ) or at a top threshold (denoted Stop ). [SOURCE: Page_111]

Regarding the number of such time intervals where optimal instruction switches, it has been proven optimal to consider active irrigation (i.e. u ̸= 0) in a single time interval. This implies that if both bottom and top thresholds are reached, then the TBISCCI will induce, in that order : one time interval at the bottom threshold, one time interval at maximal flow of irrigation, and one time interval at the top threshold. Another order of these three intervals cannot be part of an optimal solution, and outside of this concatenated interval there is no irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Moreover the values of the optimal bottom and top thresholds are in fact known. The optimal opti bottom threshold, denoted Sbot      , corresponds to the operating boundary Sope . The optimal top opti threshold, denoted Stop , is the point of incipient water stress S ∗ . [SOURCE: Page_111]

The initial quota of available water Q, recalled to be such that our work belongs to a context of deficit irrigation, has to be entirely consumed to ensure an optimal use of water. More precisely, it is optimal to finish consuming all the water during the season, then switching off irrigation (i.e. u = 0) for the end of the cropping season. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Finally, the optimal strategy belongs to a class of strategies, summarized by the following pa- rameterized control, where time and state variable “soil moisture” (S) are involved, as well as the decision variables “available irrigation water” (V ) and “decision time” (td ), in accordance to the general TBIS framework, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_111]

0           , if V = Q or (S > Sope ) and (t < td ) ,                                                        ũ(t, S ) , if (S = S ) and (t < t ),  ope               ope           d Utd (t, S, V ) =                                                                 (4.8) ∗ )   if  (S =   ∗ ) and (V      and (t      )                                         ũ(t, S     ,          S            < Q)        > td    , 1           , otherwise,  [SOURCE: Page_111]

where S and V are the state variables of our dynamical system, td is a decision parameter, referred to as a “decision time” (which therefore may or may not be optimal) and the quantity ũ represents a time-varying irrigation keeping the soil moisture constant at some threshold value S̃. This irrigation can in fact be expressed analytically by φ(t)KS (S̃) + (1 − φ(t))KR (S̃) ũ(t, S̃) =                                   .                      (4.9) k^2 Notice that with k^2 = Fmax /ET$_0$ > 1 (as mentioned in Section 4.2.2.1), this expression fulfills the condition ũ(t) ∈ [0, 1]. [SOURCE: Page_111]

The “decision time” is in fact not a proper “triggering time” since the irrigation can start before reaching this time td (if the soil moisture reaches Sope before the time td ). This “decision time” can be understood as a tipping point between a period where irrigation was kept at a relatively low rate (and soil moisture was kept at a relatively low value), and a period where we irrigate at full rate until reaching a sufficiently high soil moisture and then maintain that soil moisture value with relatively high irrigation rate. This behaviour intuitively follows the needs of the plant, since crop’s develop- ment is slower at early stages, and the water demand is higher when the crop’s development is faster. [SOURCE: Page_111]

The problem of finding the optimal solution is thus reduced to a one-dimensional direct opti- mization over the class of feedback controls as above, by exploring the range of possible values for the decision time td . Its optimal value is denoted topti d . [SOURCE: Page_111]

4.2.3.2   Sensitivity analyses Since the analytical results do not provide a full characterization of optimal TBISCCI , in particu- lar the optimal decision time topti d , we propose to study its drivers by means of numerical exploration [SOURCE: Page_111]

## 112                                                   CHAPTER 4. EXPLORING OPTIMALITY OF TBIS

using global sensitivity analysis (GSA) techniques [41]. Indeed the use of GSA can lead to relating the variability of topti d    to those of model inputs, and thus to point out in particular which input drives early or late irrigation triggering. Moreover, this global exploration is complemented by a multi-local perturbation analysis that will allow to understand the consequence of sub-optimal choices of soil moisture threshold and decision time. [SOURCE: Page_112]

4.2.3.2.1 Model inputs and outputs In order to perform a global exploration through numer- ical simulation, let us precise the nature of inputs and outputs. First, the duration of crop season T can be seen as a silent variable, since the model is built in a way that it is always possible to rescale the time span to [0, 1] for numerical solving, without altering the structure of the policies (it simply consists in rescaling the time, including in the definition of φ). The values of the operating (Q, Fmax ) constraints are seen as external forcings, related to water availability and rights (Q) or to equipment performance and characteristics (Fmax ), deemed sufficient here as in most real-life cases. By contrast, the assumed “operating constraint” (Sope ) relates altogether to the irrigator’s risk aversion, perception of water stress harmfulness, and willingness to use as few water as possible, resulting in a self-imposed constraint. We will then consider different study cases in the form of different (Sope , Q) couples, in combination with fixed Srw and Spw values, allegedly known from soil and plant characteristics. [SOURCE: Page_112]

The remaining model entries are the exploration parameters listed in Table 4.1. They consist in the initial soil moisture value S$_0$ , the values of ET$_0$ , V ol and S ∗ , the value k^2 corresponding to the quotient between the maximal irrigation rate Fmax and the reference evapotranspiration ET$_0$ (it might be seen as the capacity of the irrigation material), and the fraction of intercepted radiation φ. For this function φ related to the canopy cover, we will follow most crop models and consider a function built from a logistic shape with two parameters, rescaled over [0, 1], as follows: [SOURCE: Page_112]

1 ∀τ ∈ [0, 1], La,b (τ ) =                           , with a > 0, b > 0. 1 + exp(a − 2aτ b ) [SOURCE: Page_112]

Then we normalize it to be 0 at τ = 0 and 1 at τ = 1, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_112]

La,b (τ ) − La,b (0) ∀τ ∈ [0, 1], Fa,b (τ ) =                        . La,b (1) − La,b (0) [SOURCE: Page_112]

This leads to a parameterized logistic-like function Fa,b whose parameters (a, b) may then be used to shape the φ function, as follows. Consider a parameterization (δ, γ), where δ represent the time of half growth (i.e. such that Fa,b (δ) = 0.5) and γ is related to the characteristic growth rate (i.e. the derivative of Fa,b at the time δ), with the following bijective transformations: [SOURCE: Page_112]

1                           exp(a) + 1 δ = f (b) =          ,    γ = g(a) = a ·              . 21/b                         exp(a) − 1 [SOURCE: Page_112]

By computing the inverse transformations f −1 and g −1 , we can finally describe the (rescaled) function used for the fraction of intercepted radiation φ e as follows: [SOURCE: Page_112]

∀τ ∈ [0, 1], φ eδ,γ (τ ) = Fg−1 (γ),f −1 (δ) (τ ).                 (4.10) [SOURCE: Page_112]

This way, the time-rescaling of φ writes φ(t) = φ eδ,γ (t/T ) for all t ∈ [0, T ]. [SOURCE: Page_112]

We refer to Table 4.2 for a list of the model inputs considered in the numerical exploration. [SOURCE: Page_112]

The outputs of a simulation of the CCI model are the trajectories of soil moisture (S), biomass production (B) and water budget consumption (V ). These trajectories are computed by integrating Equations (4.2) to (4.4) over the interval of crop season, thus require a last crucial ingredient : the irrigation strategy u. As shown in [19, 28], in order to compute an optimal strategy, it is sufficient to perform a simulation of CCI for a given grid of possible values of each of the parameterized controls Equation (4.8). From the process of optimization, we extract the optimal decision time topti d , which is the subject of the sensitivity analysis in Section 4.3.2.1. [SOURCE: Page_112]
