4.2. MATERIAL AND METHODS                                                                              113 [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.2.3.2.2 Model parameters ranges As mentioned before, the CCI model has been imple- mented in such a way that the crop season can be rescaled to a normalized time interval [0, 1]. The soil water content values (Srw , Spw ) are considered fixed and follow [3, 143] with the aforementioned scaling of the soil moisture θf c to S = 1. [SOURCE: Page_113]

As discussed previously, we consider several cases as declensions of different contexts. We set two values of operating constraint (greater than the permanent wilting point Spw and less than the (low)                                     (high) incipient water stress point S ∗ ). We chose Sope = 0.37 for the riskier behaviour and Sope = 0.45 as a safer approach. The water quantity Q (quota needed for absence of stress) is computed nu- ∗ [SOURCE: Page_113]

merically. Then we introduce three contexts of water availability : a “low initial quota” defined by Q(low) = 0.55 × Q∗ , a “medium initial quota” defined by Q(med) = 0.7 × Q∗ , and a “high initial quota” defined by Q(high) = 0.85 × Q∗ . [SOURCE: Page_113]

Regarding model inputs investigated through the numerical exploration, we consider wide ranges of values for reference evapotranspiration ET$_0$ , water reservoir V ol and point of incipient water stress S ∗ , following [3, 143]. The initial soil moisture S$_0$ is supposed to be sufficiently high at the beginning of the season, therefore S$_0$ is greater than any value of S ∗ , and up to the field capacity S = 1. The remaining non-documented exploration parameters are : k^2 the capacity of irrigation material to bring water to the soil, δ the (rescaled) date of half plant growth, and γ a characteristic value of growth rate. For these exploration parameters, we chose to consider sufficiently large intervals of values allowing to deal with many types of crop growths and operating systems. The exploration settings are summarized in Table 4.2. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Notation      Unit        Range of values ET$_0$           mm/d        [2, 7] k^2            -           [2, 6] S$_0$            mm/mm       [0.88, 1] S∗            mm/mm       [0.58, 0.87] V ol          mm          [70, 270] δ             d/d         [0.2, 0.8] γ             -           [2, 7] [SOURCE: Page_113]

Table 4.2: List of exploration parameters with their range of values for the numerical exploration. [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.2.3.2.3 Global sensitivity analysis on optimal controls A global sensitivity analysis was conducted in order to obtain insight into the model parameters and better understand their influ- ence on the shape of optimal TBISCCI . This analysis was repeated to provide a broader view of model responses to model inputs in various typical situations of targeted study cases, represented by the pair of contextual parameters (Sope , Q). [SOURCE: Page_113]

We considered a set of 6 typical situations : two values for the tolerance setpoint Sope (a lower one representing riskier irrigation strategies allowing more water stress, and a higher one allowing less water stress corresponding to a behaviour looking for production guarantees), combined with three values for the initial water quota (a low one, a medium one and a high one, for exploring the range of possibilities in terms of water availability). [SOURCE: Page_113]

For each situation, the sensitivity analysis was based on a global exploration of the space of model parameters, each of these parameters belonging to an interval with a uniform distribution according to the range of variation given in Table 4.2. Thanks to the low computational time of the simulation, we can conduct a variance-based sensitivity analysis over our global exploration settings, providing first-order Sobol Indices (SI) and Total order Sobol Indices (TSI) [166] which quantify the contribution of input variance (either alone or including all interactions effects) to the variance of model outputs. [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.2.3.2.4 Multi-local perturbation analysis Once an optimal solution has been identified, in which irrigation dynamics is dictated by an optimal set of control parameters, a complementary analysis investigates the effects of mistakes committed on the most important control parameters. This analysis consists in a perturbation around the optimal control parameters values to account for either experimental limitations (difficult-to-measure parameter values), conceptual flaws (the problem has not been perfectly understood) or other incidents (the correct decisions were known but have not been taken, or not in due time). [SOURCE: Page_114]

The multi-local perturbation analysis was conducted as follows. For each exploration parameter opti opti set drawn from the ranges described in Table 4.2, we determined the pair (Stop         , td ) that cor- opti                         opti responds to the optimal TBISCCI , Stop being given by S and td being numerically computed. ∗ [SOURCE: Page_114]

At this point, the final biomass is thus what we called the “contextual biomass optimum” B (S$_0$ ,Q) associated to the initial soil moisture S$_0$ and water quota Q. We then built a grid of pairs (Stop , td ) around that optimal point, using a prescribed grid size: ∆Stop = Stop − S ∗ = ±10% of relative soil moisture, and ∆td = td − toptid   = ±10% of the overall duration of the cropping season. At each point P = (Stop , td ) of the grid, we numerically compute the corresponding sub-optimal terminal biomass BP (T ). Then we determine the relative loss of biomass production compared to the optimal one, expressed in percentage the following way: [SOURCE: Page_114]

B (S$_0$ ,Q) − BP (T ) ∆sub−opti (P ) =                          .                    (4.12) B (S$_0$ ,Q) [SOURCE: Page_114]

Finally, at each grid point P , we compute mLoss(P ) the mean value of relative biomass loss ∆sub−opti (P ) averaged over the exploration parameter set. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Besides percentage losses in final biomass values, in comparison to the contextual optimum, we provide complementary indications as regards situations in which soil water content goes below the chosen operating constraint Sope , or conversely, situations in which the water quota Q is not entirely consumed. Justifications for this are that biomass sub-optimality may likely be explained by cumulative water stress, and that reaching high water use efficiency, relating biomass with cumulative irrigation amounts (“more crops per drop”), may also be a relevant management criterion. To do so, we considered 6 “typical” situations combining riskier or safer operating constraints, with limiting or comfortable initial water quotas, to explore various near-optimal situations strategies. [SOURCE: Page_114]

4.2.3.2.5 Implementation details To run a simulation, the model requires to solve Equa- tions (4.2) to (4.4), then the code relies on the classical Euler explicit method for discretization and integration of systems of ODEs. The time horizon was discretized using 400 time steps. This has been implemented with the Python language. [SOURCE: Page_114]

The first order Sobol Indices and Total order Sobol Indices were computed through a Sobol- Jansen methodology [88, 150] using the R package “Sensitivity” for implementation [82]. Our simu- lation was done using a sample of 6000 points, with 7 model parameters, leading to an exploration plan of 54000 points. [SOURCE: Page_114]

For the multi-local sensitivity analysis, we used a sample of 3000 random points combined with a (Stop , td ) grid of size 21 × 29 to produce a simulation output. [SOURCE: Page_114]

4.3. RESULTS                                                                                           115 [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.3       Results 4.3.1     Soil filling from possible irrigation strategies 4.3.1.1    Reference optimal structure For the indicated context, connecting the CCI model (and its theoretical analysis) to the TBISCCI (and its physical interpretation) provides first results. We can conclude from theory (Section 4.2.3.1) that five successive stages are present, at most, in the irrigation planing, in the general case, and always in the same order, as listed below and illustrated in Figure 4.2. Considering a bottom threshold Sbot for the first stage of the season and a top threshold Stop for the productive phase, a corresponding TBISCCI can be written as the following comprehensive set of successive instructions, containing guidelines about the amount of irrigation to employ during each stage and a “switching condition” to move from one stage to the next, throughout an intermediate period starting at the decision time td . [SOURCE: Page_115]

• Stage (i) : u(t) = 0 At the beginning of the cropping season, the initial soil moisture S$_0$ is supposed to be high, thus the irrigator lets the system evolve only by relying on soil resources, until it reaches the aimed value Sbot for the first stage of the cropping season. - The switching condition (i) → (ii) is S = Sbot . • Stage (ii) : u(t) = ũ(t, Sbot ) During this stage, the operator irrigates with limited water amounts in order to keep the soil moisture at the chosen value Sbot . This irrigation is kept until the irrigator decides at the time td to not tolerate high stress anymore and move on to the production phase. - The switching condition (ii) → (iii) occurs at the decision time td to trigger maximal flow of irrigation. • Stage (iii) : u(t) = 1 Once the irrigator decides to advance towards the production phase, the irrigation needs to be applied at its maximal capacity, in order to reach the desired level Stop as soon as possible. - The switching condition (iii) → (iv) is S = Stop . • Stage (iv) : u(t) = ũ(t, Stop ) When the soil moisture reaches the value corresponding to the production phase, the operator irrigates with the appropriate rate to keep the soil moisture at this chosen value Stop , until the whole water quota is entirely consumed (i.e. V = Q). - The switching condition (iv) → (v) is V = Q. • Stage (v) : u(t) = 0 When the entire quota of water has been consumed, then there is no choice but let the crop finishing its growth only using the remaining soil resources. [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.3.1.2    Declensions in particular cases Particular cases, still fulfilling necessary conditions listed in Section 4.2.3.1, will arise from spe- cific combinations of the contextual parameters so that some of these five stages may be absent (Figure 4.3). In much more details, each of the eight different possible shapes of soil moisture dynamics, all shown in Figure 4.3, appears for certain combinations of values of the contextual parameters. Those exhibit general as well as specific features, listed below. [SOURCE: Page_115]

A very general feature is that the slope of the falling limbs in the dynamics of soil moisture (corresponding to the “u = 0” irrigation periods) is dictated both by the capacity of soil reservoir (V ol) and the transpiration and evaporation rates, calculated from crop status (KS , KR , φ), in response to the climatic demand (ET0 ). Smaller soil reservoirs and higher demands will result in steeper downward slopes, although the relative contributions of transpiration and evaporation will [SOURCE: Page_115]

Figure 4.2: General case of a parameterized TBISCCI showing the irrigation chronicle u(·) and associated soil moisture dynamics in time S(·), across the five successive stages, (i) to (v), known from theory (see text for details). Note that the td value, indicating the “decision time” at which the bottom threshold should be left and the top threshold should be aimed at, is an outcome of problem parameterization and not a prescribed value. [SOURCE: Page_116]

differ for different canopy covers thus crop development stages. Conversely, the slope of the rising limbs (corresponding to the “u = 1” irrigation periods, initiated at td ) is controlled by the Fmax characteristic of the irrigation system, as well as by V ol (the relative filling of smaller reservoirs will increase faster) and the transpiration and evaporation rates, acting against Fmax . In complement, the greater the quota, the longer soil moisture should stay at the top threshold Stop during the corresponding stage, and the shorter the bottom stage Sbot should last. [SOURCE: Page_116]

By contrast, specific features arise from specific cases. Insufficient irrigation rates and (or) too limited quotas will thus suppress stage (iv) and prevent reaching the setpoint Stop value (b). High initial S$_0$ values, when combined with high quotas, will suppress stages (ii-iii) thus the necessity to stay a certain time at the Sbot level (c), low initial S$_0$ values, combined with limited irrigation rates, will suppress stages (i-ii) in which water contents are allowed to drop before initiating irrigation (d), the large difference between high setpoint Stop values and low Sbot values, with a sufficient quota, will suppress stage (ii) as well as the risk to approach those low Sbot values (e). High initial S$_0$ val- ues, combined with a low reference evapotranspiration rate (limited water losses) and a low quota will suppress stages (ii) and (iv) thus hold S values in an intermediate region between Sbot and Stop (f). Initial S$_0$ values close to the setpoint Stop values and large quotas will suppress stages (i, ii, iii) thus the necessity to temporarily tolerate low water contents (g). Low initial S$_0$ values, combined with low maximal irrigation rate, will suppress stages (i, ii) and (iv) and suggest to progressively approach the setpoint Stop value, in line with the canopy growth rate, before ceasing irrigation (h). Examples of sets of model parameters for some of these particular cases are given in Table 4.3. Situations (a) to (h) might also be reached by other non-trivial combinations of effects following less easily interpretable sets of model parameters. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Notice that (d) (g) and (h) are not part of the global exploration since we have S0 > S ∗ and opti Stop  = S ∗ , but these cases might occur with particular settings in the multi-local perturbation analysis. [SOURCE: Page_116]

## 118                                            CHAPTER 4. EXPLORING OPTIMALITY OF TBIS

4.3.2     Numerical exploration 4.3.2.1   Sensitivity of the optimal decision time to the explored parameters We present the results of the global sensitivity analysis conducted with the CCI model on 6 study cases following the possible pairs (Sope , Q). The values and ranges for each exploration parameter are those defined in Table 4.2. Results are summarized in Figure 4.4. [SOURCE: Page_118]

4.3.2.1.1 Variability of the optimal decision time First notice that the optimal decision time topti d   has a quite significant variability (globally, its possible values practically cover the whole time span) over the global exploration. This variability is reduced when the quota increases and when the operating constraint becomes lower. The latter seems quite intuitive, as lower thresholds mean stronger water stress which expectedly encourages leaving low moisture levels during an earlier (thus smaller) window of time. The former may seem less intuitive, as it means that lower quotas enhance the diversity of admissible solutions whereas one would probably expect that larger quotas would open way for a diversity of optimal solutions. In fact, greater quotas lead to more admissible trajectories, i.e. wider windows for the choice of optimal decision time. This way, admissible trajec- tories are able to generate optimal values for different exploration parameter settings that tend to be closer. In contrast, lower quotas induce smaller windows for the choice of optimal decision time for each parameter settings, then optimal choices are a bit more spread over time. [SOURCE: Page_118]

In addition the median optimal decision time tends to be slightly more precocious in situations with low Sope compared to high Sope . Even without any explicit description of water stress effects, other than a reduction of transpiration and evaporation, this tends to indicate that more risky sit- uations (lower operating boundary) should not last long while less risky situations can last a little longer (higher operating boundary). This suggests the existence of a “hidden effect” by which the overall impact on biomass production is the integral of water deficit over time, as usually found only in more complex models. Conversely, lesser quotas tend to delay the optimal decision time so that increased impacts over the plant stress (lower Sope , lower quota), when considered individu- ally, have opposite effects on the optimal decision time values. Finally, high and almost non-limiting quotas lead to significantly earlier and clearly identified optimal decision times, with little variability. [SOURCE: Page_118]

In summary, this analysis of the topti d    values and variability allows identifying the roles played by the assumed key external constraint (Q) and the allegedly associated irrigator’s decision (Sope ) so as to frame the overall context in which irrigation management and optimization will take place. For a complementary and thorough view, next section details which of the “internal” problem exploration parameters (soil, crop, climate, performance of the irrigation system) control those general insights. [SOURCE: Page_118]

4.3.2.1.2 Explored parameters that dictate the optimal decision time For a low operat- ing constraint, the topti d    value mostly depends on one exploration parameter (δ), while it depends on the pair of exploration parameters (S ∗ , δ) for a high operating constraint. More precisely, with a low operating constraint, the TSI of each exploration parameter except δ does not exceed 0.14 for any initial water quota, while the SI of δ is always above 0.65. With a high operating constraint, the TSI of each exploration parameter except S ∗ and δ does not exceed 0.15 for any initial water quota, while the SI of S ∗ varies from 0.19 to 0.48 and the SI of δ varies from 0.19 to 0.59. This motivates further investigations, wondering about the nature of the dependency of topti d    with respect to these most influential exploration parameters. (low) We illustrate, in Figure 4.5 (low operating constraint Sope ) and Figure 4.6 (high operating (high) constraint Sope ), the nature of the response with respect to their corresponding most influential exploration parameters. For lower Sope values, apart from limited dispersion around a clearly visible trend, the topti d   values tend to increase with δ values, in a roughly linear and identical way, whatever the (Q(low) , Q(med) , Q(high) ) quota values (Figure 4.5). For higher Sope values, the trend to increasing topti d   values with increasing δ values remains whatever the S ∗ values (reading the plots from left to right) but the larger gradients of topti d    values are obtained for jointly increasing δ values and decreasing S ∗ values (Figure 4.6). Finally, the range of topti d   values found for higher Sope values is larger, which denotes the influence of S ∗ values, or rather that of a reduced distance between S ∗ [SOURCE: Page_118]

A likely implication, in operational terms, is that high risk aversion, signaled by high Sope values close to the assumed S ∗ stress initiation threshold, introduces a stronger dependence of the optimal decision time topti d    on crop growth characteristics. Overall, crops characterized by a rather late quick growth phase (high δ values, which is the case for most crops) will need delayed topti    d values to obtain the best possible biomass production, all the more for reduced quotas and high risk aversion. 4.3.2.2   Biomass losses with sub-optimal strategies We present the results of the multi-local perturbation analysis conducted with the CCI model on 6 study cases following the possible pairs (Sope , Q). The values and ranges for each exploration parameter are those defined in Table 4.2. Results are summarized in Figure 4.8. [SOURCE: Page_120]

4.3.2.2.1 Possible consequences of small mistakes Figure 4.7 shows the principles and out- comes of this exploration. In accordance, biomass production was computed at each point of the tested grid, i.e., in the tested configurations of Figure 4.7, so as to assess the loss in percentage com- pared to the optimal biomass value obtained with (S ∗ , topti d ) (known as the “contextual optimum”). Some pairs (Stop , td ) in the exploration grid might lead to non-admissible values of S in light of the constraints defined in Section 4.2.2.2, here S < Sope in some cases, late in the season. Non-admissible trajectories are discarded from the computation of the average relative biomass losses, but will be counted to indicate their ratio of occurrence. Other couples (Stop , td ) in the grid lead to trajectories consuming less than the total amount of available irrigation water Q. These two characteristics of some of the irrigation scenarios (non-admissibility, under quota thus water savings) are illustrated, among others, in Figure 4.7. As also possibly anticipated from Figure 4.7, a too early decision time td combined with a too high Stop value might lead to too severe final stress conditions (S values below Sope ) while a too late decision time td combined with a too low Stop value might lead to water savings (water consumed for irrigation is below the initial quota) but necessarily sub-optimal biomass production. Those situations are described in much more details in Figure 4.8. [SOURCE: Page_120]

4.3.2.2.2 Performances of sub-optimal strategies Overall, the most striking feature of Fig- ure 4.8 is the difference in biomass loss between cases Stop > S ∗ (upper half of each plot) and Stop < S ∗ (lower half). Indeed, slightly (less than 10% of the optimal soil moisture) overestimating the setpoint value during the productive stage leads to less than 5% of biomass loss in almost all [SOURCE: Page_120]

4.4. DISCUSSION                                                                                       123 [SOURCE: Page_123]

cases (with both low and high operating constraints, and any initial water quota), while underes- timating it (by up to 10%) may cause up to at least 15% of biomass loss. In complement, in the lower half of the plots, the isovalues tend to be approximately horizontal, especially for high quotas: gradients of biomass loss are therefore due to mistakes in Stop threshold values (rather than mistakes in the decision time td ). Conversely, in the upper half, and to a lesser extent, it is rather mistakes on the decision time td values that cause losses in final biomass (rather than mistakes in Stop values), especially for low initial quotas, where percentages of biomass losses are about twice as large as for high water quotas. Notice that there is no significant overall difference in terms of relative biomass losses between the two situations of risk inclination (left column) or aversion (right column). [SOURCE: Page_123]

4.3.2.2.3 Complementary operating indications As regards non-admissible or water-saving parameterization and trajectories (Table 4.4), the greater the initial water quota, the less probability there is to violate the stress constraint near the end of the cropping season (S < Sope ) when triggering maximal rate of irrigation too early, and the more probability there is to save a fraction of the quota when delaying the decision time. In contrast to the analysis over biomass losses, we observe quite significant dissimilarities between risk inclination (left column) and risk aversion (right column), especially regarding the occurrence of non-admissible trajectories. Indeed it is definitely more likely to not fulfill the operating constraint when the productive threshold is set at a higher value, and it is intuitive that riskier behaviour allows to consider a wider variety of trajectories. We also remark a non-intuitive phenomenon, that there is slightly more chances to save water with a higher operating constraint in the case of a low water quota, but there is slightly more chances to save water with a lower operating constraint in the case of a high water quota. It might be explained by the fact that a lower operating constraint leads to more water stress then reduces the possibility to allocate irrigation water at will, which is particularly affected when the water quota is low. Conversely, the increase of water quota leads to more choices of trajectories, thus greater possibility to modulate the allocation of water, which is even amplified when the operating constraint is low. [SOURCE: Page_123]

4.4     Discussion This paper offers the possibility of handling very different cases (initial, tolerance and setpoint water content levels, soil volume, crop characteristics, maximum irrigation rate, climatic demand) with the same framework, deriving the optimal, associated chronicles of S (water content, soil filling) and u (irrigation chronicle) in the general case (Figure 4.2) broken down into different declensions for the different particular cases (Figure 4.3). This may have interesting operational applications, as knowledge of the contextual and exploration parameters will decide which of the strategies to follow, either with maximal rate of irrigation scheme (Figure 4.2) or with shortcuts and missing stages, in the relevant sketch of Figure 4.3. [SOURCE: Page_123]

For the irrigation operator, the emerging part of the previous developments, their outcome, certainly is the so called “decision time” (td ), depending on all previously mentioned elements. No- ticeably, those elements include the risk aversion (high Sope values) or inclination (low Sope values) of the irrigator, which also show throughout the chosen values for the Sope parameter denoting the lower limit of admissible water contents. One step further, the proposed framework altogether accounts for those irrigator’s choices, seeking the optimal solution (S(t), u(t), td ) for the assigned context, and allows questioning those choices, comparing the final biomass production in other para- [SOURCE: Page_123]

## 124                                           CHAPTER 4. EXPLORING OPTIMALITY OF TBIS

metric configurations. Investigating the effects of mistakes committed on the essential TBISCCI and decision parameters (S ∗ , Sope , td ), whether those mistakes arise from imperfect measurements and (or) conceptualization, is making another step towards future operational applications. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Regarding the implementation of TBISCCI in real-world irrigation systems, the capacity to ir- rigate at given rates depends on the irrigation equipment. In general, an irrigation event consists in providing in a certain time frame some amount of water. The irrigator may define a setpoint level to trigger this irrigation, which may consist in either a fixed amount of water, or watering up to a target setpoint level. A TBISCCI can be achieved by splitting in two successive time periods to keep the humidity level close to the desired setpoint value on each period. This will require to perform up (when irrigating) and down (when not irrigating) fluctuations around the setpoint value, and the "decision time" td corresponds to the moment of switching from the first period to the second one. Such implementation can be seen as a time discretization of the theoretical TBISCCI . [SOURCE: Page_124]

Since the optimal strategies of the CCI model are written as TBISCCI , thus are consistent with usual irrigation practices, it is reasonable to foresee putting to the test the scenarios generated by those optima. Thanks to their low-dimensional structure, implementing TBISCCI in experimental process or into complex numerical crop irrigation models can be considered. Such studies should bring more reliability on the validity of qualitative results presented in this work, and help under- standing the potential and limitations of TBISCCI for improving water management. [SOURCE: Page_124]

The previous sections illustrate the merits and drawbacks of different strategies, either opti- mal or sub-optimal, in maximizing biomass production from a given cumulative irrigation amount. Hence, a given irrigation strategy may be characterized by those two values: the final biomass and the irrigation amount. An additional, elaborate variable may be introduced to describe irrigation efficiency, relating the biomass produced to the irrigation amount needed for it. This will expect- edly not replace the separate criteria in terms of quota (using less than a certain quota) and yield (producing more than a contractual yield) but rather provide an additional criteria, allowing to sort the eligible strategies according to their efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_124]

The proposed framework may be enriched with the adjunction of economic variables of three kinds: fixed costs accounting for system operation, variables costs linked to water and energy consumption and the expected selling price (or valorization) of the produced biomass. From the economic point of view, the eligible strategies would still respect the quota and ensure sufficient, if not maximal income. Biomass production and economic income (say E) are expected to be as closely related as variables costs are limited and irrigation efficiency is high but still the optimisa- tion problem should likely be relaxed, as “joint optimization” (both the B and E variables at their maximum possible values) is not achievable in the general case. Due to its genericity, the CCI model is compatible with such considerations of various criteria ([28]). [SOURCE: Page_124]

Fertilization is not addressed here, meaning that non-limiting nutrient availability is hypothe- sized. The proposed framework may also be widened by the introduction of nitrogen requirements evolving with plant growth, likely following the dynamics of the φ function with a slight delay (e.g. the nitrogen demand sharply increases in the phase of quick canopy growth) (see [73]). Introducing nitrogen concentration as an additional state would bring more complexity for the methodology, and the decision policy would also depend on a more diversified set of rules. Here, nutrients could certainly be brought via fertigation (the irrigation system also delivers nutrients) in complement of an initial amount. This would consequently affect the economic budget. [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.5     Conclusion In this work, we dealt with the problem of improving crop irrigation strategies, in various con- texts suffering limited resources. For this purpose, we considered a class of crop irrigation strategies whose structure is based on assumed known or operator-defined setpoint levels of soil moisture, in succession throughout the cropping season. It has been established that these Threshold-Based Ir- rigation Strategies emerge from solving an optimal control problem formulated with the Controlled Crop Irrigation model, which is an infinite dimensional optimization problem. More precisely, op- [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.5. CONCLUSION                                                                                    125 [SOURCE: Page_125]

timal policies are described as TBISCCI with two soil moisture thresholds, and a time of decision to trigger maximal irrigation rate leading to a one-dimensional optimization. This optimization is thus suited to efficient numerical simulations, and we implemented a global model exploration in order to provide properties about the nature of the crucial time of decision, and the consequences of near-optimal decisions in terms of biomass losses and water savings. [SOURCE: Page_125]

Our first numerical exploration aimed at assessing Sobol Indices giving valuable insights on the global sensitivity of the decision time with respect to a wide range of soil, plant, operational char- acteristics. We highlighted that in both alternatives of risk aversion or inclination, the optimal time of triggering maximal irrigation rate is strongly related to plant characteristics, regardless of the initial irrigation water quota. In particular, the values of optimal decision time globally follow an increasingly monotonous behaviour with respect to a characteristic growth time, and a decreas- ingly monotonous behaviour with respect to the soil water content of incipient stress. A second numerical experiment investigates the sub-optimality of TBISCCI based on small errors around the optimal values of decision time (in the window of admissible decision times) and top soil moisture threshold. We observed that in both alternatives as above and in any case of initial irrigation water quota, mistakes over the top soil moisture threshold may undermine the biomass production by up to 15% compared to the maximal potential biomass. Conversely, mistakes over the decision time tend to have moderate effects over the biomass production. However, early and late decisions lead to noteworthy different outcomes in terms of chances to violate the operating constraint or saving a fraction of irrigation water. The combination of low irrigation quotas, high risk aversion and early decisions, creates strong operational constraints on the use of water resources. It makes more difficult (or unlikely) the identification (or the existence) of admissible soil moisture trajectories. The opposite extreme combination of high irrigation quotas, high risk inclination and late decisions, provides favorable conditions for unhindered and efficient operational use of water. [SOURCE: Page_125]

Our results demonstrate the capabilities of the coupled optimal control and global exploration methodology, to point out some principles of enhancing the management of irrigation scheduling. With our in-depth analysis of TBISCCI , we opened a perspective to assess their potential in terms of water use efficiency. This could lead to suggest a multi-criteria approach for a decision making based on a compromise between producing more biomass, saving more water, and preventing pro- longed periods of water stress. The present study case was limited to greenhouse systems. Future work might extend the methodology to more general systems including rainfall events. Further perspectives might additionally consider identifying sets of parameters corresponding to particular contexts (soil type thus properties, crop growth dynamics), in order to put the TBIS to the test on experimental studies. [SOURCE: Page_125]

Part II [SOURCE: Page_127]

On-going works towards an application to the Optirrig model and an extension to open-field conditions [SOURCE: Page_127]

127 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Chapter 5 [SOURCE: Page_129]

The Optirrig and CCI models [SOURCE: Page_129]

This chapter corresponds to a work in progress, included in both the thesis objectives and the col- laborative multidisciplinary project conducted for the development of Optirrig. Indeed we tackle here the subject of double modeling, involving the Optirrig model formalisms and the simple CCI model that we studied in the previous chapters (in the greenhouse context). This work aims for providing a correspondence framework between those two models in order to conduct a shared investigation of irrigation strategies. In particular, the Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies (that are proven to be optimal for the CCI formulation) present a promising candidate scenario for practical and opera- tional efficiency purposes. As a consequence, it is of great interest to adapt these irrigation strategies for an implementation into Optirrig, in order to evaluate their performances in study cases closer to real-world conditions. Furthermore, a thorough comparison between the physical processes and the conceptual formalisms of both models allows us to notice where the approximations in the simple CCI model may have to be completed in order to improve its biophysical viability. Conversely, the genericity of the CCI model enables to point out the most essential intrinsic roles and effects of the system, and may help to elaborate new versions of Optirrig where we concentrate the description on these essential features. [SOURCE: Page_129]

5.1     Models comparison guidelines Following the conceptual scheme of the double modeling framework Figure 1.1 that we intend to carry out between Optirrig and the CCI model, several directions and elements of development may be considered. First we may wonder if the theoretical results obtained in the greenhouse context with the CCI formulation (see Chapter 3 and Chapter 4) are still valid, or at least provide interest- ing material, when trying to transpose them into operational situations with Optirrig. Then, the standard loop of double modeling involves calibrating the two models at some operating point, and running parallel simulations in order to conduct, for example, a joint optimization. In any case, the first step for comparing models is to evaluate their compatibility, and establish a set of connections as complete as possible. [SOURCE: Page_129]

As previously mentioned, Optirrig is focused on implementing how the different irrigation strate- gies (or water management) may have different impacts on the crop production (or financial bene- fits). In order to cover a wide variety of farming types, objectives, needs or constraints, we present in Figure 5.1 a list of possible strategies for the operator when using Optirrig. The specificity of Optirrig is that it allows the operator to evaluate and compare different classes of strategies which are an integral part of the implementation for the simulation runs. Moreover, it is expected that an operator may want to define several successive time periods where they will conduct the irrigation following different strategies, or even they may decide during the season to shift their scenario to another strategy. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Moreover, the CCI model formulation precisely relies on the control framework, where the de- cision variable (the irrigation rate) is the key feature of analysis. As a result, the compatibility discussion between Optirrig and the CCI model should begin with a work plan explaining how irri- [SOURCE: Page_129]

5.2. PRESENTATION OF THE OPTIRRIG MODEL                                                                       131 [SOURCE: Page_131]

gation strategies (i.e. the main object of study for both models) can be transferred from one model to the other. Since the mathematical framework allows complete flexibility for the nature of irri- gation chronicles (measurable functions in continuous time), then any irrigation scenario produced by Optirrig with any strategy can at least be expressed as a time-varying function suited to CCI control framework. Conversely, we do not aim for representing every measurable function in the Optirrig formalism, but only the theoretical strategies of interest. In particular, the 2-TBIS and their declensions detailed in Chapter 4 are strategies that we should be able to test in Optirrig. Notice that the strategies in Figure 5.1 may be formulated in open-loop (n°1), closed-loop (from n°2 to n°8), or closed-loop with scheduling constraints (from n°9 to n°16). Moreover, the criteria of irrigation triggering may be based on soil water levels, stress levels, water accumulation or temper- ature accumulation over a sliding window time. We illustrate in Figure 5.2 two typical ways to base a strategy on a soil moisture threshold (denoted pctRU for “percentage of available water capacity” in these cases), that only differ in the type of action: strategy n°2 consists in giving always the same amount of water (regardless to the resulting soil moisture level), and strategy n°3 consists in giving the amount (regardless of the dose value) of water to reach a given soil moisture level. [SOURCE: Page_131]

Figure 5.2: Visual comparison of the strategies n°2 and n°3, which are based on the same irrigation triggering criteria, but differ in the decision of irrigation: while strategy n°2 consists in giving always the same amount of water (regardless to the resulting soil moisture level), strategy n°3 consists in giving the amount (regardless of the dose value) of water to reach a given soil moisture level. [SOURCE: Page_131]

The objective of the following sections is to provide a detailed correspondence between Optirrig and the CCI model, in order to prepare in particular the implementation and evaluation of the 2-TBIS in Optirrig. We may suggest here that we identified the strategy n°3 as a candidate for such purposes. Indeed, an irrigation strategy based on two thresholds appears to require a succession of two periods associated to two different thresholds (of triggering, thus also two of target), on which we could conduct the two corresponding strategies n°3 successively. The previously called “decision time” would correspond here to the date of shifting from the first to the second strategy n°3. [SOURCE: Page_131]

5.2         Presentation of the Optirrig model 5.2.1       A wide-purpose irrigation optimization tool The Optirrig model [34, 33] was built to perform the generation, analysis and optimization of irrigation scenarios, assuming the simplest possible description of plant physiology while focusing on the identification of relevant resources allocation, in ex-post (analysis), ex-ante (prospective) or real-time (decision support) contexts. Model development relies on collaborative, multidisciplinary contributions. Its logic of development is to initiate collaborative developments regarding the de- scription of soil and crop-related physical processes, the specification of variables of interest or the exploration of case-studies in envisioned model applications. Moreover, the purpose of Optirrig is to be used for interactions with policy makers, to propose, demonstrate and promote new ways to tackle the water allocation issue. [SOURCE: Page_131]

## 132                                              CHAPTER 5. THE OPTIRRIG AND CCI MODELS

The model is formulated with discrete time and a fixed step, and relies on a parsimonious descrip- tion of soil water budget, plant growth, properties and biomass production to rather place emphasis on plot-scale water management, via the identification of the most appropriate (constrained) irri- gation strategies. Being a 1-D vertical model, Optirrig neither allows any explicit description of lateral fluxes nor any indication as regards the area of its domain of application. The latter only has to be a domain in which heterogeneities are not too strong, hence supporting spatially-averaged descriptions. Some key physical variables are mandatory components of the model, such as the time-varying content (or percentage filling) of soil water reservoirs, the evolving Leaf Area Index (LAI) of the crop and the daily rate of biomass production. [SOURCE: Page_132]

In the Optirrig model, water stress is the driver of losses in crop growth, and the issue addressed here is about reducing such losses as much as possible, in various so called ”deficit irrigation” situ- ations. Here, piloting irrigation thus means acting upon water stress, described as deficit of plant transpiration, over certain period of time. In other words, stress terms are the emerging, accessi- ble part of the conceptual scheme, irrigation being the lever of action upon them (notice that it is however difficult to conduct direct measurements of stress). Water stresses are visible through their short-term ans long-term effects on the other variables, which are observable for most of them. Providing efficient irrigation schedules therefore consists in managing water stresses for given con- straints and objectives. [SOURCE: Page_132]

The piloting of irrigation and its optimization purpose may differ according to various points of view (water management, crop production, financial aspects) with their associated metrics (water use efficiency, water productivity, economic viability, societal expectations, contractual crop yields, water or environmental footprints). To this end, the Optirrig model focuses on order-1 processes and levers of action, i.e. how the manipulated variable (which is irrigation) spreads out in the model conceptual scheme, seeking compromise between being right for the right biophysical reasons and providing meaningful evaluation of the operational variables (biomass production, harvest index, crop yield) regarding harmfulness and overall effect due to water stress. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Obtaining irrigation strategies consists in the difficult task of finding irrigation decision rules able to cope at best with (i) contradictory objectives (in the “more crop per drop” view), (ii) operational constraints (insufficient water rights or quota, limited water availability or shared access to water, characteristics of the irrigation equipment), (iii) regulatory constraints (restrictions during periods of tension on the shared resource), (iv) poor availability of supervision data (ungauged or poorly- gauged contexts), (v) multiple uncertainties (biases and shortcuts in the model, errors affecting model forcings, parameters and observation data), (vi) unexpected difficulties (equipment defaults, personal issues, irrigations missed) and (vii) events that are unpredictable by nature, such as vari- ations in the climatic forcings, especially in rain chronicles, to which irrigation must react and adapt. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Different irrigation decision rules, i.e. irrigation settings and parameters, will likely translate into different irrigation scenarios, i.e. chronicles of irrigation dates and amounts. In other words, different sets of irrigation parameters (triggering criteria, setpoints and controls) will result in differ- ent dynamics for the filling of soil reservoirs, with cascading effects throughout the entire conceptual scheme. This will produce different dynamics for the main state variables (reserves, leaf are index, biomass) and, likely, different final values for the operational variables (cumulative irrigation, crop yield) which opens way for a wide and multi-criteria case comparison. The latter strongly depends on the model ability to handle and analyse multiple runs. Put together, all components take place in a software (manual, graphical interface, versioning...), not described here but being the vehicle for model transfer, which is the actual development object that we call Optirrig. [SOURCE: Page_132]

5.2.2     Model rationale This section provides an introduction of the Optirrig model concepts, variables and parameters that are relevant for both the understanding of the Optirrig model and the correspondence with the CCI model. This presentation is not exhaustive but is as detailed as needed for the current purposes. [SOURCE: Page_132]

5.2. PRESENTATION OF THE OPTIRRIG MODEL                                                          133 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Figure 5.3: Reference conceptual scheme of Optirrig, briefly detailed in the current section, showing the causal links between model variables (coloured boxes, see Table 5.1) as well as the position and limited number of key model parameters (see Table 5.2). This scheme pertains to field crops modelling in classical conditions. For some families of species, this reference scheme will need some adaptations, but will not be discussed here. [SOURCE: Page_133]

## 136                                               CHAPTER 5. THE OPTIRRIG AND CCI MODELS

Window of time The Optirrig model is written as a discrete-time evolution model, and any variable or time-dependent quantity a(t) wil be denoted ai , where i represents the current time step. Moreover, according to its conceptual scheme (Figure 5.3), the Optirrig model is a thermal time model, which means the driving force behind crop development is the sum of daily-averaged air temperature values. More precisely, crop development is calculated on a daily basis from the sum of the differences between the daily-averaged air temperature value Ti and a crop-specific base temperature Tb under which crop growth is supposed to halt, so that the actual variable representing time is Xi Ts,i =    Tk − Tb ,                                    (5.1) k=1 [SOURCE: Page_136]

with an initial cumulative thermal time set at Ts,0 = 0 (at sowing). [SOURCE: Page_136]

The phase of crop expansion is assumed to end when Ts,i reaches the crop-specific Tm value, associated with the maximum development of plant foliage and canopy, i.e. the maximum Leaf Area Index (LAI) value. This way, the thermal time interval of system evolution (from sowing to maximal LAI value) is [0, Tm ]. Let us thus define the normalized elapsed thermal time Te,i at day i as the following fraction: Ts,i Te,i =      ,                                     (5.2) Tm where Te,i ranges between 0 and 1 from the initiation to the end of crop expansion phase. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Potential Leaf Area Index The potential LAI growth, in absence of any stress, is calculated as [SOURCE: Page_136]

LAIp,i = LAIm T Li (Te,i , α1 , β),                           (5.3) [SOURCE: Page_136]

where LAIm is the magnitude term and the maximal possible LAI value, while T L is a pseudo- logistic, S-shaped function of thermal time (Te ) and shape parameters (α1 , β). The expected prop- erty of S-shape is present in the chosen expression for T L, as follows: β                  β T Li = Te,i exp           (1 − Te,i α1 ) ,                       (5.4) α1 where only β > α1 > 1 will ensure the T L and LAI curves have the required S-shape, which means an inflection point is present in the curve. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Evaporation and transpiration Let us begin with the transpiration (T P 0) and evaporation (ES$_0$) demands, which are evaluated from the crop coefficient Kc, the partition coefficient Cp, and the climatic demand ET 0, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_136]

T P 0i = Kci Cpi ET 0i ,                                (5.5) [SOURCE: Page_136]

ES0i = Kci (1 − Cpi )ET 0i ,                              (5.6) where both the crop coefficient and the partition coefficient (also called canopy coefficient) are func- tions of LAI. [SOURCE: Page_136]

The crop coefficient writes [SOURCE: Page_136]

Kci = Kcmax 1 − exp(−kLAIi ) ,                                 (5.7)  [SOURCE: Page_136]

where Kcmax is the maximal possible value of Kc for a given crop. In previous studies, the k coefficient has always been set to k = 1. Smaller k values would rather affect the plateau values of Kc than its dynamics, but might enable some modeling flexibility, to handle situations in which the link between LAI and Kc could otherwise not be correctly modeled. [SOURCE: Page_136]

The partition coefficient writes [SOURCE: Page_136]

Cpi = c1 − exp(−c2 LAIi ),                                (5.8) [SOURCE: Page_136]

5.2. PRESENTATION OF THE OPTIRRIG MODEL                                                            137 [SOURCE: Page_137]

where, again, c^1 and c^2 have always taken fixed values (c^1 = 1, c^2 = 0.7) but offer potentially inter- esting degrees of freedom, unmentioned so far, with associated difficulties in parameter estimation. In fact, as Cp aims to depict how the transpiration demand takes over the evaporation demand with foliage development and canopy growth, Cp is expected to start with Cp = 0 for LAI = 0, which requires c^1 = 1. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Once the demands (ES$_0$, T P 0) are known, the next step is to estimate the answers (ES, T P ). First define the evaporation reservoir as follows: [SOURCE: Page_137]

RES,i = ZES θES,i ,                                     (5.9) [SOURCE: Page_137]

where ZES is a characteristic depth of soil from where the evaporation is possible (it strongly depends on soil texture), and θES is the average soil water content in the evaporation reservoir (of depth ZES ). Then define the transpiration reservoir as follows: [SOURCE: Page_137]

RT P,i = ZT P,i θT P,i ,                               (5.10) [SOURCE: Page_137]

where ZT P represents the root growth, and θT P is the average soil water content in the transpiration reservoir (of depth ZT P ). [SOURCE: Page_137]

The ES term is then taken as the most limiting between the content of the evaporation reservoir and soil ability to respond to the evaporation demand. The latter is represented by the crop coefficient KES (called efficiency of the evaporation process) describing the way soil evaporation occurs with respect to the current soil water content. The general expression of ES is the following: [SOURCE: Page_137]

ESi = min(RES,i , KES,i ES0i ).                              (5.11) [SOURCE: Page_137]

Consequently, if the product ZES θES is low, the soil evaporation can not match the evaporation demand and it only releases the low water content that is contained in the evaporation reservoir. Conversely, when the soil capacity matches the evaporation demand, its evaporation response is still weighted by the coefficient KES which depends on soil water content. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Next comes the necessity to evaluate the actual transpiration T P from the comparison between the content of the transpiration reservoir and soil ability to respond to the transpiration demand. The latter is represented by the crop coefficient KT P (called efficiency of the transpiration process) describing the way plant transpiration occurs with respect to the current soil water content. The general expression of T P is the following: [SOURCE: Page_137]

T Pi = min(RT P,i , KT P,i T P 0i ).                         (5.12) [SOURCE: Page_137]

As the plant root is progressively deepening, the transpiration reservoir is growing. As a consequence, transpiration only releases low water contents (ZT P θT P ) at the beginning of the cropping season. Conversely, when the transpiration reservoir can match the transpiration demand, its transpiration response is still weighted by the coefficient KT P which depends on soil water content. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Water stress and its effects on LAI We define the relative deficit of transpiration as follows: Pi−1 k=i−nd T Pk Sw,i = Pi−1           ,                                  (5.13) k=i−nd T P 0k [SOURCE: Page_137]

where Sw,i corresponds to the coefficient of effect of water stress over a sliding window of the last nd days. Typically, for an instantaneous stress affecting the daily increment of LAI, it may be suit- able to set nd = 1. When Sw = 1, it means that the crop transpiration matches the transpiration demand, i.e. crop is not suffering from any water stress. Moreover the effect of transpiration deficit over plant development depends on the harmfulness λ > 0 associated to the crop, enabling us to depict the way crop development is limited under the different levels of water stress. [SOURCE: Page_137]

It is possible to consider a cumulative effect of stress, either implicitely by adjusting the value nd in the previous Sw formula, or explicitely by adding a multiplicative term Sc that acts on the [SOURCE: Page_137]

## 138                                             CHAPTER 5. THE OPTIRRIG AND CCI MODELS

current LAI to represent the eventual early senescence induced by long periods of stress. [SOURCE: Page_138]

As a result, the actual development of LAI taking into account the incremental stress (generating the effect Sw ) and cumulative stress (generating the effect Sc , which is for the moment a facultative feature) is formulated dynamically as follows: [SOURCE: Page_138]

LAIi = LAIi−1 Sc,i + (LAIp,i − LAIp,i−1 )Sw,i λ .                       (5.14) [SOURCE: Page_138]

We verify that if the crop does not suffer from any stress Sc or Sw , then the LAI develops at its potential rate, following the shape and magnitude of LAIp . [SOURCE: Page_138]

Drainage As reviewed by [39] there is a large consensus in the literature on the definition of field capacity as the threshold water content value above which significant drainage occurs, in the form of gravity flow, resulting from non-equilibrium conditions and aiming at returning to the equilibrium conditions. Denote Di the drainage occuring at the date i, then Di = 0 if the soil water content is below the field capacity, and the chosen formulation for daily drainage when soil moisture exceeds the field capacity is Di = min(ZD (θi − θf c ), KD ),                             (5.15) where θi − θf c is the excess water content to be drained, evaluated the current day, and ZD is the vertical extension of the considered soil reservoir (thus their product indicates the water amount to be drained, say RD ) while KD is the current drainage rate, representing the near-saturation hydraulic conductivity, calculated from both soil structure and current filling. [SOURCE: Page_138]

It should be noted that this expression of drainage only holds for vertically-averaged water contents above θf c , taking the average over the full extension (ZD ) of a given reservoir, to be taken as the deepest reservoir between the evaporation one (ZES ) and the transpiration one (ZT P ). [SOURCE: Page_138]

Soil water content variations All crop related variables involved in the variation of soil moisture have been introduced, there only remains the water inputs from both rainfall events (denoted P ) and irrigation (denoted I). The model Optirrig does not directly compute the soil water content balance between all these contributions, but rather proceeds in successive steps, priorising evaporation from the upper reservoir, then considering transpiration (if the soil water content is sufficient and the transpiration demand has yet to be matched) occuring in the dedicated reservoir, then adding the positive terms of rainfall and irrigation. However, we may write a conceptual balance corresponding to the result of these steps as if they happened simultaneously, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_138]

Ri = Ri−1 − T Pi − ESi + Ii + Pi − Di ,                            (5.16) [SOURCE: Page_138]

where R is the deepest reservoir between RES and RT P . [SOURCE: Page_138]

Biomass, water stress effect and yield The lower part of the conceptual scheme of the model (Figure 5.3) shows that biomass, here biomass accumulation, is calculated from LAI. Biomass in- crement between successive time steps writes γ Bi = Bi−1 + P ARi IRi (LAIi )RU ESw,i ,                            (5.17) [SOURCE: Page_138]

where Bi is the biomass at day i, P ARi is the photosynthetically active radiation, taken as half the global daily radiation Rgi , IRi is the fraction of intercepted radiation, calculated from the current LAI value, RU E is radiation use efficiency, considered a fixed parameter for a given species, and Sw,i is the transpiration deficit affecting biomass production, with its specific harmfulness coefficient γ. In the latest, currently-developped versions of the model, γ is tested as an increasing function of time (thermal time) to account for the known, increased sensitivity of most crops to water stresses occurring during their (final) reproductive stages, while stresses occurring during their (initial) veg- etative stages are assumed of lesser impact. [SOURCE: Page_138]

Finally, radiation interception is calculated by means of the Beer-Lambert law as follows: [SOURCE: Page_138]

IRi = 1 − exp(−kBL LAIi ),                                   (5.18) [SOURCE: Page_138]

5.3. TOWARDS A CORRESPONDENCE WITH THE CCI MODEL                                                   141 [SOURCE: Page_141]

also, indirectly, on time. For the CCI model, we directly use the real time denoted t, and the system is supposed to evolve over a time interval [0, T ] of cropping season. By contrast, Optirrig is written as a thermal time system, meaning that it is a variable accounting for the accumulation of temperature that plays the role of the time component. In the particular case where the temperature would be controlled and kept constant throughout the whole season, then the thermal time is simply a dilatation of the real time. In the general case, the thermal time consists in a monotonous change of time variable that slightly accelerates when the warm season arrives. The analogous “thermal time” interval representing the cropping season is [0, Tm ], and in the following we will speak about time-dependent variables and thermal time-dependent variables without distinction, since they play the same role in similar ways. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Water components Regarding soil water budget, several terms deal with external contributions to the soil processes : irrigation, rainfall and drainage. In turns out that the Optirrig and CCI models contain very similar concepts of these contributions. Indeed, both present the action of irrigation as a single external chronicle that adds water directly into the soil (in a discrete or continuous time, using discrete or continuous watering amounts). Rainfall is generally seen as a daily contribution in Optirrig, and acts on the soil water content the same way as the irrigation contribution. In the CCI model framework, we allow a more generic formalism of rainfall since we wrote the contribution of rainfall with a time-dependent measure, enabling both continuous and instantaneous precipitation. However, in both cases the contribution of rainfall plays the same role, similar to the irrigation contribution, in the soil water contents. Then, in both Optirrig and CCI in the open-field conditions, drainage is assumed to be possible when the soil moisture exceeds the physical equilibrium point of the field capacity. Moreover, in both cases drainage is bounded even in extreme flooding situations. It is thus affordable to consider that the Optirrig and CCI models have quite direct analogous external water components. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Soil components One of the main differences between Optirrig and the CCI model is that Optirrig provides rather explicit formulations of the order-1 physical processes while the CCI model contains rather abstract formulations. These are possibly endowed with physical sense, although most often combining processes treated separately in the companion model. More precisely, Optirrig calculates the responses in evaporation and transpiration as logical consequences of a demand, and their calculation is explicitely formulated as the combined effects of the climatic demand, the current soil filling and the radiation interception (due to plant covering the soil surface). Furthermore, the level of soil filling might induce explicit stress that reduce the development of LAI, which bounces back in the expression of the evapotranspiration capacity. In the CCI formulation, this combined effect is implicitely contained in a single formula partitioning evaporation and transpiration, which are moreover weighted by efficiency crop coefficients. These coefficients have to solely carry the effect of water stress, that is not concretely calculated in the CCI formalism (due to the absence of a loop of processes in its conceptual scheme). As a result, the terms of evaporation and transpiration respectively may be seen as similar contributions for Optirrig and the CCI model into the soil water content, but we have to be conscious that they come from different descriptions. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Plant components The common purpose for Optirrig and the CCI model is making crops grow with the best possible irrigation, for assigned objectives, in presence of external constraints. Conse- quently, both formulate in their respective way a time-dependent variable that represents the growth function of the crop. Optirrig jointly describes root growth and canopy development (LAI) in func- tion of thermal time. More precisely, the depth of the transpiration reservoir is dictated by root deepening, which allows a progressively larger access to soil water, for transpiration, throughout the season. Conversely, the CCI model assumes that the available volume of soil water reservoir is fixed during the whole cropping season, implying in particular that early transpiration effects are quite reduced compared to those in Optirrig. Regarding the evolution of leaves, the Optirrig formulation involves the concrete Leaf Area Index, quantifying how the plant covers the surface with its canopy, and it then enables to assess the intercepted radiation IR and the surface partition Cp. In the CCI model, one may consider that the time-varying function φ represents the LAI growth (with its pseudo-logistic shape), but this function is rather accounting for the partition of surface receiving radiation. As a consequence, the function φ in CCI plays a double role of both IR and Cp in Optirrig, knowing that these two variables are calculated from LAI using a similar formula. [SOURCE: Page_141]

## 142                                               CHAPTER 5. THE OPTIRRIG AND CCI MODELS

5.3.2     Term-to-term correspondence Irrigation, rain and drainage The daily amount of water introduced into the soil through irrigation in the Optirrig model is denoted by Ii = Imax IN,i . In the CCI model, irrigation is a fraction of the maximum rate, identified as k^2 = Fmax /ET 0, which represents the capacity of the irrigation equipment to deliver several times the daily climatic demand, thus to exert a strong control on the soil-plant system. The irrigation rate thus writes FET     0 u(t), recalling that in this expression max [SOURCE: Page_142]

the climatic demand ET 0 is a constant, taken as the average value of the climatic demand throughout the season. Similarly, in Optirrig, the ratio of irrigation to the climatic demand writes ET    0i IN,i , this Imax [SOURCE: Page_142]

time with time variable ET 0i values. Consequently, Fmax and Imax being considered known, given some data as ET 0 and ET 0i , it is possible to compute the irrigation chronicle u in CCI from the one IN in Optirrig, or conversely. This relationship will be useful in terms of transfering the irrigation scenarios from a model to the other. We can translate the rainfall contributions in an analogous way, since the respective daily or instantaneous amounts of rainwater are written as Pi (for Optirrig) and αr(t) (for the CCI model). Finally, the drainage terms match (at 0) in Optirrig and CCI when the soil moisture is below the field capacity, then they are written by Di = min(ZD (θi − θf c ), KD ) (for Optirrig) and DR = min(ksat (S − 1)/(Ssat − 1), ksat ) (for CCI). Given that θf c corresponds to S = 1, we observe that the parameters (ZD ,KD ) of one side can be deduced from the parameters (ksat ,Ssat ) of the other side, in both directions. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Evapotranspiration rates The transpiration rate is calculated in Optirrig with the formula T P = min(RT P , KcCpET 0KT P ) and as T R = ET 0φKS for the CCI model. This allows both identifying the terms playing similar roles in the two models, and identifying structural differences between models. As for the latter, the min term in Optirrig specifies that transpiration should only be taken from soil water contained in the soil region accessible to the transpiration demand. This region is taken as the rooting depth of the crop, starting from almost zero then increasing up to a maximum value, assumed to be reached at the same thermal time that is needed to reach the maximum development of the aerial part of the plant (LAI). This limitation, via the min term, does not exist in the CCI model which calculates T R over “a certain soil depth” that one is tempted to adjust to the full soil profile of Optirrig - but other options are likely possible when needing to specify the depth of the soil profile in the CCI model. Apart from this discussion on the depth over which T P is calculated and on the subsequent limitations of the avaliable water amount, the comparison between formalisms shows that φ in the CCI model plays the same role as KcCp in Optirrig, once Kc normalized by its Kcmax value. Both Kc and Cp are exponential functions of LAI which is a sigmoid of thermal time. This also makes the KcCp product a sigmoid of thermal time, while φ is a sigmoid of time. There is therefore a strong parallel between formalisms. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Evaporation is calculated as ES = min(RES , Kc(1 − Cp)ET 0KES ) in Optirrig and EV = ET 0(1 − φ)KR for the CCI model. The min term in Optirrig specifies that evaporation should only be calculated from the soil region accessible to evaporation. This is a region of fixed and limited depth, known from soil structure : sandy soils allow deeper evaporation uptake than silty or clayey ones. This is a region which does not cover the full rooting depth : in most cases, plant roots largely overcome the evaporation depth during crop growth phase. This limitation, via the min term, does not exist in the CCI model which calculates EV over “a certain soil depth”, with the same elements of discussion as for the T R term. Then if φ in the CCI model plays exactly the same role as KcCp in Optirrig, as previously voiced, Kc(1 − Cp) is not stricly equivalent to (1 − φ) and one likely has to admit the link between Kc, Cp and φ is a bit loose and relies on the shape of the functions with time. Another option would have been to involve the efficiency terms KT P , KES , KS , KR in the comparison between Kc, Cp and φ, but this option may likely be discarded because of the obvious, strong analogy between the efficiency terms of the two models : in both models the efficiency of the evaporation and transpiration processes is linked to threshold values of soil water content (in θ for Optirrig) or saturation level (in S for CCI). [SOURCE: Page_142]

Plant growth, radiation interception and biomass production The previous argument on the similar shapes of the canopy-related terms (KcCp and φ) appears rather crucial for model com- parison. It calls for further investigation of the roles played by those terms, the biophysical processes they actually describe and the overlaps between the reduced fomulations of the CCI model and the developed ones of Optirrig. An interesting example is the representation, in the models’ conceptual [SOURCE: Page_142]

5.4. DISCUSSION AND PERSPECTIVES                                                                    143 [SOURCE: Page_143]

schemes, of the causal link between plant growth, canopy development, radiation interception and biomass production. In Optirrig, plant growth with thermal time is signaled by increasing LAI val- ues, accompanied by increasing radiation interception IR(LAI) values taking place in the equation used to describe biomass production. There are two parameters (α1 , β) controlling the S-shape of the LAI curve and one parameter kBL controlling that of the IR curve, an exponential that satu- rates for high-enough LAI values, which finally makes IR another S-shaped function of thermal time. [SOURCE: Page_143]

The next step is to compare the expressions used for biomass production, which are for Optirrig γ P ARi IRi (LAIi )RU ESw,i , and k^3 φ(t)KS (S(t)) for the CCI model. [SOURCE: Page_143]

• Radiation interception IR in Optirrig has a S-shape, as φ in the CCI model, which tends to indicate a link between those terms. However, from previous arguments, φ has already been linked to Kc and Cp. Put together, the two parameters (δ, γ) controlling the shape of φ would have to be selected in coherence with the three parameters (α1 , β, kBL ) present in Kc, Cp and IR. A good starting point is that the match is good between the shape of the different functions. • The k^3 term (parameter) being the scaling term for the CCI model, it somehow describes the potential daily biomass production for Optirrig, combining P ARi and RU E, that is the amount of photosynthetically active radiation (forcing variable) and radiation use efficiency (parameter) denoting the plant’s ability to convert solar radiation into biomass. γ • From the two points above, there remains the equivalence between Sw,i        in Optirrig and KS in the CCI model, which questions the equivalence previously assumed between KT P and KS , when comparing transpiration efficiencies. In Optirrig, KT P indicates how transpiration efficiency is reduced for too low water contents, while Sw,i converts the effect of this reduction in a stress term (comparing the effective transpiration to the transpiration demand over a γ certain number of days), and finally Sw,i denotes the effect of the transpiration reduction (and deficit) on biomass production. [SOURCE: Page_143]

5.4     Discussion and perspectives Follow up implementation As hinted in Section 5.1, the framework of the Optirrig model allows us to consider irrigation strategies as an entry of a simulation run. We noticed that the strategy n°3 was suitable for an attempt of implementing the so-called 2-TBIS, and that the “decision time” could be identified as the time of switching from the first period to the second period applying strategy n°3 (using different thresholds). While the identification of triggering thresholds (in Optirrig, in terms of percentage of available water capacity) appears to be directly related to the values of soil moisture thresholds (in the CCI model, in terms of S variable), the definition of the threshold target pctRU tar is far from trivial. Indeed, we may choose any threshold that is above the triggering threshold, while staying reasonably close to be easily reachable in one irrigation. This flexibility suggests that different practices may emerge, depending on the material constraints or the desires of the operator. Indeed, selecting a target threshold relatively far above the triggering threshold implies that the operator would irrigate less often with larger amounts, and conversely selecting a target slightly above the triggering threshold implies irrigating quite consistently with small amounts. The first case is easier to carry out in practice, but moves away from the theoretical singular arc. The second case requires a higher frequency of management, and relies on the accuracy of soil moisture sensors to support an effective irrigation design. The closer we define the triggering and the target thresholds, the closer we approximate a singular arc, thus the closer we are of obtaining an additional correspondence between theoretical values of singular controls (using ) and “singular” irrigation amounts in practice. Such implementation perspectives consolidate the capacity to transfer properties and informations through the double modeling loops in Figure 1.1. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Calibration issues As mentioned in the previous section, and as expected since we are comparing two models whose formulations differ in terms of dimensionality, some phenomena described in Optirrig are challenging to identify from the approximated formalism of the CCI model. As a result, when the correspondence is not a direct transfer of terms, it may seem reasonable to adjust the value(s) of the CCI parameter(s) in order to choose one that allows to match the main variables (soil [SOURCE: Page_143]
