## 144                                             CHAPTER 5. THE OPTIRRIG AND CCI MODELS

moisture, biomass production) produced by Optirrig and the CCI model. However, doing so means accepting that the physical sense endowed in the CCI formulation becomes more difficult to interpret accurately. Furthermore, both term-to-term correspondence and parameter fitting will likely not be complete, in the sense that the validity of such connections will depend on the operational context, and there may exist situations that the CCI model would not be able to represent (for example the case of early senescence induces a temporary decrease of LAI, which makes it difficult to reach with the CCI formulation). That being said, Optirrig is an irrigation model, which also has its own domain of validity, and its purpose is to generate and optimize irrigation scenarios. That means considering situations where the available irrigation water is sufficient to foresee potential significant benefits from an optimization. This suggests that its domain of validity should contain (or focus) a certain range of classical situations, which are likely to be in the range of application of the CCI model too. [SOURCE: Page_144]

Further research directions Another perspective would be to use this previous discussion about the term-to-term correspondence, in order to point out which components of the CCI model show a potential lack of description. It would thus be natural to wonder how the CCI formulations may be improved or supplemented. For example, the fact that every process in the CCI formalism assumes that they are carried out over a fixed soil volume is one of the main limitations mentioned above. Some issues of correspondence might then result from such an approximation, given that considering a fixed volume alienates the description of evaporation and transpiration especially at the beginning of the cropping season. In order to bypass this limitation, we might suggest different modeling options, such as writing the reservoir volume as the soil porosity ρ multiplied by the root depth z(t). This way, we would explicitely provide the time dependence of the reservoir size, and the root depth function z might be considered as a parameterized time-varying function (the same way as φ). Consequently, it would be easier to adjust the correspondence between Optirrig and the CCI model, but it would certainly come along with additional difficulties for the theoretical analysis of the optimal control problem based on the CCI model. However, it is possible that with reasonable hypotheses over such a function z, we might still be able to work out some properties of optimal solutions. This is at least an interesting perspective for future works. [SOURCE: Page_144]

Chapter 6 [SOURCE: Page_147]

Formulation and study of optimal av- eraged cost control problems in view of operational irrigation management [SOURCE: Page_147]

This chapter corresponds to works in progress, in collaboration with Nathalie Khalil, Maria do Rosario de Pinho (researchers at Faculdade de Engenharia da Universidade do Porto, Portugal) and Sofia Lopes (assistant professor in the Mathematics Department at the School of Science of Minho University, Portugal), during a three month stay at University of Porto, from January to April 2025. The aim of this chapter is to study an approach based on the so-called “averaged cost control“ which is a way to formulate optimal control problems under some uncertainties, described as a set of all possible scenarios with their known probability of occurrence. This approach consists in looking for a control which is common to all scenarios, and which is optimal in the sense that it optimizes the averaged cost of all these scenarios. The goal of introducing this tool is to take into consideration uncertainties, typically climatic ones, closer to real-world conditions. In this chapter, we present the frameworks in which such situations may be formalized and are intended to be more deeply investigated in the near future. [SOURCE: Page_147]

6.1     Robustness of optimal strategies with respect to the crop growth dynamics variability [SOURCE: Page_147]

This section is devoted the application of the averaged cost control framework to the irrigation problem, in order to look for irrigation strategies that optimize the expected value of biomass produc- tion, with respect to uncertainties over the function φ (which has been proven to be the main driver of the shape of optimal solutions). We consider that the function φ belongs to a finite set of possible profiles φi , associated to a probability µi . Then we formalize our irrigation problem by duplicating the known dynamics as many times as the number of φi . We obtain a high dimensional optimal con- trol problem for which we write optimality necessary conditions as available in the literature for this kind of problems. Deriving these conditions will provide properties for characterization of optimal solutions. [SOURCE: Page_147]

6.1.1    Problem formulation [SOURCE: Page_147]

We consider the CCI model, in its version in greenhouse context, and we suppose that there exists N possible profiles of functions φ, that we denote (φi )1≤i≤N , associated to their probability of occurrence (µi )1≤i≤N . Writing the augmented dynamical system consists in copying N times the originate dynamical system, and indexing them according to their corresponding function φi . Then, [SOURCE: Page_147]

147 [SOURCE: Page_147]

we write for t ∈ [0, T ] the following system:  Ṡi (t) = k^1 (−φi (t)KS (Si (t)) − (1 − φi (t))KR (Si (t)) + k^2 u(t)) ,  Ḃi (t) = φi (t)KS (Si (t)),                                                                   (for 1 ≤ i ≤ N )   (6.1) V̇i (t) = u(t).   [SOURCE: Page_148]

We keep all the assumptions of the greenhouse context valid, and we precise that all the functions φi play the same role as expected. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Assumption 6.1.1. Assume that φi : [0, T ] → [0, 1] is increasing and of class C 1 for every 1 ≤ i ≤ N. [SOURCE: Page_148]

We also indicate that the initial condition is taken as uniform with respect to the scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Assumption 6.1.2. The system has the following initial conditions: [SOURCE: Page_148]

Si (0) = S$_0$ ∈ (S ∗ , 1] ,   Bi (0) = 0 ,      Vi (0) = 0 ,            for 1 ≤ i ≤ N . [SOURCE: Page_148]

Remark 6.1.1. Following the definition of the dynamics and the initial conditions, notice that all the Vi are in fact equal. Indeed, this problem considers a unique control for piloting all the dynamics, then their variable Vi representing the water consumption (which is thus common) has no dependence on the scenario i, but we keep the notation Vi instead of V for convenience. [SOURCE: Page_148]

In order to define the terminal target Q of irrigation water quota in a suitable range of initial water quotas, we would like to set a framework of deficit irrigation as before, but having several trajectories of soil moisture controlled simultaneously brings supplementary difficulty. ## 152     CHAPTER 6. AVERAGED COST CONTROL FOR CROP IRRIGATION MANAGEMENT

6.1.3.2     Singular arcs and analysis of the switching function Notice that our problem formulation involves a dynamical system in finite dimension, where the dynamics satisfies as before the hypotheses of being piecewise affine and continuous with respect to the state. This allows us to once again invoke the exented bang-bang principle for optimal control problems with such dynamics. We obtain the same following result about the existence of singular arcs, adapted to the multiple trajectories situation. Proposition 6.1.12. For any optimal solution to the problem (PAV G ), a singular arc can only occur when there exists i such that one trajectory Si that satisfies Si = Sope or Si = S ∗ . The only major difference in this section compared to Chapter 3 relies in the condition of max- imization of the Hamiltonian. Indeed, in the current section, we are dealing with an averaged Hamiltonian, thus the switching function is also averaged. As a result, the previous argument con- sisting in showing that the set where the switching function is non-negative is connected, relying on a proof by contradiction, does not hold in our present case. In fact, replicating the argument by contradiction in Proposition 3.4.8 would imply the existence of a local minimum of the new averaged switching function ΨAV G , which is no longer directly related to an adjoint function qiS , thus we can no longer claim that there also exists a local minimum for one of the adjoint functions qiS . Though the argument is no longer valid in this case, we still believe that a similar (yet more complicated) ar- gument could provide a similar result. For instance, the demonstration process in Proposition 3.4.8 seems to only exploit a local minimality, which is far from all the underlying properties that may arise in our problem, especially some local convexity (or concavity) properties. [SOURCE: Page_152]

Such a successful argument would allow us to deduce the following result yet to be proven. Conjecture 6.1.1. For any optimal solution to the problem (PAV G ), the set IAV G defined by [SOURCE: Page_152]

IAV G = {t ∈ [t∗min , T ], ΨAV G (t) ≥ 0} [SOURCE: Page_152]

is non-empty and connected. [SOURCE: Page_152]

6.1.4      Conclusion The above Conjecture 6.1.1, if proven, would enable a conclusion about the structure of opti- mal solutions to the problem (PAV G ), which would in fact be the same as the “single-dynamics” situation. Such a result would have significant consequences for the operational point of view, since it would mean that the Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies based on two thresholds (Sope and S ∗ ) that we studied in detail in Chapter 4 are robust with respect to the variability of their most sensible parameter (the function φ). [SOURCE: Page_152]

Furthermore, some numerical simulations were conducted to test this conjecture, and we did not find yet any counter-example, i.e. any example of parameterization of a set of functions φi associated to some occurrence probabilities µi for which our simulations would provide a different structure of optimal solution. As a consequence, this strengthens our belief that the 2-TBIS are indeed optimal for the averaged cost control problem formulation of this section. However, since there is now multiple soil moisture trajectories evolving simultaneously, and since we mentioned that a singular arc can occur if one of these trajectories is located at Sope or S ∗ , then it is not easy to anticipate about the exact structure of the optimal solution, especially the number of singular arcs and the number of switches from singular arcs to bang (u = 1) arcs or conversely (as shown in Figure 6.1). [SOURCE: Page_152]

6.2       Integrating rainfall uncertainties and soil moisture jumps This section focuses on the irrigation problem in open-field conditions, representing rainfall as instantaneous events, i.e. Dirac leading to dynamics with jumps. We consider that an annual sequence of rainfall belongs to a finite set of possible sequences, associated to some probabilities. As a consequence, we once again formulate the irrigation problem using the averaged cost control framework, aiming for optimizing the expected value of biomass production, with respect to the rainfall uncertainties. However, such systems with discontinuous state do not belong to the standard application framework. As a result, we seek to employ a well-known technique of time variable change, based on a graph completion, but we need to formalize it in such a way that the new time variable is the same for every considered scenario. This way, we will be able to apply the dedicated optimality necessary conditions in order to search and characterize optimal solutions. [SOURCE: Page_153]

6.2.1         Framework of rainfed irrigation We consider the CCI model, in its version in open-field context, and we represent the rainfall events by jumps in the soil moisture trajectory. More precisely, let us consider one annual rainfall sequence (denoted R(i) , associated to its probability of occurrence µi ) defined by its timings (tk ∈ [0, T ]) and depths (hk > 0) of rainfall events, as follows: n                                         o (i)                 (i) R(i) = (t^1 , ..., t(i) ni  ), (h 1   , ..., h (i) ni  )  ,                                            (6.7) [SOURCE: Page_153]

where ni is the number of rainfall events for the sequence R(i) . Then, assuming that there exists N possible profiles of annual rainfall sequences, we write the following system representing our crop [SOURCE: Page_153]

6.2.2    Problem reformulation with an abstract time Following the time reparameterization technique explained in [139, 129] and generalized in [124, 160], we aim for applying such technique for transforming our non-standard dynamical sys- tem into a standard one. However, if we considered each equation on S (i) presenting state jumps individually, we would obtain, through the so-called “graph completion” technique, new abstract time variable that depends on the scenario i. As a consequence, this would not be suitable for our problem since we need to control simultaneously (thus using the same abstract time variable) all the different scenarios i. [SOURCE: Page_155]

Therefore we would like to build a unique time reparameterization for all the scenarios, thus based on a unique graph completion technique. To this aim, and for the sake of simplicity, we formulate the following additional hypothesis. (i)         (i) Assumption 6.2.2. Denoting E (i) = {t^1 , ..., tni } the set of dates of rainfall events for each scenario i, we suppose that the timings of rainfall events in different scenarios do not coincide, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_155]

∀i ̸= j, E (i) ∩ E (j) = ∅. [SOURCE: Page_155]

Remark 6.2.4. Notice that if two dates of rainfall events in two different scenarios coincide, then it does not prevent the following development, but it would induce slightly more complicated indices notations. This way, let us define the set of all dates of rainfall events, without redundancy. Definition 6.2.3. Denote Erain the set of all dates of rainfall events of each scenario i, defined by N [ Erain =             E (i) . i=1 [SOURCE: Page_155]

(j) Any time belonging to this set will be denoted by tk where j ∈ J1, N K and k ∈ J1, nj K. The idea now is to define a uniform time reparameterization, following the graph completion technique, which means that we consider “jumps” for all the state variables S (i) at all the times belonging to Erain . As a consequence, some “jumps” will be artificial, in the sense that they do not correspond to their scenario, but we simply fix it of size 0. Let us redefine then the amplitude of the rainfall events in a uniform way. [SOURCE: Page_155]

Now we can employ the uniform graph completion technique for the common time reparame- terization. It consists in rescaling the time variable t ∈ [0, T ] into a time variable τ ∈ [0, 1] by “inserting” artificial time interval at each occurrence of a rainfall event. Outside these artificial time intervals, the system should evolve accordingly to the usual time and its normal dynamics, and inside these artificial time intervals, we “freeze” the normal dynamics, enabling the “jump” to happen in a continuous way, with respect to the new abstract time. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Let us first define some auxiliary functions, starting with the function F representing the distri- bution of the rainfall events, as follows: [SOURCE: Page_156]

6.2.3     Perspectives We may now use the results from the averaged cost control problem and apply them on our new problem formulation (Prain AV G )N EW , which is written as a “standard” system of differential equations. We then project to derive the optimality necessary conditions associated to this problem, and, even if the construction was not straightforward, it only brings additional terms or factors that are time-dependent, then we expect that the derivation of the necessary conditions that we already investigated in a more simple case in Chapter 3 might be adapted here. In particular, the state dependence remains the same, and the time-dependent terms will vanish as soon as we compute the derivative with respect to the state, in the construction of the adjoint system. Once the analysis is done and we obtain properties about optimal controls v(·) and trajectories (S, e B,e Ve ), we will be able to write them back in the originate time variable using the following relations: S(t) = S(η(t)), e            B(t) = B(η(t)), e                     V (t) = Ve (η(t)),         u(t) = v(η(t)),            for a.e. t ∈ [0, T ]. Our perspectives will also include using direct methods to obtain numerical simulations for this problem reformulation and get insights on the structure of optimal solutions. Provided that such analysis process detailed above can result in properties able to characterize a structure of optimal solutions, then we may deduce a formulation of irrigation strategies that will be efficient in the context of open-field conditions, in the sense that they account for the possible various outcomes from uncertain weather, not only in the short-term, but at the cropping season time-scale. Such strategies would be a strong support for crop irrigation management, especially in the regions where sustainable production crucially relies on contribution from irrigation to supplement scarce rainfalls. [SOURCE: Page_157]

Conclusions and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_159]

In this thesis, we investigated the optimization of crop irrigation from the point of view of optimal control theory. The study was conducted at the plot scale, in greenhouse and in open-field conditions, and in a context of limited water resources. The main motivation was to integrate a double modeling approach, thus to develop optimal control tools derived from a low dimensional ODE model in order to help designing efficient irrigation strategies for an operational model. As a result, we based our theoretical work on the designed-on-purpose Controlled Crop Irrigation (CCI) model, improving its formulation and investigating its structural features, and we considered the Optirrig model as the destination model. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Summary of contributions The first contribution of this thesis corresponds to the extensions of the Bang-Bang Principle detailed in Chapter 2. These studies were motivated by the simple (yet non-linear) dynamics of the CCI model: in its complete form, the dynamics are piecewise affine and continuous with respect to the state, and contain a non-deterministic term. We first revisited the classical linear bang-bang principle for minimum-time problems in a piecewise affine continuous form, under state constraints. We showed in Section 2.1 that in this framework there exists optimal trajectories that are generated by “bang” controls at the loci where the dynamics is differentiable, and possible “singular” controls when the state is located exactly at the non-differentiable loci of the dynamics. Then we revisited the classical bang-bang principle in a linear form with additional brownian noise. We showed in Section 2.2 that the general result stating the equality of the reachable sets (with any control or with only “bang” controls) does not hold in such settings with stochastic controls, but we showed (under some conditions) that the optimal cost of a Mayer problem with a compact control set coincides with the optimal cost of the same problem with controls taking values in the closed convex hull of the compact set. These two theoretical results provide structural properties about the nature of optimal solutions and are expected to be useful for concrete applications. [SOURCE: Page_159]

The resolution of the optimal control problem based on the CCI model formulation in the con- text of greenhouse crops detailed in Chapter 3 constitutes the second contribution of this thesis. We introduced a family of optimal control problems adapted to different operational objectives, and we showed, using dedicated tools (the home-made budget compensation technique allowing to consider an equivalent problem removing a state constraint as detailed in Section 3.3, and an ap- plication of the extended bang-bang principle for piecewise affine continuous dynamics taken from Section 2.1), and applying the non-differentiable Pontryagin Maximum Principle, that optimal ir- rigation strategies for this family of problems share the same structure. More precisely, optimal strategies belong to a class of simple parameterized time-varying feedback controls, which means that the initial infinite dimensional optimization problem is thus reduced to a small dimensional (one or two) optimization problem suitable for numerical purposes. Moreover, the obtained optimal trajectories may actually be interpreted in terms of real-world irrigation practices that can be called Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies (TBIS). [SOURCE: Page_159]

The third main contribution of this thesis, presented in Chapter 4, corresponds to the in-depth analysis of such Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies. The computationally affordable features of the class of optimal strategies introduced in Chapter 3 allowed us to look for the optimal values of these parameterized controls in a large class of model settings. Moreover, in Chapter 4, we studied the sensitivity of the shape of optimal strategies with respect to the variability of the model settings [SOURCE: Page_159]

## 160                                          CHAPTER 6. CONCLUSIONS AND PERSPECTIVES

(contextual parameters) through a global exploration and variance-based computational methods, and we showed that the optimal shape (in particular the optimal decision time) is mostly driven by plant characteristics (their growth dynamics and the effect of water stress on their biomass production). We also investigated the sub-optimality of irrigation chronicles obtained by making small mistakes on the determination of decision time and soil moisture thresholds, and we highlighted that mistakes on the top soil moisture threshold may significantly undermine the biomass production, while mistakes on the decision time have moderate effects on the biomass. However, we showed that anticipated or delayed decisions, combined with some specific contexts, may either hinder the healthy crop development or provide favorable conditions for an efficient use of water. [SOURCE: Page_160]

On-going works As initially stated as the motivation of this thesis, we aim for applying the theoretical irrigation strategies developed in the contributions in the Optirrig model. To this end, we introduced in Chap- ter 5 the formalism of Optirrig, and we presented the compatibility between Optirrig and the CCI model. We highlighted their conceptual similarities, enabling to settle a term-to-term correspon- dence for a majority of the models main components, and we described their formulation differences that result in compatibility difficulties. With some mild assumptions allowing us to precise the correspondence of some slightly challenging terms, we showed that it is possible to implement the theoretical Threshold-Based Irrigation Strategies in the Optirrig framework, at least for testing purposes in a first step. The next stage of the study is to conduct a performance analysis of TBIS in a wide range of operational conditions, and especially by providing quantitative comparisons with other existing practices regarding various criteria (agronomic viability, economical outcomes, robustness to variable conditions). A further perspective involves contributing to the CCI model formulation with modeling improvements about the main obstacles identified for the correspondence (as the soil reservoir), without bringing too much complexity in the CCI model. [SOURCE: Page_160]

We presented in Chapter 3 a complete resolution of the optimal control problem corresponding to the context of greenhouse crops and with fully known conditions. As a result, the natural extension of this work is to either consider that the conditions (i.e. parameter values) are no longer exactly known, or take into account the weather conditions from open-field agriculture. In Chapter 6, we introduced a theoretical tool called optimal “averaged cost control” allowing us to address such optimal control problems involving uncertainties. We presented in Section 6.1 how the investigation of Chapter 3 can be adapted when considering uncertainties on the most sensible model parameter (the crop growth function φ). Some numerical experiments suggest that a similar result might be derived, and that a more general (yet very similar) structure of optimal control is expected to be robust to parameter uncertainties. In Section 6.2, we considered that rainfall events generate jumps in the soil moisture state variable, then we explained how a set of possible rainfall scenarios can be included in the crop irrigation problem. To this end, we employed a suited graph completion technique to transform our problem into a formulation adapted to the averaged cost control framework. We aim to further study both these averaged cost control problems, in view of the development of analytical results about optimal irrigation strategies in these contexts. [SOURCE: Page_160]

Further perspectives In addition to the above perspectives that are direct follow up studies of the preliminary work introduced in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6, we can mention here several research questions related to biological, operational or methodological aspects that were not investigated in this thesis. First the lower state constraint on the soil moisture was considered constant, but we may deal with a time-dependent state constraint that is likely higher at specific stages of plant development (as flowering). This situation was observed numerically but the search for analytical resolution may provide supplementary difficulties. Then we always considered that the irrigation material could face the climatic demand and refill the soil with maximal irrigation rate, but it may be worth of interest to examine the opposite situation (where the maximal irrigation rate cannot match the daily evapotranspiration demand, i.e. when k2 < 1). We observed numerically that this local non-controllability generates non-intuitive irrigation strategies whose structures can no longer be formulated with the time-varying feedback presented in our study. Furthermore, the optimization [SOURCE: Page_160]

161 [SOURCE: Page_161]

problem may be formulated not only by maximizing the biomass production or the financial ben- efits, but also minimizing a cumulative stress that may be related to the production quality. Such consideration would end up in optimizing a compromise between the quantity and the quality of production. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Taking one step back and integrating the crop irrigation problem in a larger view, we may recon- sider the problem formulation in several directions. First we never addressed the question of water acquisition, which may be part of a preliminary problem of resource allocation at the basin scale. Indeed, water availability is a prerequisite for water management, and operational issues about op- timal water allocation beyond the plot scale is non-trivial. Then, we supposed in this thesis that the nutrients were not a limiting factor of the crop development. However, we may consider the formulation of the CCI model with an additional state variable for the soil nitrogen content (as in [42]), whose dynamics is highly non-linear (not even piecewise affine) in the situation where nutri- ents are considered as a limited resource. We can complement such considerations by integrating different sources of irrigation water, especially reuse water which contain a certain concentration of nitrogen, and which may further address the wastewater treatment. A problem combining stress and limitations in both water and nutrients has not been addressed with an analytical optimal con- trol framework to our knowledge. Furthermore, the studies in this thesis were conducted under a spatial homogeneity assumption, but operational situations might ask for a spatialization (thus the emergence of partial differential equations), or a formal homogenization, as for example soil texture heterogeneity or irregular topography. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Finally, all these considerations and problem extensions are certainly of interest for the applica- tion point of view, but they also present the great advantage of giving rise to interesting and technical mathematical problems or open theoretical questions. As hinted by Chapter 2, a further perspective of investigation about the bang-bang principle would be to extend the framework combining piece- wise affine and stochastic dynamics, or consider a more general notion of solutions with discontinuous dynamics. Moreover, the uniform procedure of change of time variable in Chapter 6 indicates that we may be able to generalize the averaged cost control results to dynamical systems containing state discontinuities, provided that we find general and suitable hypotheses on the nature of such discon- tinuities. At last, a great variety of control problems features arise from the further perspectives in the diverse research directions about crop irrigation and water management mentioned previously, such as non-linear and non-smooth dynamics, viability theory, stochastic dynamics, multi-criteria optimization, switched dynamical systems, networks, partial differential equations, homogenization theory. There is still plenty of room in crop irrigation and control theory. What this thesis has done is a drop, what opens ahead is an ocean. [SOURCE: Page_161]
